THE BASIC RESEARCH PROGRAMS 

The basic research financed by the Atomic Energy Commission separates broadly into work in the life sciences and in the physical sciences. 

Whether the research deals with medical uses of atomic energy, or the biological effects of nuclear radiation, or the discovery of new atomic particles and analyses of intra-nuclear forces, it is all directly related to the statutory mission of the Atomic Energy Commission. 

That mission is stated in the Atomic Energy Act of 1954 which declares, as the policy of the United States, that The development, use, and control of atomic energy shall be directed so as to make the maximum contribution to the general welfare, subject at all times to the paramount objective of making the maximum contribution to the common defense and security; and the development, use, and control of atomic energy shall be directed so as to promote world peace, improve the general welfare, increase the standard of living, and strength-. en free competition in private enterprise.

Basic research provides the key to further understanding which is the basis for all development, use, and control of atomic energy for all purposes. The Congress has steadily supported increasing use of the tool of research. 

Both life sciences and physical sciences have required larger allocations of funds for research, as broader areas of inquiry, and more individual projects are pursued, and as the costs of some projects become greater. 

For basic and programmatic research in the medical and biological fields-the life sciencesthe Commission budgeted $49 million for the fiscal year that ended June 30, 1960. Some $33 million of this total was for research and operating expenses of Government-owned or financed laboratories operated for the Commission by universities or industrial organizations, and some $16 million was for research in universities, colleges, and other nonprofit institutions carried out under 593 Commission research contracts.

For research in the physical sciences, the Commission budgeted expenditures of $137 million for the same fiscal year. About threequarters of this sum went for research and operating costs of Government-owned and financed laboratories, and one-quarter for research under 516 contracts with universities, colleges, and other nonprofit institutions. In addition, the Commission expended some $29.2 million for construction of facilities and such research tools as the powerful particle accelerators known popularly as "atom smashers."

The life and physical science contracts are listed in the appendices of this report. The sums of money expended for research under these contracts make it apparent that much of the equipment and time of staffs of research institutions is devoted to atomic energy projects. 

This is a means of training new scientists as well as of using the talents of those already trained. This report describes for the most part the projects of Government-owned and financed laboratories.

We aim to present in this report such of the findings as give some indication of future usefulness in science, in manufacturing, in medicine, or in other applications. The few results set down in this introductory section are only a selection from the whole record of the year. 

The report as a whole indicates the range of events and findings in research carried out in Commission-owned and financed laboratories during 1960.

Hardly any of the research findings constitutes an end to the creation of new knowledge in that particular line of investigation. This is the nature of basic research. Its revelations at any particular time raise more questions than they answer. We hope the report gives, how-

ever, a feel for the great volume and variety of study being carried on in both the life science and the physical science laboratories sponsored by the Atomic Energy Commission. 

Descriptions of Commission laboratory research have been, in former years, a part of the Commission's regular semiannual or annual reports to Congress. The scope of the Commission's activities in industrial and administrative fields, necessarily covered in the regular Annual Report to Congress, has become so great that research findings could not, in that context, receive the attention they should have. For this reason, and to provide an appropriate publication for the Congress and the public specifically interested in research, the Commission plans hereafter to make each year a special report to the Congress on research findings and.activities. This is the first of the special reports. 

THE LIFE SCIENCES-MEDICINE 

In the life sciences, we first take up some of the medical findings and events. • For diagnosis of tumors, more accurate detection was achieved, and the location was more precisely given for certain tumors, by the use of a new radioisotope-iodine 125-that emits soft or low energy X-rays instead of the gamma rays emitted by many of the isotopes similarly used. Iodine 125 has the advantage in cost and convenience of a half-life over seven times as great as the more usual form of radioactive iodine 131. This has a longer shelf life than iodine 131. It also has the advantage of emitting no beta radiation so that the energy absorbed in tissue per atomic disintegration is 10 to 20 times less than from iodine 131.

At the E. O. Lawrence Radiation Labora tory in California, a beam of alpha particles from the 184 inch synchrocyclotron has been used to treat patients with tumor of the pituitary gland, located beneath the brain. Irradiation of the pituitary also has been used successfully on some patients with diabetes mellitus to arrest blindness developing because of damage to the eye's retina. In a study of 18 patients, this type of blindness was arrested in more than half following pituitary irradiation. The work of the past year indicates that the pituitary gland does not have to be destroyed entirely to produce this good effect. • Researchers and physicians at the Argonne

Cancer Research Hospital in Chicago are now using the electron beam from a linear accelerator to treat selected cancer patients. 

This use of an electron beam permits very precise focusing of radiation on the area to be treated. 

Previously X-ray beams, gamma ray fluxes, and neutrons have been used for medical purposes. Electron beams have been used at the 

Argonne Hospital only since 1959. It is too early to state what results can be expected, but this machine makes it possible to treat tumors that previously could not be treated with external radiation because of the danger of injuring nearby tissues. • New equipment is continually being developed by the medical researchers. During the past year, two different kinds of scanners, one a linear scanner, the other an improved area scanner, were built and started into work, the linear scanner identifying areas of radioactivity along the length of the human body but not across the width of the body, turned out to be useful in evaluating areas of diffused activity, much better than the old area scanner. It revealed metastatic lesions in patients with thyroid cancer. The improved area scanner is being used mainly to study patients with thyroid cancer and to detect and locate brain tumors. It may be of value for detecting tumors of the liver.

There is continuous improvement in the various types of counters, including whole body counters-now in use at 30 or more installations. The most recent development is that through greater sensitivity of the whole body counters, doctors now can use lower amounts of radioactivity in "atomic cocktails" or in injections for diagnostic work. • In radiotracer work using carbon 14 to label morphine, it has been revealed that morphine addicts excrete chemical compounds that are

THE BASIC RESEARCH PROGRAMS 

different from those excreted by people who are not addicts but have been given morphine for medical reasons. The results indicate that addicts and normal people metabolize morphine differently, and this fact may lead to discoveries of value in helping to solve problems of drug addiction. 

A new development in radiation detection instruments of the past year is a pocket gamma radiation detector, the size and shape of a fountain pen, to be clipped in the pocket to help protect workers in atomic installations. If the person wearing it enters a gamma radiation field, the alarm sounds a "screamer device," its pitch and loudness directly related to the strength of the radiation. The detector also flashes a warning light which blinks more rapidly for higher radiation levels. 

These are some of the findings which are written on the endlessly unrolling scroll of medical research using atomic energy instruments and materials. 

THE LIFE SCIENCES-BIOLOGY 

In biological research, development of radiation diagnosis and therapy for human beings is an important end product. Results of this research sometimes can be carried on to humans; sometimes not. But it often gives an indication of possible human treatment or diagnosis. Or again, they may simply add to our knowledge of what radiation will do to living systems in the plant field as well as in the animal field.

Following are a few results reported from 1960 research which indicate the range and the scope of findings in biological research. Here, for example, are five such pieces of research work intended to make contributions to medical science. • Argonne National Laboratory studied the influence of radiation dose rate and total absorbed radiation from an injected dose of strontium 90 on inducing cancers in mice. The results seemed to indicate that a high level of radiation immediately after injection was as much responsible for the development of bone tumors as was long continuation of radiation. 

Research continues to define the results further. • Experiments have confirmed that the regression of rat tumors accomplished by injecting radioactive antibodies into the laboratory animals was the result of beta radiation from iodine 131 integrated into the antibodies. Intensive work is continuing looking toward the possibility of using radioactive antibodies to treat certain types of tumors in human beings. • Studies of bone fractures in young and aged mice and of intestinal cell formation in normal and aged mice, demonstrated that one phenomenon of old age in mice was a slower production of the cells being examined. In the bone fracture work, it was found that the slower production of cells could account for slower healing of bone fractures in the aged. • Studies of radiation-induced leukemia in mice indicate that there was a direct relationship between the amount of high intensity radiation and the incidence of leukemia; that female mice are more subject to leukemia under these conditions than were males, that physiological factors are more important in the development of leukemia than are radiation induced mutations in cells. • Further findings in genetic work with mice have confirmed the early observation that chronic irradiation over a period of weeks caused fewer genetic mutations than the same total amount of radiation administered at one time. Similar results were found in backup research with fruitflies.

Biological research which does not contribute directly to human medicine includes a variety of items. The following are examples of 1960 

results in this type of research: 

The observation of vegetation at the Lockheed reactor site in Georgia, where a special testing nuclear reactor is operated without shielding for research connected with the Commission's Aircraft Nuclear Propulsion program, gave some surprising findings about the sensitivities of trees to radiation. The trees received a cumulative dose ranging from 30,-

roentgens of gamma rays and neutrons at 

500 feet to about 4,000 roentgens at 1,000 feet.

Even at 4,000 roentgens, all pine trees were killed. Hardwoods oaks and hickories-were not killed but they lost their leaves earlier in the fall and leafed out later in the spring. Because of late leafing, there was an island of lifeless hardwoods after surrounding vegetation had come into full leaf in the spring of 1959. 

Some 10 years of experiments with plants lead to a conclusion that the larger the nuclear volume of cells, the smaller the amount of irradiation required to inhibit growth of the plants. It is not yet clear whether such factors can be used to predict what are the lethal levels of radiation for various species of plants. 

THE PHYSICAL SCIENCES 

It is difficult for the nonscientist to grasp the detail of what has been found by the scientists who do physical research in the Commission program. However, some illumination can be shed on progress in this field. We are selecting from this report five accounts of events, or results of work, to provide examples of the progress of Commission research in the past year. The examples were drawn chiefly from work done in Commission-owned laboratories. Representative research done in universities and other institutions under Commission contracts is reviewed in the body of this report.

One notable event of the year was the attainment of a beam of protons at an energy of more than 30 billion electron volts at the new

Alternating Gradient Synchrotron at Brookhaven National Laboratory, Upton, Long 

Island, N.Y. This occurred on July 29, 1960.

The Brookhaven accelerator is one of the world's two largest. The other accelerator of comparable energy is a proton synchrotron at the CERN (European Organization for Nu clear Research) Laboratory near Geneva, Switzerland, which, late in 1959, first produced a proton beam and has since attained an energy of 28 billion electron volts. 

Operation of the AGS will enable scientists in the United States to study nuclear interactions in the laboratory at energies about 5 times greater than ever before possible. By carrying on such studies, the physicists at Brookhaven expect to learn more about the many kinds of particles such as mesons and hyperons and the various "antiparticles" that are produced by bombarding target nuclei with high energy protons.

Thus is taken another step in the high energy physics research of the Commission. 

If everything goes according to schedule, two other machines with energies in the billions of electron volts will come into operation this next year. One is the 6-billion-volt Harvard- 

Massachusetts Institute of Technology electron synchrotron in Cambridge, Mass. The other is the 3-billion-volt proton synchrotron for 

Princeton-Pennsylvania at Princeton, N.J.

Thus 1960 and 1961 will see the most extensive addition to the high power research equipment in high energy physics ever accomplished. 

Other physics research brings results which are mainly of interest to physicists. 

We may cite, first, the experiments in resonance absorption at Argonne National Laboratory, Los Alamos Scientific Laboratory, Brookhaven National Laboratory, and in several other laboratories. The experiments have made use of the so-called "Mössbauer effect" as a very sensitive detector of small shifts in radiation energy and therefore also of small shifts in frequency and wavelength. 

Some of the experimental "Mössbauer effects" apparently are sensitive enough to measure under laboratory conditions the "gravitational red shift" predicted by Einstein's Theory of 

Relativity. The "red shift" theory predicts that the frequency of light will decrease somewhat under the influence of a gravitational field, that is, will vibrate more slowly when in free space, and hence shift toward the red part of the spectrum as far as visible light is concerned.

Although astronomers have been searching for evidence of this red shift using spectro-

THE BASIC RESEARCH PROGRAMS 

LO scopes to analyze the wavelengths of light from the sun and other stars that have very strong gravitational fields, the results are not at all clear cut.

Experiments are currently underway at Harvard University and at the British Atomic 

Energy Research Establishment, Harwell, England, to measure resonance absorption with the source of radiation and the absorber of radiation treated by the "Mössbauer" method at different heights from each other. Preliminary results so far have indicated that there is an effect, even though the quantitative data are still insufficient to provide a conclusively sensitive test of the gravitational red shift. • Research in metallurgy and materials like that in physics is usually recondite. This report gives several pages to results in metallurgy. 

One of the most significant is the use of new techniques which produce extremely rapid rates of cooling of metal systems. Through the use of this method, new structures in metal systems have been produced that had been predicted theoretically but never before observed. Use of this technique contributes to an improved understanding of the fundamental nature and behavior of solids. The technique is to project a molten alloy to be studied on a cylinder of solid copper rotating at high speed so that the molten alloy forms a thin film. 

This technique permits small amounts of liquid alloys to be cooled to room temperature sufficiently rapidly to prevent the normal process of nucleation and growth of equilibrium phases. The structure of these alloys is different from that normally obtained and their properties are unusual. They are ideally suited for studying the effect of electron irradiation on the structure of alloys. 

The new method was first applied to alloys of silver and copper; then it was used on silvergermanium alloys. As a side consequence of the experiment, the scientists discovered a new compound of silver and germanium.

The main effect, however, was the demonstration that some alloys could be quenched fast enough from the molten state to attain a quasiliquid structure. Such alloys could be studied in the solid state and thereby provide a greatly simplified experimental approach to studying the structures of liquids.

In chemistry, one of the interesting things now going on is the program for producing larger quantities of transplutonium elements. 

About three years ago, there was inserted in the Materials Testing Reactor at Idaho a spe cially fabricated shim rod containing 78 grams of plutonium. After two years of irradiation in the MTR's high neutron flux, followed by 4 months of cooling, the rod was chemically processed at Argonne National Laboratory to separate the remaining plutonium isotopes from the heavier elements formed by neutron capture. The processing yielded the largest quantities of certain heavy isotopes ever preparedabout 5 grams of plutonium 242, one gram of americium 243, and a half gram of curium 244.

The materials will be converted to oxides, mixed with aluminum powder, and compressed into pellets. These will be irradiated again in the MTR. In approximately three years, these samples are expected to yield about 1 milligram of the elements, berkelium and californium, and some micrograms of einsteinium-elements No. 

98, 99, and 100 in the Periodic Table of the 

Elements. 

A project for producing larger amounts of the very heavy elements is now going on at the 

Savannah River Plant. There, 12 kilograms of plutonium were inserted in a reactor in 1959. 

By 1963 and 1964, the irradiation is expected to produce relatively large quantities of plutonium 242, americium 243, and curium 244 as feed material for further irradiation in the MTR and in a new special High-Flux Isotope Reactor which the Commission plans to build at Oak Ridge National Laboratory. This irradiation will eventually lead to production of gram quantities of berkelium and californium. 

With relatively large quantities of very heavy elements available, the scientists hope during the middle and late 1960's to synthesize elements 103 and 104, and perhaps even heavier elements.

Element No.

102 is the highest so far identified in the Periodic Table of the Elements. 

The thermonuclear research programaimed at production ultimately of power from the fusion of the light elements-continues to command the work of several hundred scientists and technicians. By now it is generally understood that they are trying to produce a plasma at thermonuclear temperatures, to confine it with magnetic fields, and to determine finally the possibility or impossibility of fusion machines producing net power. In the early days of thermonuclear research, optimistic statements were made about the date of the general availability at competitive costs of power released by fusing hydrogen isotopes. 

Now it is understood that this lies well in the future.

The people in charge of the thermonuclear program present in this report the highlights of progress during the past 4 years, which are summarized in the following paragraphs: 

In 1956, there was no work on high-energy injection of plasmas into a magnetic fieldonly preparations for it. In 1960, ions have been injected and trapped copiously at energies far above those required for effective thermonuclear reaction. Here the problem is to increase the confinement time, and with it, the plasma density.

In 1956, stability of plasmas in magnetic mirror machines had not been demonstrated convincingly. The theoretical indications then were that the plasmas would be unstable at all pertinent energies and densities. Transient confinement now has been demonstrated at energies some 30 times above those of 1956. In early 1956, it was not possible to say with certainty that any of the four major laboratories working in this field had produced a plasma with an ionic energy greater than 100 electron volts. Now it appears very probable that this energy has been pushed up well beyond 1,000 ev, an increase by a factor of more than 10. A further increase by a factor of about five would achieve the ideal ignition energy predicted theoretically for the fusion reaction between deuterium and tritium. 

Energies of 1,000 to 2,000 ev were achieved by means of shock waves in a magnetic mirror device and with relatively dense plasmas containing more than 10 million billion ions per cubic centimeter. In addition, an adiabatic compression mirror device has produced a stable medium density plasma, containing about 20 billion ions per cubic centimeter and in which the ion temperature is about 2,800-about half the ideal ignition energy, predicted theoretically for the fusion reaction. 

Achieving "ignition energy"—that is, the equivalent in kinetic energy of the temperature that is necessary to create a self-sustaining thermonuclear reaction-is significantly closer to attainment than in 1956. Attaining ignition energy is only the first step toward production of power from the fusion reaction. Before the reaction can become self-sustaining, at least as much energy must be produced as is dissipated in the reaction. Net production of energy then must be achieved and afterward an economically competitive power producer must be designed.

We are continuing to push forward with all promising lines of research in the controlled thermonuclear field to the end that we may ultimately produce power from fusion reactors.

The Commission's program of biomedical research is directed primarily toward understanding the effects of ionizing radiation on living systems and developing effective methods for preventing or counteracting these effects. It continues also to provide leadership in developing uses of radiation and radioisotopes in medical diagnostic and therapeutic studies, and in biology and agriculture.

The work is carried on in the Commission's national laboratories and research centers (see 

Appendix 2 for list), and under contracts at institutions of learning and research (see Appendix 3 for list of contracts for fiscal year ended June 30, 1960). Only research in Commission laboratories is described in this report. 

The medical and life sciences program described here concentrated research in four major categories: biological research, medical research, environmental sciences research, and radiation detection instruments.

Details of the Commission's program in nuclear effects related to civil defense, including studies of blast biology, self-protection against fallout and blast and thermal effects from nuclear detonations will be described in the Annual Report to Congress for 1960, to be issued 

January 1961. Results of the Commission's program for measuring deposition of fallout from nuclear weapons tests have been published periodically as collected (see bibliography in 

Appendix 1), and are summarized each quarter in the regular publications of the U.S. Public 

Health Service, Department of Health, Educa tion, and Welfare, which now has responsibility for issuing this class of information.

CANCER STUDIES Medical Research 

The Commission supports cancer research because nuclear radiations can be both a cause of cancer and an effective means of treating it. 

Use of nuclear radiation devices with extremely high intensities and energies has opened up new avenues of treatment for these diseases. 

The causes of cancer are complex; radiation is only one way of systematically investigating this field. Cancer research sponsored by the 

Commission embodies studies into the causes. and prevention of cancer as well as into new approaches to treatment that use the tools of atomic energy. 

DANGER 

HID Electron-beam Accelerator. General view of the evacuated accelerating tube of the linear accelerator used by 

Argonne Cancer Research Hospital to produce beams of high-energy electrons for the treatment of cancer. The unit was constructed to investigate the clinical advantages of electron beams which, to a greater extent than X-rays and gamma-rays, have a well-defined penetration range based on the energy of the electrons. The device can produce energies from 5 to 50 million electron volts.

Rotational Irradiator. Upper photo shows the projection head, of the electron-beam linear accelerator during its construction at the Argonne Cancer Research Hospital. The electrons, entering the focusing head from the accelerator on the other side of the wall (to right), are directed at the patient by three electromagnets. The lower photo shows the completed device as it is used for cancer therapy. The electrons emerge from the cylindrical tube in controlled patterns ranging from a beam the size of a small pencil to area coverage eight inches in diameter. Deep-seated lesions can be treated by rotating the focusing head around the patient.

FILMS EXPOSED TO ELECTRONS FIELD SIZE 

10 x 10 CM 5 cm 10 MEV 15 MEV 20 MEV 25 MEV 30 MEV Controlled Penetration. Photographic film, exposed at the focusing head of the Argonne Cancer Research Hospital's linear accelerator, graphically shows how the penetration power of an electron beam can be controlled. 

The depth of penetration is determined by the energy of the electron beam and, unlike X-rays and gamma-rays which continue to penetrate the body tissues past the target, electron penetration can be limited very precisely. 

At 5 million electron volts (Mev) the beam will penetrate one inch of human tissue and at 50 Mev, ten inches. 

Tumor Treatment 

High-energy electrons in treatment. X-ray beams and gamma-ray fluxes have been used for medical purposes for more than 60 years. 

Electron beams, on the other hand, have been in use for a comparatively short time. Among instruments used for electron-beam therapy is a linear accelerator having a beam-scanning device at the Commission's Argonne Cancer Research Hospital, University of Chicago.

The electron source consists of a 50-million electron volt traveling-wave linear accelerator. 

Electrons are accelerated by means of an electro-magnetic wave-the kind used in some radar systems confined to an evacuated accelerating tube. The wave is formed initially by a magnetron tube and amplified by two high-powered (20-megawatt) klystron tubes, like the tubes used in television broadcasting. Use of two klystrons permits regulating the phase relationship between two parts of the electro-magnetic wave in the accelerator, thus providing precise control over beam energy. The unit can produce an electron beam with energy from 

5 to 50 million electron volts. 

The accelerated electrons emerge from the accelerator and enter a beam-scanning device

in which three electromagnets can guide a beam of electrons over any portion of the body. The range of sizes available is from a spot the size of a pencil to an 8-inch band completely encircling the patient. Any size and shape within this range can be selected for uniform irradiation simply by inserting a plastic cutout template which will guide the scanning movement of the beam.

Experimental treatment of selected cancer patients with this machine was started in June 1959, which has proved capable of treating tumors not previously accessible with other machines. 

Pituitary irradiation. The 184-inch synchro cyclotron which produces a 900 Mev beam of alpha particles, has been used at the E. O. 

Lawrence Radiation Laboratory, University of California, Berkeley, to destroy the pituitary selectively in patients with pituitary tumors, and in palliative control of widespread breast cancer 

Pituitary irradiations known to influence favorably retinitis (damage to the eye's retina) which leads to blindness in certain patients with diabetes mellitus. In a study of 

18 patients, this type of blindness was arrested in more than half following pituitary irradiation. The work of the past year indicates that the pituitary does not have to be destroyed entirely to produce this beneficial effect. 

Palladium 103. Argonne Cancer Research

Hospital is systematically studying radiotherapeutic agents suitable for use in conjunction with surgical procedures for advanced cancers. 

Palladium 103, which emits low energy X-rays, may prove to be useful for special purposes.

In preparing this material for clinical use, a coarse suspension of palladium was designed which may be more likely to remain at the implantation site and have less tendency to migrate into the lymphatic system of the patient. 

This therapy has been tested thoroughly in experimental animals, and now is being used in patients. Some appear to have received very significant palliative effects. 573517 0-61- Cest Radioisotope "Bank". Radioisotopes used at the 

Argonne Cancer Research Hospital are kept in lead containers within a steel and concrete below-floor-level vault when not in use. In above photo, a technician lifts the radioisotope source holder from its lead "can" while a fellow technician monitors the radiation level for safety. The blackboard in the background is used to inventory the radioactive contents of each of the vault's storage holes. 

Wrapping the material with thin lead foil is more than adequate to eliminate any significant radiation exposure to the physician. 

One difficulty with palladium 103, as produced by neutron bombardment in reactors, is that it must contain large amounts of inert, or carrier, palladium. The hospital has investigated the possibilities of making palladium free of the inert material by proton bombardment of rhodium with the 86-inch Cyclotron at 

Oak Ridge National Laboratory.

Iodine 125. The use of isotopes that emit soft 

X-rays has been explored for the diagnosis of tumors. In scanning the liver with radiocounters after injection of radiogold 198 or radioiodine 131, for example, metastatic tumors are not readily detectable against a background of

ACRH 

Remote Handling. Remote control "master-slave" arms are used in the preparation of material for radioisotope therapy at the Argonnne Cancer Research Hospital. In addition to using the tested radioisotopes available from 

Commission and commercial sources, scientists at the Argonne hospital and elsewhere investigate the possibilities of using new and untried radioisotopes for cancer therapy and the problems involved in their production and medical application. high-energy gamma radiation penetrating through the tumors from underlying tissue. A much higher degree of contrast can be achieved by using an isotope such as iodine 125, which emits soft X-rays. The 60-day half-life of iodine 125 which at first appeared a problem, has proved to be an advantage. Since the biological half-life of carrier-free radioiodine in the body is 6 to 8 days, the long physical half-life is really of little importance. The difference in physical half-life is even less important when this iodine is used to tag rose bengal dye for liver scanning, since this material is eliminated from the liver within a few hours, and from the body within a day. 

On the other hand, the longer shelf life of the material, 71/2 times that of iodine 131, offers an advantage in cost and convenience. An additional advantage is that iodine 125 emits no beta radiation, and the radiation energy absorbed in tissue per atomic disintegration is less than that of iodine 131 by a factor of 10 to 20. 

Production of iodine 125 is by the neutron bombardment of xenon 124. If it proves technically possible to irradiate xenon gas com-

ОП pressed to 100 to 200 atmospheres, curie quantities of iodine 125 can easily become available. 

The feasibility of using a thick-walled cylinder of zircaloy 2 as a vessel to contain xenon during irradiation is being investigated.

Studies in Internal Use of Radioisotopes 

Use of radioisotopes as internal sources of irradiation are being emphasized at the Medi cal Division, Oak Ridge Institute of Nuclear 

Studies. Specific objectives include (a) appraisal of the administration of radioisotopes in the lymphatics for selective irradiation, and (b) external scanning to characterize rapidly the general pattern of internal radioisotopic behavior.

Yttrium 90 for selective irradiation. A few years ago, yttrium for the treatment of malignancies in body cavities was tested. Animal studies at Oak Ridge Institute of Nuclear

Studies showed the need for a stable yttrium carrier to immobilize the radioactive yttrium 

90 as a source of internal irradiation. A severe pain reaction of some patients after intraperitoneal administration of yttrium chloride led to evaluation of a preparation of yttrium that would be immobilized. Distribution studies with animals indicated, however, that the preparation was not sufficiently stable biologically for selective immobilization. 

Earlier studies indicated that certain rareearth radioisotopes (including radioactive isotopes of lutetium, holmium, and yttrium), potentially useful in irradiating specific sites of malignancies, become concentrated in the lymphatic vessels and nodes draining the particular region of the body in which they had been administered. This led to an evaluation of direct intralymphatic administration in experimental animals of radioactive colloidal gold, radiocerium chloride, and radioyttrium chloride. All these materials showed a strong tendency to irradiate selectively many of the lymph nodes along the path of drainage. 

Proper combination of chemical, dose volume, and administration rate, may provide a practical use for intralymphatic therapy.

Greater availability of such relatively shortlived radioisotopes of medical interest as yttrium 90 would greatly promote their usefulness. The Institute is testing a generator designed at Brookhaven National Laboratory to prepare yttrium 90 from its parent, strontium 90, quickly and with minimal strontium contamination. The generator, loaded with 

100 millicuries of strontium, yields more than 

80 millicuries of yttrium 90 weekly. The product can be separated and prepared for administration in about one hour.

Distribution pattern. A small-animal linear scanner at Oak Ridge Institute of Nuclear

Studies has proved a convenient way of screening rapidly the general internal pattern of radioisotopic distribution. A scan of the animal, recording radioactivity in transverse sections from head to tail, together with a general knowledge of a particular radioisotope, supplies much useful information. Repeated scans of the same animal can trace in a general way the translocation of the material. Planned studies include the use of radioisotopes which have different patterns according to the compound in which they are included. The method is not quantitative. 

Linear scanner. Studies with an improved scanning instrument, a linear scanner, suggest that the device has practical value in indicating the location and relative amount of certain radioisotopes in various parts of the body. It can be used for diagnostic and therapeutic applications. 

The linear scanner consists of two rows of scintillation crystals with appropriate electronic equipment for pulse-height analysis and continuous recording of count rate. One row of detectors is above the table that holds the patient, positioned at right angles to the length of the patient's body; the other, identical row of detectors is similarly positioned beneath the table. Both rows of detectors scan only one

Small-animal Scanner. A small-animal linear scanner is used at the Oak Ridge Institute of Nuclear Studies as a quick and convenient method of studying the distribution of radioisotopes administered to rats and mice. 

In above photo, a white rat (center left) rides over the scanner head while the radioactivity in various parts of its body are recorded automatically on paper tape. Repeated scans of the animal can determine movement of the radioactive material within the body. transverse segment of the body at a time. As the patient and table are automatically moved through the scanning area, the record shows the amount of radioactivity at all points along the length of the patient's body. A scan of the whole length of the body takes about 15 minutes. 

The linear scanner fills an important gap between area scanners and whole-body counters, but, as now used, it identifies areas of activity only along the length of the body, not across the width of the body. However, it can evaluate areas of diffuse activity, not sharply localized, much better than an area scanner.

The instrument has revealed unsuspected metastatic lesions (spread of cancer) in patients with thyroid cancer and has given patterns of isotope distribution in several diagnostic and therapeutic situations.

The linear scanner is one of a number of instruments being developed to define, without discomfort, the presence of radioactivity in patients. Assays of radioactivity in living tissues-muscle, liver, etc.-often involve operative procedures. The scanners, and related instruments, obtain much the same information from outside the patient's body.

Information obtained by such studies includes identification of accidentally ingested radioactivity, assay of radioisotopes formed in the body after accidental neutron exposure, diagnostic studies of liver and kidney function, and study of the localization of radioisotopes given for therapeutic purposes. Area scanner. An improved area scanner, designed and built for research by Oak Ridge National Laboratory, has been in clinical use since January 1960, at the Oak Ridge Institute of Nuclear Studies, primarily to study patients with thyroid carcinoma, and to detect and locate brain tumors. It may be of value for detecting tumors of the liver. An area scanner is used to locate and define, both longitudinally and laterally, an area in the body which has concentrated differentially an appropriate radioisotope by recording the radiation emissions of the isotope.

The heart of this improved scanner is the focusing element, or collimator. Made of solid gold it is a three-inch-long tapered honeycomb with many tapered hexagonal holes focused on a point three inches from the tip of the collimator; the hole is enclosed by a tungsten shield. Gold and tungsten were chosen for their shielding characteristics, and their use improves the directional characteristics of the radiation counter. 

Preliminary work with thyroid carcinoma. indicates this scanner may be able to detect some areas better than older scanners because a tumor in the area in question may have taken up only small amounts of radioiodine, and hence the radiation produced would have been only very slightly above the general level of

LINEAR SCAN 

Scanning Results. The linear scanner produces an easily-read picture of the distribution of a radioactive therapeutic compound administered to a patient at the 

Oak Ridge Institute of Nuclear Studies. The linear scan is a record in which the height of the line above the base line indicates the amount of radioactivity in that particular portion of the body. In above photo, the highest concentration is in the chest of the patient. radiation in the surrounding tissue. Radioactivity distributed throughout the body is less prominently shown by this scanner, whereas localized areas of uptake stand out clearly. 

Thus, this new scanner appears to offer an improved facility for diagnosis and treatment. 

Use of this scanner also may improve diagnostic location of brain tumors. Three patients with brain tumors receiving radioiodinated serum albumin or arsenic 74 were diagnosed by use of the new scanner. Microscopic study of removed tissue confirmed the diagnosis in these cases. 

In one case, the scanner failed to show a pituitary tumor. 

Calcium 47 studies. A number of laboratories now are using newly available calcium 47, which has a short half-life and easily detected gamma emissions, in studies of mineral metabolism and bone physiology. Some possible uses include studies of malignant tumors with bone involvement, Paget's disease, and diseases of the parathyroid glands. High counts at the sites of bone metastases of cancer have been reported following administration of calcium 47, but this has not been fully explored. Conceivably, this isotope might be employed to detect lesions in bone before X-rays could reveal them. 

Calcium 47 also can be used to measure efficacy of therapy in cases of malignant tumors involving bone lesions, as for example hormonal therapy of cases of carcinoma of the breast that has metasticized to bone. A number of such studies are now underway and a great deal of information is expected to result as more calcium 47 becomes available. 

A study of the fate in man of intravenously and orally administered calcium 47 has been undertaken at the Oak Ridge Institute of Nuclear Studies. Thus far it has confirmed the patterns of plasma disappearance, and excretion and accretion rates found with calcium 45. 

Total-Body Irradiation and Marrow Grafts 

It has long been thought that low doses of total-body irradiation might benefit patients with chronic leukemia and polycythemia, forms of cancer of the blood. Much larger doses of total-body irradiation will produce temporary remissions also in certain cases of acute leukemia. Of some clinical usefulness, this response opens possibilities for basic discoveries about leukemia, and helps explain some remissions previously believed to have resulted from successful transplantation of bone-marrow.

Research on total-body irradiation has continued at the Oak Ridge Institute of Nuclear

Studies, where clinical applications have been made of the results of laboratory investigations on animals. Studies of the usefulness of transplants of bone-marrow are a part of this work. 

Attempts to transplant bone-marrow from volunteer donors to human patients usually have been unsuccessful except in the rare circumstance where an identical twin is available as donor. The patient's body rejects the alien bone-marrow cells by a process analogous to its rejection of foreign proteins. Some temporary survival of homologous (same species) bone-

Research Scanner. A specially-built research scanner at the Oak Ridge Institute of Nuclear Studies is used to define, laterally and longitudinally, minute amounts of radioactivity in a human body. The scanner, whose focusing element is made of gold and tungsten, is proving extremely useful in the study of thyroid carcinomas and for detecting and localizing brain tumors. The radioactivity pattern, as detected by the scanner, is traced on paper automatically (center of photo). marrow grafts is believed to have occurred. If techniques for more successful homologous marrow grafts can be perfected, this may open the way to improved treatment of certain blood diseases, and may allow advances in the use of certain anticancer drugs, or in transplantation of other tissues. Studies of total-body irradiation followed by marrow grafting are of importance also in understanding the effects of radiation, and have contributed to methods of treating people exposed to large doses of irradiation. 

Analysis of results of treating with whole body radiation a group of patients having acute and subacute leukemia has indicated that there were distinct, brief remissions of acute leukemia after relatively high dosages. Cells of acute leukemia appear often to be quite radiosensitive; rapid breakdown of leukemic cells occurred soon after large doses of irradiation. 

Similarly, in subacute leukemia, irradiation gave some remission. All leukemic patients, after treatment, showed some degree of bonemarrow hypoplasia (defective development).

In other patients the leukemia reactivated after only temporary suppression of the leukemic cell count without any real remission in the disease.

Besides high doses of irradiation in acute leukemia, physicians have treated several patients with chronic leukemia and other malignant diseases with low doses (50 r) of totalbody irradiation. None experienced significant symptoms or adverse effects from the radiation. 

Some patients have shown improvement in the disease together with a reduction toward normal of the elevated white blood counts; other patients showed no definite change. As knowledge is gained at this dose level, higher doses will be given. The present study is planned to relate the results gained to the effects of using higher doses, and to test newer biochemical and hematologic techniques in evaluating possible benefits. 

In May 1960, the first patients were treated in a newly constructed facility for total-body irradiation. It provides much more uniform radiation dose to the whole body than was possible with previous instruments or techniques.

The new equipment also makes it possible to deliver a given total dose over a wide choice of time intervals. The optimal dose for clinical work is not yet known, but better control is essential to any precise understanding of therapy by total-body irradiation.

The facility consists of a small room which has eight cesium 137 sources of 500 curies each, one at ceiling and floor in each corner of the 

The sources are fixed on thick lead wheels which can rotate the source to face into room.

The patient lies on a bed in the center of the room where radiation intensity is uniform. Thick concrete walls and a labyrinthine entrance confine radiation. to the treatment room, but by means of a series of angled mirrors-a periscope arrangementthe patient can be observed during treatment. 

Contributing Research with Animals 

Radiation as a cause of cancer. Argonne National Laboratory studies the influence of radiation dose rate and total absorbed radiation dose on inducing cancers in mice by varying the pattern of injecting radioactive carcinogens.

Strontium 90 was given to mice either in a single injection, or in 5 injections once a week for 5 weeks, or in 20 injections five times a week for 4 weeks. Three levels of strontium 90 activity were used, all within ranges known to

The response of the skeleton to these injections was influenced as much by the high dose rate immediately after injection as by the continuous irradiation resulting from material retained in the body. By extending this technique to other levels of exposure and other degrees of divided doses, it may be possible to delineate the minimum dose rate, and the minimum total accumulated energy, that induces malignant bone tumors.

Radiosensitivity and oxygen. Radiosensitivity of living tissues is increased by increasing the oxygen present or dissolved in tissues, and decreased when this oxygen tension is decreased. 

Changes in oxygen tension in several tissues

Unit Model. A model shows the layout and construction of the new total-body irradiation unit at the Oak 

Ridge Institute of Nuclear Studies. The treatment room is at the center, the light grey rectangular objects representing the cesium 137 radiation sources. The angled entry-way reduces the possibility of the radiation affecting the unit operator (black rectangle is control panel) since radioactive particles and rays travel only in straight lines. The unit was built into a hillside to obtain additional shielding from the soil. and tumors in rats, given air or pure oxygen to breathe, have been studied at the Medical 

Division of Oak Ridge Institute of Nuclear 

Studies. Breathing pure oxygen raised the tension of tissue oxygen about threefold in rat skin, muscle, and bone-marrow. The relative increase of oxygen in a tumor was, however, extremely variable, ranging from no increase at all to an approximation of that observed in normal tissues. This variation probably is due to the disorganization of blood supply that occurs in rapidly growing tumors. 

Antibody therapy. At the University of 

Rochester Atomic Energy Project, successful experiments in treating rat tumors with radioactive antibodies have been supplemented by control experiments measuring the effect on tumor growth of nonradioactive antibodies, of X-ray therapy (either whole body or directed

at the tumor area only), and of X-rays directed at the tumor to supplement the effect of nonradioactive antibodies. These control experiments make it clear that the tumor regression obtained in experiments with radioactive antibodies resulted from the beta-gamma radiation of the iodine 131 carried to the tumor by the antibody. 

Intensive work designed to lead to human therapy is continuing in related fields. 

TRACER TECHNIQUES IN MEDICINE 

The labeling of molecules with radioactive substances has opened up new techniques for tracing the pathways in the human body of metabolically active substances, for determining the ways in which cells divide and multiply, and for studies of the formation of bone and healing of fractures.

Cell Metabolism 

In cancer. The synthesis of tritium-labeled thymidine allows the determination of what cells among others are about to reproduce themselves by dividing. Cells that have incorporated tritium labeled thymidine can be followed to determine their life span since thymidine, once incorporated into the nucleus of cells about to undergo mitosis (division),. stays there. The tritium can be followed by radioactive tracing methods. 

At the Brookhaven Medical Research Center 

Hospital, tritium labeled thymidine was injected into cancer patients to measure the rates of producing cancerous cells compared with the normal. It was found that thymidine was very rapidly removed from the blood and that the labeled material appeared in bone-marrow cells as soon as one minute after administration. 

In leukemia. The presence of cells that are synthesizing DNA (deoxyribonucleic acid) in the blood of normal human beings can be demonstrated by incubating the blood for one hour with tritiated thymidine, followed by concentration of the white cells and preparation for autoradiography. In studies at Brookhaven National Laboratory, blood cells labeled in this way has been measured in a series of leukemic patients, and studies also were carried out with patients in whom chronic myelocytic leukemia was being treated with the drug, myeleran. The general conclusions drawn from these studies are as follows:

In 14 patients with chronic lymphocytic leukemia (leukemia of the lymphoid tissue) usually 0.1 percent or less of all white cells were labeled, whereas in normal individuals a much smaller number-approximately 0.04 percent, become labeled. The fact that the small round blood cells of this disease were rarely labeled, taken with the increased number of white blood cells, indicated the presence of large mononuclear cells many of which otherwise resemble cells that label normally.

The blood of five patients with acute and seven patients with chronic myelocytic leukemia (leukemia of the bone-marrow cells) usually showed from 2 to 5 percent labeling, confined essentially to those cells associated with the disease process. In later stages of this illness, more than 25 percent became labeled. Myeleran therapy drastically reduced the number of labeled cells; this effect parallels the decrease in total white cell count.

The findings in general indicate a relatively long lifetime of the blood cells characteristic of leukemia, particularly chronic lymphocytic leukemia. The results with myeleran therapy indicate that this drug affects blood cells equally regardless of whether or not they are synthesizing DNA prior to mitosis.

Molecular Metabolism 

Uric acid. Using radioisotope-labeling of molecules, the metabolism of uric acid in normal subjects and in pathological conditions such as gout, polycythemia and leukemia, has been investigated at the Argonne Cancer Research Hospital. 

By labeling uric acid components with car-

bon 14, and identifying and measuring it subsequently, it has been established that the intestinal tract is an important excretory pathway for uric acid in man. Normal persons appear to excrete one-third of their uric acid in digestive juices. In pathological conditions associated with a high concentration of uric acid in the blood, this type of excretion is still more important since it does not depend on the kidneys.

In the intestinal tract, bacterial enzymes destroy uric acid with the formation of allantoin, allantoic acid and urea as intermediary products, and carbon dioxide and ammonia as principle end-products. Small amounts of the intermediary products are absorbed and excreted in the urine, but most of it becomes carbon dioxide and ammonia. Part of the carbon dioxide is absorbed and excreted through the lungs; another part is utilized by intestinal bacteria. No breakdown of uric acid occurs in human tissue cells. The lumen of the intestinal tract therefore is the only site of this process in man. The content (pool) of uric acid in normal subjects determined by means of isotope dilution tests is of the order of 1.2 grams, and approximately 50 percent is turned over daily. 

Studies of this type in gouty patients have indicated that primary gout can be divided into two types: (a) primary metabolic gout characterized by an over-production of uric acid, and (b) primary renal gout, characterized by normal production and reduced excretion, apparently due to dysfunction of the renal tubules. Three types of secondary gout were identified: (a) secondary to blood dyscrasias (disorders) associated with an increased breakdown of nucleic acids in cellular destruction (b) secondary to renal diseases in which there is a reduced glomerular filtration rate, and (c) drug-induced gout in which a drug (chlorothiazide, pyraxinoic acid) interferes in the renal excretion of uric acid.

Glucose in diabetics. The general extent and metabolic pathways of formation of blood glucose have been studied at Brookhaven National Laboratory in patients with different types of diabetes. This has been done by analyzing the total amount and molecular distribution of carbon 14 appearing in blood glucose within two hours after intravenous administration of precursors of glucose such as lactate and pyruvate labeled with carbon 14.

At times of maximum activity (about one hour after injection), the amount of carbon 14 in free body glucose ranged from 15 percent to 30 percent of the administered amount.

These minimal figures for degree of conversion indicate that glucose formation may be a major product formed by blood lactate, at least in the diabetic. Both insulin and tolbutamide (in sensitive patients) reduced by 50 percent the apparent incorporation of carbon 14 into glucose when injected as the labeled lactate and pyruvate. These findings suggest the treatment has some early inhibitive effect on the production or release of glucose by the liver. 

Protein-bound iodine. Iodine 131 is widely used in medical practice for diagnosis of thyroid disorders. Another diagnostic aid has been provided by refining methods of measuring the amount of protein-bound iodine (PBI) in the blood. Since thyroid hormones contain iodine and are bound to a specific blood protein, the amount of PBI is a direct measure of the amount of thyroid hormone circulating in the blood.

Chemical determination of iodine in the small amounts found in blood is a difficult process, and for this test, protein-bound iodine must be separated from non-protein-bound iodine. A visiting scientist from Norwegian Radium

Hospital, Oslo, has spent a year at Brookhaven National Laboratory stuying factors that influence the determination of protein-bound iodine, and a definitive method has been developed. 

Morphine. Some chemical differences in morphine metabolism between normal humans and

1 1 1 Breath Sampler. This increase in knowledge of morphine metabolism may have value in helping to solve problems of drug addiction.

Bone Cells Studies 

Origin of cells. The process of bone development, growth, and repair depends upon bone formation by cells called osteoblasts and upon bone reabsorption and removal by cells called osteoclasts. Cells of the osteoblastic type containing a single nucleus are known to originate from pre-existing cells called osteogenic cells,

Bone-forming Cells. Through the use of autoradiographs made of broken bones of mice, scientists at the Brookhaven National Laboratory are determining the contribution of various bone cells to the repair of fractures. These autoradiographs, made by exposing stripping film for 20 days to sections of radioactive femoral (thigh bone) tissue, show the formation of new cells in the periosteum (fibrous membrane at bone surface). Tritium labeled thymidine was injected into the animals to label newly forming, or formed, cells. 

Upper photo shows the new cells around the break eight hours after the fracture occurred. The lower photo shows a much greater increase in new cells 

16 hours after the fracture. also containing a single nucleus. However, the origin of the multinucleated osteoclastic cells has remained unknown. This problem has been clarified by studying the appearance of labeled osteoclasts after adminstration to mice. of tritiated thymidine which localizes only in the nucleus of cells about to divide. 

In mice sacrificed shortly after administration of tritiated thymidine, osteogenic cells were found to be frequently labeled, osteoblasts rarely labeled, and osteoclasts labeled not at all.

These findings indicate that osteoclasts do not originate as a consequence either of cell division or of fusion of osteogenic cells. In mice sacrificed after a longer time, when osteogenic cells have divided often enough to produce a large population of labeled osteoblasts, numerous osteoclasts were found that contained one or more labeled nuclei. This suggests that the multinucleated osteoclasts originate with or from the mononucleated osteoblasts. 

Fracture repair. An autoradiographic study made at Brookhaven National Laboratory has helped to determine the contribution of various bone cells to healing of bone fractures at various ages, and to compare the rate of production of these cells with increasing age.

In 45 female Swiss Albino mice taken at ages 

1, 5, 8, 26, and 52 weeks, the right femur (thighbone) of each animal was fractured and the left femur used as a control. Observations were made at 1 day, 1 week and 2 weeks after fracturing. One hour before sacrificing, each mouse received a subcutaneous injection of 0.5 microcuries of tritium labeled thymidine per gram of body weight to label newly forming or formed cells. Autoradiograms were prepared by exposing stripping film for 20 days to sections of the radioactive femoral tissue. 

It was found that the osteogenic (bone forming) cells of the periosteum (fibrous membrane at bone surface) were most frequently labeled, indicating these were reproducing most rapidly. With increasing age, fewer cells appeared. One day after fracture, an increase in population of labeled new cells appeared about the fracture site, even in 52 weeks old mice; however, in this case, the total number of new cells was very small. The overall proliferative response of the osteogenic cells to the stimulus of a fracture decreased with increasing age. 

At the time when the cartilagenous fracture callus was being replaced by bone (next step in fracture repair), numerous osteogenic and osteoblastic cells (another type of bone forming cell) were found labeled.

The results indicate that the potential for

fracture repair resides essentially in the capacity of the cells of the periosteum to proliferate, and this capacity diminishes with increasing age. This could account for the slower rate of repair of fractures of older individuals. 

RADIATION AND CHEMICAL TOXICITY 

Safe beneficial uses of atomic energy depend to a considerable extent on accurate estimates of the concentrations of radioactive material in air, water, and food so that people will not be exposed to the possibility of adverse effects of radiation. These types of estimates are needed for low-level chronic exposure, for short-term or emergency exposure for various population groups, and for occupational exposures. Studies cover a variety of radiotoxic and chemicotoxic materials.

Projecting animal results to man. A critical point in experimental biology and medicine is the extent to which information collected by research on laboratory animals may be extrapolated to man. Uptake, excretion, and retention of gamma-emitting radionuclides in mice, rats, dogs, and monkeys are being studied with whole body counters and compared with studies of men accidentally exposed in similar ways.

Interspecies correlations and comparisons can be sought from such data and possibly some useful common metabolic factors established.

The results help provide data for man, even if constant interspecies correlations cannot be established.

General Studies 

Normal trace elements. Information concerning the normal distribution of a number of trace elements in man is being obtained by Oak Ridge National Laboratory under a subcontract with the University of Tennessee. Spectrographic analysis is being made of dry-ashed tissue from the organs of persons suffering instantaneous accidental death.

Bone-building Response. Studies with mice at Brookhaven National Laboratory show that the proliferative response of osteogenic (bone-forming) cells to repair a fracture drops sharply during the first eight weeks of a mouse's life and then levels off at a very low rate. Such studies as these may later have a bearing on developing techniques for quicker bone repair in humans. may be useful for estimating the allowable concentrations of radioactive material, this program is giving a first indication of the variability of individuals in respect to quantities of trace elements, and of some of the differences to be expected with age, geographical location, and race. The information is useful in determing what modification, if any, should be made in concentration values based on the so-called adult "standard man" when they are applied to members of the population-at-large. 

Radiotoxicity Studies

Radioiodine. Results of work with radioiodine so far do not indicate detectable injury to test animals at levels of exposure to iodine 131 considerably higher than those authorized for workers in atomic energy plants. 

In research at the Hanford laboratories, sheep fed 0.15 microcuries (uc) of iodine 

131 each day (about three times levels considered allowable) showed no damage to the thyroid gland after 8 years of such dosage.

Damage was evidenced by a slight reduction

in thyroid uptake of iodine in the groups of ewes fed 0.5 μc/day (30 times the allowed level) and 1.5 µc/day (100 times) for about 6 years.

Two sheep receiving 1.5 μc/day eventually showed thyroid tumors.

The relative rates of iodine 131 uptake in the thyroids of swine were studied after intraperitoneal and oral administration. Peak uptake was 55 percent at the 10 hours following intraperitoneal administration as compared to 

40 percent at 24 hours after oral administration.

Work on iodine 131 is continuing with emphasis on studies dealing with the effects of exposure in early life, and the possibility that prolonged low-level administration of iodine 

131 may cause thyroid tumors.

Gonadal dose. Other research at Hanford was concerned with the radiation dose to the sex glands that result from internally deposited gamma-emitting isotopes. Sheep were fed two common isotopes, cesium 137 and zinc 65. Prolonged daily consumption of radiozinc resulted in a radiation dose to the testes equal to several times the dose that would be assumed on the basis of the concentration occurring in testes themselves. This is due primarily to gamma rays from the deposition of radiozinc in thigh muscles adjacent to the testes. 

When these data were extrapolated to man for calculation of the concentration for radiozinc allowable in drinking water, the resulting value was within the range recommended as a guide by the National Committee on Radia tion Protection and Measurements. 

Microdosimetry of radium. At certain levels of strontium 90 in mice, or radium 226 in human beings, there is a probability that bone cancer will occur. However, the direct quantitative relationship between the size of the radiation dose received by specific cells, and the probability that these cells may become cancerous, has not been determined. In fact, the actual radiation doses delivered to the cells had not been previously calculated, although the mean dose received by average bone tissue had been derived.

Argonne National Laboratory concentrated attention on localization and the measurement on a microscopic scale, of the actual radiation doses delivered to cells and tissues in bone. 

From suitable high resolution autoradiographs, several techniques have been devised to determine the actual doses delivered to microscopic volumes in bone: measurements of microscopic densitometric film darkening in the case of beta emitters; alpha track counts on high resolution photographic films for low level alpha emitters. 

Studies of the bones of persons with radiumpoisoning showed that there are localized tiny areas of concentrated deposits of the radioisotope, the so-called radium "hot spot". Thus much higher radiation doses are delivered to the cells and tissues of such hot spots than to the rest of tissues in bone; there is a shading off zone between the hot spot and ordinary bone. 

Quantitatively, the studies have shown that, in the case of some 19 individuals who have carried radium 226 in their bodies for many years, the hot spots are, on an average, about 73 times as intense as the diffusely distributed radiation. 

This is because the length of time over which these people were exposed to radium was short, compared to human lifetime.

In the case of radium 226, the ratio of the dose in a hot spot as compared to the dose in diffuse distribution is essentially identical with the ratio of radium concentrations in these regions. This is a consequence of the extremely short range of the radium alpha particles, some 

30 microns in bone. In the case of a beta emitter in bone, such as strontium 90, the greater penetration of the beta radiations would reduce the ratio of exposure between hot spots and diffuse distribution so that the dose would approach that from a diffuse distribution. the case of the alpha emission from radium 226 the actual diffuse distribution is about one-half the hypothetical concentration.

Strontium 90 Toxicity 

In A continuing study at Argonne National Laboratory is concerned with long-term effects

of fission products. For the past few years, major attention has been focused on the relationship between pathologic consequences and (a) patterns of exposure to strontium 90, and (b) age at the time of exposure. These problems are of considerable interest, not only because strontium 90 is a component of fallout, but also because they involve fundamental questions relating to the role of dose rate in radiobiological response, and to the belief that the young are more radiosensitive than mature individuals.

The response of mice to a single intravenous injection of strontium 90 is being compared with the response to its continuous ingestion in food. Adult females were provided with food containing strontium 90 shortly before they were bred. They and their female offspring, exposed to a constant dietary ratio of strontium 90-to-calcium since conception, have been followed in a duration-of-life study. Particular attention has been given to life span, neoplastic, degenerative and infectious diseases. 

The highest dietary level, 10 microcuries of strontium 90 per gram of calcium (equivalent to 5,000 times the limits set for occupational exposure) did not interfere with conception, gestation, parturition, or lactation. Nor were there any obvious defects among the mice maintained at this level since conception. 

However, the median survival time of the young was decreased by 41 percent, and that of the mother was decreased by 17 percent.

Tumors attributable to radiation have appeared among mice maintained throughout life at the highest level of dietary strontium 90 at which they accumulate about 2 microcuries per gram of calcium. However, the incidence of bone cancer has been much lower than anticipated, and probably will not exceed 25 percent.

In mice given a single equivalent injection, the tumor incidence at 450 days (the time when the first tumor death occurred among mice chronically fed strontium 90) was 82 percent. Since the total integrated doses of radiation resulting from these two patterns of exposure are very similar, the differences in response must be based on some other factor of exposure, such as dose rate. When the 450-day period between birth, and death with an osteogenic sarcoma, for the continuously exposed mice is compared with the 150-day period between a single injection in young adult mice and death with a tumor, it appears that very young mice are not necessarily more sensitive than adults to this radiocarcinogenic stimulus.

One of the most striking consequences of the comparison of the single injection experiment with the continuous feeding experiment has been the demonstration that information gained from injection could not be used directly to predict the results from feeding. The inference is that estimates of the hazards of fallout should be based upon data derived from continuous exposures, not from single exposures alone.

In a study at Hanford designed to define the daily oral dose of radiostrontium that will cause significant damage, no lesions were observed in adult miniature swine after one year of feeding strontium 90 at levels of 1, 5, and 25 microcuries a day. One microcurie a day results in a bone concentration of about ten times the level established as a guide for acceptable human exposure in industry. However, offspring of the swine fed 25 microcuries a day showed some bone damage at 4 to 6 months of age.

Six of seven monkeys given strontium 90 in 

1954 and 1956 at the University of Rochester- 

Atomic Energy Project have died, three with malignant neoplasms attributable to strontium 

90 irradiation, the fourth from acute radiation injury, the fifth of sub-acute radiation injury, and the sixth of parasitism accompanying subacute injury. The seventh is alive and well, 52 years after receiving enough strontium 90 to give a present body burden of 1.6 microcuries per kilogram. This monkey has no hematologic evidence of radiation injury at 1,600,000 times the human guide levels.

In another study at the same project, a group of 30 female rats, when 6 months old, were given strontium 90 in their drinking water for a period of 2 weeks. After this they were al-
