Plutonium. In preliminary experiments at

Hanford laboratories, an estimate of the acute lethal dose of inhaled plutonium 239 in dogs has been obtained. Thirty dogs died within four months after intrapulmonary deposition of about 100 microcuries. The lungs showed massive changes consisting mostly of consolidation and fibrosis.

Two dogs were sacrificed 40 weeks after inhaling about two microcuries of plutonium 239. 

During this period, one dog excreted 55 percent of the deposited material in feces and retained 

21 percent in lung, and 22 percent in tracheobronchial lymph nodes. The other dog excreted 38 percent in feces, retained 53 percent in lung, and 7 percent in tracheo-bronchial lymph nodes.

The daily variation in excretion of plutonium 239 was often more than 100 fold. Data showed greater retention of plutonium 239 in dogs than in mice, where the maximum halflife observed for lung clearance was 460 days.

Rapid accumulation of plutonium in tracheobronchial lymph nodes was observed in about 50 dogs sacrificed at intervals after inhalation exposure. By 15 weeks after exposure, the average concentration of plutonium in tracheobronchial lymph nodes exceeded the average concentration of plutonium in lungs by as much as 50 times. as a

Chelating agents. The effectiveness of DTPA (diethylenetriaminepentaacetic acid) chelating agent for removal of plutonium deposited in the body was studied in some detail. 

Of particular interest is its demonstrated effectiveness in preventing the death of rats given normally lethal injections of plutonium. 

DTPA was utilized in cases of accidental human exposure to plutonium with encouraging results at the Hanford laboratories. 

Chemical Toxicity

Rare-metals metabolism. Expansion in industrial availability of rare-earth elements has made their study important.

One line of research is concerned with the largely unknown field of the biochemical behavior of nonradioactive rare earth metals. 

The general reactions of these substances in test animals suggests that many of them yield insoluble products or soluble complexes in plasma and tissues, but efforts to design a simple testtube system for determining the reactivity of a wide range of biochemical compounds has had limited success. Spectrophotometry and polarimetry are presently being used as indices of interactions. 

The effect of rare earths on enzyme systems is of profound significance, but information on specific actions on these biological catalysts is extremely scarce. The anticoagulant properties of certain rare-earth ions has been repeatedly reported, but the mechanism is unknown. 

A preliminary study of the effect of rare earths on hydrolytic enzymes showed that cerium was inhibiting.

In distribution studies of intravenously administered cerium, comparison of the biochemical effects with localization in the liver of various doses of radiocerium indicate no correlation between the actual presence of cerium in the liver and the short-lived acute biochemical disturbances that occur in the liver. Detoxication of the rare metal, i.e., its being rendered chemically nontoxic, seems to take place in the liver. This work covers both acute and chronic toxicological experiments.

The major metabolic effects of rare earths appear to be related to the relationships of the gonadal, adrenal, and pituitary glands. The rare earths tend to localize in the adrenal gland and preferentially in its cortex, but the relative distribution to the gonads and pituitary is quite low. It is therefore difficult to correlate observed metabolic effects with this distribution. 

In studies on the microbiological response to various rare metals, especially the fission products, the uptake of cerium was investigated in bacteria, fungi, and yeasts as typical of the rare-earth component of fission products.

Generally, all organisms showed a prominent concentration of radioactivity associated with their growth phase. Uptake of cerium generally exceeded that of mixed fission productsnot a surprising observation in view of the diverse composition of the fission products. The next phase of study is designed to indicate the nature of this uptake. 

Rare-earth fatty liver. Toxicity reactions of rats to rare earths given intravenously showed, first, acute fatty infiltration of the liver, and, second, reversion to normal in about a week in the rats that survived. This fatty infiltration was more rapid and intense than other known responses of this kind. This system is a potential tool for biochemical study of lipid (fats) metabolism, as well as for defining the effects of other rare earths. Oak Ridge Institute of

Nuclear Studies has completed a study on characterization of this type of fatty liver with respect to various influencing factors, and is undertaking research to define the biochemical mechanism involved.

This rare-earth fatty liver follows toxic doses of all the cerium group of elements (lanthanum, cerium, praseodymium, neodymium, and samarium) but other members of the rareearth group do not produce it, even in greatly increased doses. The response occurs to various degrees in different species. The rat is most responsive and some strains of rats are more responsive than others. Hamsters and mice respond at very high dose levels while chickens, pigs, guinea pigs, rabbits, and dogs show no fatty infiltration. Females of all species and strains show more response than males. The sex difference was confirmed both in castrated animals of both sexes and in those given sex hormone supplements. Adrenalectomy (removal of adrenal glands) will protect the male, whereas hypophysectomy (removal of the pituitary gland) will protect both sexes of strains that usually show acute infiltration.

Indications are that the fatty infiltration is a result of mobilization and redistribution in the body's depots of total fats. Results of enzymatic studies also indicate that oxidative processes are affected.

Studies outlined for further clarification of metabolic effects of certain rare-earth elements include additional enzyme studies, measurements of turnover of specific lipid fractions, and examination of the individual fatty acids and the involvement of specific hormones. 8. 

Uranium. A series of experimental studies at

Oak Ridge National Laboratory on inhalation of uranium oxide (U.Os) will be concluded this year. Dogs, rats, and mice have been exposed to a single inhalation of U3Os. Lung burden data, obtained on mice and dogs, will be compared with human data from whole body counter measurements. The long-term retention of UO, seems to increase in relation to the size of the animal, but this hypothesis requires confirmation by experiment with additional species. These data, along with information obtained from chronic inhalation by mice and dogs, are used by the health physics division of the laboratory to interpret human inhalation cases, and provide a basis for extrapolation of animal data to man.

Niobium. The toxicity of niobium (columbium), a potentially hazardous material which is increasing in use, is being studied at the Uni versity of Rochester-Atomic Energy Project. 

As with studies of other possibly toxic materials, several avenues of investigations have been pursued. First, large doses of niobium were given to laboratory animals in an attempt to define the lethal range. Second, animals were maintained on diets containing the metal to determine what effect continued intake might have. A third experiment delineated the distribution of the material in the body to answer such questions as how long niobium is retained or how quickly excreted.

Single, oral doses of niobium pentoxide (Nb2O5) in aqueous suspension were administered to female rats. Doses of from 0.78 to 2.1 grams per kilograms of body weight were tolerated over રી one-month observation period. This compound, as well as a soluble niobium compound, will be administered by several routes to several other species. 

Four groups of young male rats were maintained on a diet containing no niobium (controls), or 0.01, 0.10, or 1.0 percent niobium chloride for a period of seven weeks. There has been no evidence of growth retardation, nor have any deaths occurred which could be attributed to niobium.

Studies of distribution and excretion of niobium in rats exposed to aerosols of niobium 95, and of niobium 95 plus a carrier, are in progress. 

At 32 days, the lungs of a sacrificed rat accounted for approximately one-half the body burden, and the body burden equaled three percent of what it had been when exposure ceased. 

Animals exposed to niobium plus carrier showed no great difference in distribution-

excretion pattern from those which inhaled the carrier-free aerosol.

Inhaled iridium. A study of iridium 192 has been made at University of Rochester-Atomic

Energy Project. Thirty lightly narcotized female rats were exposed to an airborne suspension of metallic iridium 192 for about 30 minutes. Animals were sacrificed immediately after exposure, and at 3 hours, 1, 3, 6, 9, 13, and 14 days after exposure, and tissues and excreta examined. 

Of the iridium 192 retained, more than 95 percent was in the upper respiratory tract; redistribution to other organs was negligible. 

However, 96 percent was excreted in feces by virtue of the upper respiratory tract being cleared into the gastrointestinal tract. Virtually all the unbound iridium 192 had been cleared from the body 9 to 14 days after exposure.

No significant pathological lesions were observed. Autoradiographic examination is still in progress.

WHOLE BODY COUNTERS 

Investigation of radioelements found normally in people's bodies was begun in 1948 and has resulted in the development at Argonne National Laboratory of radiation detecting and measuring devices which have high sensitivity and ability to differentiate among various gamma-ray emitters with considerable precision. About 30 similar installations are in use or under construction, and at least an equal number of others is planned in the United

States and several foreign countries. 

This device has found many uses in industrial hygiene, radiation safety and medical research. It also has opened up a new field in the use of radioactive tracers in medical diagnosis because, with this device, the quantities formerly considered necessary may be reduced to one-hundredth or one-thousandth the amount formerly used. Most Commission installations have whole body counters, and the Commission has recently supported construction of whole body counters at University of 

Rochester and Vanderbilt University medical centers, the Argonne Cancer Research Hospital and the Brookhaven National Laboratory

Medical Division, and funds have been allocated for counters at the University of Arkansas Medical Center and the Massachusetts

Memorial Hospitals. 

The installations are available to the staff of the medical centers for further development of techniques utilizing low levels of gamma-emitting isotopes in clinical research. Problems connected with liver cirrhosis, heart disease, blood dyscrasias, dietary deficiencies, vitamin metabolism, electrolyte balance, and others are currently under investigation. The utility of the whole body counter has been demonstrated also in industrial and laboratory accidents, both within and outside the Commission. Studies with mock-ups, called "phantoms", help to determine the interpretation of exposures. 

The biomedical program of the Los Alamos Scientific Laboratory also uses a gamma-ray spectrometer to complement the program of biological and environmental studies begun with a large liquid scintillation counter. 

Large liquid scintillation counters appear to have great promise for routine medical applications, as well as for specific problems in which the geometry of radiation and radiation detection is not an important factor. In conjunction with the Office of Naval Research, the Commission is supporting basic work in large liquid scintillation counters at Catholic University.

The use of large counters for low levels of radiation has been extended to studies with large and small animals. The University of 

Utah has employed a counter for measuring the body burdens of radioisotopes in dogs currently under observation to determine long-term effects of several important radioisotopes. Extension of these techniques to the study of growth and maturation in farm animals will be pursued by laboratories at Cornell and Pur-

88418 Whole-body Counter. Through use of a super-imposed cutout, this photo shows the exterior and interior of the 

Argonne National Laboratory's whole-body counter. The counter room is constructed of lead and steel to exclude any background radioactivity so that the scanning head (circular object above model) can accurately determine the radioisotopic content of the subject's body. The term "whole body" is used in the sense that the device can differentiate among the whole spectrum (body) of gamma-ray emitting radioisotopes. due Universities, and at the University of Tennessee.

Among the latest improvements in whole body counters has been the marked increase in abilities of large single crystals to resolve the different energies of radiation. A body content of 100 microcuries of cobalt 60 is quite readily identified and precisely quantitated by this device in the presence of the greater radioactive levels of potassium 40 and cesium 137 in the body. This is possible despite the similarity of energy of the emitted gamma rays. 

This type of analysis would have been impossible with an instrument that gave less resolution of the energy levels of various gamma rays.

Diagnostic Applications 

The whole body counter has proved adaptable to clinical diagnosis and research. It is very useful wherever measurement of retention of a radioactive tracer in the whole body can be substituted for laboratory examination of levels in blood and urinary or fecal excretion. 

Using very small doses of radioactivity, it permits more frequent repetition of the labeling procedure. The counter is easily operated by a technician and requires less of a patient's and doctor's time. Patients also seem to prefer it 357 흐 ​4아 ​Counts per Minute per 13.7 30 20 10 CEM-4 100 200

Technique Comparison. The above two graphs, based on work done at Argonne National Laboratory, show the obvious advantages of the coincidence summing technique over the conventional single-crystal technique in determining the body contents of radionuclides despite the radioactivity interference of normal potassium. TIME (DAYS) Clinical Diagnosis. The versatility of the whole-body counter is such that it can be used in the place of chemical analyses of blood samples for clinical diagnosis. The chart above, made at the Los Alamos 

Scientific Laboratory, shows the uptake and retention of iodine 131 (I 131) by a hypothyroid patient before and after the administration of a thyroid-stimuluating hormone (TSH). The measurement required no blood samples and was done in a fraction of the time needed for a chemical analysis. was directly related to the physiologic iron demand of the individual. Other anemias, not associated with iron deficiency, have shown no increased uptake of iron 59. The counter, therefore, provides a simple way of diagnosing primary iron deficiency anemias as opposed to those of secondary origin. It is necessary to make only two measurements of 100 seconds each—one immediately upon ingestion of the iron 59, and one 5 to 10 days later. 

Whole-body counting of gamma radiation from potassium 40 naturally present in body potassium has proved to be a reliable method of determining the total amount of body potassium. Since body potassium is almost entirely concentrated in the lean body mass, measurement of potassium 40 is a good reciprocal index to total body fat-a quantity of considerable interest to physiologists.

The total body counter at the University of 

California at Los Angeles is presently being used to measure the day-to-day retention of bone-seeking short-lived strontium 85 in patients who are suffering from various diseases

Studies have been continued on the use of bone-marrow to support the blood-forming system in recovering its function after radiation damage. Other studies have been aimed at the use of certain chemical compounds which remove toxic materials deposited in the body, and of other compounds which, in animals, afford some protection against high levels of radiation.

Clinical Experience and Studies 

Y-12 plant accident patients. Men who were exposed to significant amounts of whole body radiation in a Y-12 plant accident at Oak Ridge on June 16, 1958,¹ have received periodic medical examinations at the Oak Ridge Institute of 

Nuclear Studies. Blood counts and bone-mar row appear normal and indicate recovery from the depression of bone-marrow present earlier.

These men will continue under careful medical observation.

Biochemical effects. Studies of the effect which irradiation has on excretion of amino acids in urine of persons accidentally exposed to radiation have been pursued at the Medical Division of the Oak Ridge Institute of Nuclear Studies, with two primary objectives:

The first aim is to determine whether the loss of amino acids, or of a single amino acid, might be related to the precise amount of damage caused to the individual, and thus possibly be used to estimate the extent of damage, or exposure, in a patient who has received an unknown amount of irradiation. This would be helpful in treating whole body radiation accident cases, and might be useful in estimating the effect of intentionally applied therapeutic irradiation.

The second objective is to determine whether patterns in excretion will suggest pathways in human metabolism that are particularly sensi- 

1 See pp. 213-214, Twenty-fourth Semiannuel Report to 

Congress (January-June 1958), and pp. 190-191, Annual

Report to Congress (January-December 1959). 

tive to radiation. Thus, it might be that the deleterious effects of irradiation could, in part, be prevented, or recovery could be accelerated, by supplying the patient with the products of the metabolic sequence that is damaged. 

These objectives have been partially realized. 

However, much more study is necessary to characterize the identified changes in terms of altered human metabolism, and to relate these changes specifically to the amount of damage caused by a given dose of radiation to normal humans, and to patients suffering from disease. 

The results apply to excretion in urine of several nitrogen-containing compounds: creatine, and four amino acids, taurine, serine, cystine, and beta-aminoisobutyric acid. All are found in urine of normal persons. Serine and cystine are constituents of normal cell proteins, and serine also enters into many reactions within the cells which lead to formation of other important cell-constituents. Cystine (containing sulfur in addition to nitrogen) enters, also, into other cellular reactions. 

Taurine, a highly acidic amino acid containing sulfur is excreted in large amounts relative to other amino acids, and is present in high concentration in cells. Despite this high concentration of taurine in the nonprotein-nitrogen of cells, blood, and urine, little is known about its function except that it is used by the liver in the formation of a bile salt. Beta-aminoisobutyric acid is an unusual amino acid that may arise in metabolism, chiefly from a constitutent of cell nuclei. Creatine is an amino acid derivative manufactured by the liver and used, with phosphate, by muscle (and other cells) in storing energy. 

Excretion of taurine and beta-aminoisobutyric acid was increased in the urine of the five men exposed to radiation in an accident at the Oak Ridge Y-12 plant in June 1958.2 The excretion of serine was decreased or abolished while that of cystine was somewhat elevated. 

These were normal men exposed to a complex of gamma rays and neutrons. The effects were

Automatic Analyzer.

A technician at the Oak Ridge Institute of Nuclear Studies places a urine sample in the automatic amino acid analyzer used in studying the biochemical effects of radiation on humans. The analyzer can determine the amounts of amino acids present in a sample of urine in a fraction of the time taken by former laboratory methods. not noted in three men exposed to 86 rads or less of radiation, whereas all five exposed to more than 200 rads showed the increase in excretion of taurine and beta-aminoisobutyric acid and the decrease or absence of serine. The cystine effect was less consistent. The increase in beta-aminoisobutyric acid seemed to be a better indication of dose of irradiation than is increase in taurine excretion. The limits of this correlation between increased levels of excretion and dose of irradiation need to be worked out in future research, as does the mechanism by which irradiation brought about excretion of these compounds. 

Increased excretion of taurine and betaaminoisobutyric acid also has been found to occur in leukemia patients treated with 935 r of cobalt 60 irradiation, or in patients with cancer of the lung given a large dose of nitrogen mustard.

In a man accidentally exposed to a lethal dose of radiation at Los Alamos 3, the excretion

Sample Results. The wavy lines on the chromatogram coming from the automatic analyzer show abnormal amounts of taurine and beta-aminoisobutyric acids in the urine of a person accidentally exposed to radiation in an incident at Oak Ridge National Laboratory. 

Studies have shown that there may be a correlation between the amount of radiation received by a person and the amount of amino acids subsequently excreted, a fact that might be useful in determining treatment of patients exposed to unknown amounts of radiation. of amino acids was very abnormal. Most were excreted at very low levels, but more than 200 milligrams of taurine and 20 milligrams of cystine were excreted in 22 hours. Since the patient was in cardiovascular-renal shock during the entire period, the kidneys were not excreting nitrogenous waste products at a normal rate; hence it cannot be certain that all taurine or cystine formed was excreted. The extremely abnormal distribution of urinary amino acids could have been due to kidney failure.

In connection with all these studies, it was important to have detailed information about the effect of changes in diet on amino acid excretion since food is a source of the amino acids. 

When two normal men were put on controlled dietary programs, urine specimens showed that excretion of several amino acids, including taurine, was affected by dietary changes. 

Excretion of taurine was considerably increased when the diet contained only 180 grams of protein a day. This can explain differences in taurine excretion levels among people from different geographical and economic groups. 

Thus, changes in food intake and type of diet must be considered in interpreting data on excretion of amino acids by diseased or irradiated people.

The excretion of creatine was not affected by the irradiation doses sustained by the men of the Y-12 accident. However, there was an increase in creatine excretion in one patient who had leukemia and was treated with 935 r irradiation. High excretion continued for 6 days, after which creatine was excreted at lower levels than before irradiation. In another patient, with widespread malignancy, creatine suddenly appeared in the urine in large quantities-presumably due to progression of the malignant disease. When this patient was given total-body irradiation as part of treatment during this period of elevated creatine excretion, irradiation had no observable effect on creatine excretion. Seven days after the treatment, creatine excretion had dropped. 

These observations seem to indicate that irradiation does not affect creatine excretion in humans, even when creatine metabolism is abnormal, unless the exposure is to a very high dose (as in the patient given 935 r) or unless the patient has certain kinds of disease. 

In summary, it appears tentatively that persons who had received lethal irradiation could be identified on the basis of elevated excretion of three compounds: taurine, betaaminoisobutyric acid, and creatine. The first two would be elevated at doses similar to those involved in the Y-12 accident, whereas all three compounds would be excreted at increased levels when the exposure doses approached 1,000 r.

STANDARD STANDARD 

Creatine Excretion. Chromatogram shows significant above-normal amounts of creatine and creatinine in the urine of a leukemia patient who was given radiation therapy at the Oak Ridge Institute of Nuclear Studies. 

The radiation treatment increased the amount of the nitrogen-containing compound excreted for several days. 

Urine samples of healthy persons accidentally exposed to radiation have shown no increase in the normal creatine excretion rate.

Therapy for Acute Radiation Syndrome 

Several hundred rads of total body irradiation, the amount varying with the species, kills mammals by (a) damage to the gastrointestinal tract that results in loss of water and minerals, and (b) damage to the blood-cell forming systems in the bone-marrow that results in hemorrhage and infection. Therapy for this acute radiation syndrome has been under investigation at a number of Commission laboratories for many years. Recent studies at Brookhaven

National Labratory have shown that 9 out of 

10 dogs exposed to 400 r of X-rays died within 

30 days after exposure if not given therapy, while 8 out of 10 dogs similarly exposed survived when given therapy.

The therapy used consisted of transfusing whole blood to the dogs to control bleeding, administering antibiotics to control infection, and supplying fluids to combat dehydration. Each treatment was withheld until it was clinically indicated in order to avoid premature transfusion reactions and resistance to antibiotic therapy. This rather simple therapy, with no resort to bone-marrow transplants, reduced fatalities from a normal 90 percent to only 20 percent. The treatment used was directed toward

Quick Freezing. The use of a liquid nitrogen centrifuge has enabled scientists at the Oak Ridge National 

Laboratory to preserve animal bone marrow by quick freezing. In above photo, isolated cell components are being injected into the centrifuge through its plastic cover. Studies have shown that homologous bone marrow injections help reduce severe irradiation effects in animals. Such work might lead eventually to stockpiling bone marrow for human irradiation patients. ameliorating symptoms, and was not specific therapy to the radiation injury itself. 

Transplantation and Immune Response 

Protection of immune response. Ten years ago, no treatment for irradiation damage was known. Within recent years, however, a series of experiments have shown that the lives of lethally-irradiated animals may be saved by the injection of cells of blood-forming tissues. 

These experimental approaches began with work at the University of Chicago which showed that mice could withstand a dose of Xrays that otherwise would be lethal if the spleen was protected from radiation by a lead shield. The spleen contains several types of blood-forming cells and, when protected, it is able to seed off into the circulation system primitive cells that are capable of giving rise to red blood cells, and the white blood cells, granulocytes, and lymphocytes. Later, bone-marrow transplants were attempted.

Studies by several groups have shown that considerable protection against the after-effects of irradiation is given to a lethally irradiated animal by the injection of bone-marrow which it can tolerate. These studies are important in treating cases of radiation accidents. The preservation and stockpiling of bone-marrow cells from human donors to be used in event of a radiation incident would be an ultimate development of this line of research. Successful results have been obtained so far in preservation of animal bone-marrows by using liquid nitrogen to accomplish quick freezing. 

The administration of donor bone-marrow is actually a problem in tissue transplantation, related, for example, to skin grafts, and carries with it many inherent difficulties, particularly in the immunological sense since the body builds up antibodies against alien tissues with serious effects on the system. The white blood cells usually present in bone-marrow may present a very serious immunological problem, particularly in man.

Lymphoid tissue has become more and more implicated in the immune reactions that follow transplants of blood-forming cells used as a means of repopulating the marrow following lethal irradiation. Important information about the immune reaction may be gained from study of the ability of pure lymphoid tissue to colonize in other parts of the body, from observing the effects of this activity upon recovery of the immunizing mechanism which typically is lost after irradiation, and from determining the minimal number of cells capable of restoring the immune action.

The group at the Argonne Cancer Research Hospital has conducted experiments in which, instead of protecting the spleen, a single small lymphoid nodule in the intestine of mice, called a Peyer's patch, was shielded while the remainder of the body was irradiated. A spectacularly rapid repopulation with white blood cells of the lymph nodes, spleen, and thymus occurred in the mice after radiation destroyed the original cells, but no red blood cell formation was observed. 573517 0-61-4 It is well known as a result of experiments that massive irradiation destroys the ability of the immunizing mechanism of the mice to react against foreign proteins such as rat bonemarrow. Thus, the rat cells are able to colonize the body of the mouse and save its life from acute radiation death. When a single Peyer's patch containing about five million lymphoid cells was protected during the irradiation of the mouse and rat cells were subsequently injected, the mouse's immunizing mechanism rejected the grafted rat cells and death ensued. 

Similarly, when bone-marrow cells from mice of a different strain were use, the cells derived from the protected Peyer's patch rejected these cells also.

This technique also has been used to study tumor rejection. Cancers in mice may be transplanted to other mice of the same inbred strain, but will not live in mice with a different genetic composition unless the host mice have been weakened by radiation so as to destroy their immune defenses. When a single Peyer's patch is shielded during irradiation, the transplanted tumor is rejected, and the mice live. 

The use of this tiny organ with its anatomically distinct lymphatic aggregate offers a unique system to study behavior of the immune mechanisms of irradiated animals. Subsequent experiments will elucidate the factors affecting growth, differentiation, and migration of cells of lymphatic origin. 

Limited studies were performed at Hanford Laboratories on testing the efficacy of transplanting fetal blood-forming tissue (liver and spleen) into lethally irradiated swine in order to prevent death. So far one pig has survived and is doing well 6 months after exposure to a normally lethal dose of 900 r. 

Studies on transplantation immunology. Every day the clinician encounters patients, both young and old, who are generally healthy, but are doomed as a consequence of disease in a particular organ or organ system. If that organ or organ system could be replaced with healthy tissue, life could be prolonged.

Tumor Transplantation. At Argonne Cancer Research Hospital, studies have been made on the effects of transplanting tumorous and normal cell tissue from mice into young rats. In some cases, the young rat develops the features of "runt disease" and does not live long, others show no effect and live normally. The above photo shows two 14-day-old rats from the same litter. The one on the left is growing normally, the one on the right is a "runt" and died two days later.

To this end, a vast amount of research has been undertaken to determine whether any specialized circumstances will make possible transplantation of tissues. An animal saved from lethal irradiation by the infusion of bonemarrow from a genetically unrelated donor then will accept skin and other grafts from this donor strain. If an animal is injected in its early life with cells from a foreign donor, various tissues of that donor may be grafted onto this host far into adult life. If the circulatory systems of two animals are surgically joined for a sufficiently long period of time (a technique known as parabiosis), they will accept grafts from each other following their separation. 

These and other experimental approaches have succeeded in breaking the naturally existing, genetically determined immunological barriers, and have contributed to a new medical discipline coming to be known as transplantation immunology.

This initial success has been dampened, however, by the fact that the host animal frequently deteriorates after being supplied with genetically foreign bone-marrow and often dies within a few weeks or months. This phenomenon of the wasting that follows apparent successful transplantation is variously called secondary disease, parabiotic intoxication, or runt disease.

Scientists at Argonne Cancer Research Hos pital have inoculated both human and mouse tissues into newborn rats. These tissues have been both normal and cancerous in nature. 

Whereas none of these genetically foreign tis-

sues will grow in the adult rat, four mouse tumors have taken in infant rats and grown progressively and killed the hosts. Here again the concept of successful transplantation to an immunologically immature host is borne out. 

Of interest is the fact that three of these tumors belong to the lymphoma category, a group which heretofore was generally resistant to serial transplantation between such genetically foreign species. These findings merit further attempts to transplant human lymphomas into the newborn rat.

Means of excluding immunological reactions that occur as a consequence of successful transplantation of foreign material must be found before large-scale clinical experimentation can be undertaken.

Studies on formation of antibodies. The antibodies which form in response to infections of various kinds are the body's natural protection against disease. It is by stimulating the formation of antibodies with immunizing injections that people are provided with protection against smallpox, poliomyelitis, typhoid, typhus, tetanus, and other diseases. As important as the antibodies are to survival, scientists still do not understand very well how they are formed by the cells of the body. 

Research is carried out by using antigensmaterials which, when injected, cause antibodies to be formed—and labeling the antigens with radioactive tracers. Radiation-tagged antigens can be followed through the body of experimental animals, and localized in tissues and cells, to determine how the body handles them. 

Several experimental methods interfere with animals' ability to manufacture antibodies, among them total-body X-ray irradiation and feeding a diet deficient in proteins. Under many conditions, the treatments do not alter the way in which the animal handles the antigens, but instead, stops the multiplication of cells in several organs. Apparently these cells must divide and undergo other changes to form antibodies. It is this process that is disturbed. 

Various experimental animals differ in their ability to make antibodies. The white rat produces a large amount of antibody if the antigen is in the form of particles such as bacteria. If the antigen is in a solution such as serum proteins or egg white, the rat forms very little, if any, antibody. In the rabbit, the form in which the antigen is administered makes very little difference in the quantity of antibody manufactured. The Argonne Cancer Research 

Hospital is analyzing how the rat and rabbit handle a variety of materials so as to determine the pattern of cellular changes in the tissues that form antibodies.

The investigations may help in understanding how antibodies are formed in people, and thus point a way toward better protection against diseases. Antibodies have been of help in treating some kinds of cancers in mice. Rabbits vaccinated with a mouse tumor form antibodies against that tumor in much the same way that horses form antibodies against the tetanus toxin.

When mice are treated with this rabbit-produced antiserum before the tumor is implanted the tumor does not take. Sometimes mouse tumor can be cured if the antibodies are given after the tumor has begun to grow, but it is difficult for antibodies to enter a large tumor mass. Other forms of treatment are being used to help antibodies enter the tumor. For example, some kinds of viruses are able to grow inside tumor cells and interfere and injure them. 

By combining such viruses with antibody injections, decrease in tumor size and complete tumor regression has been observed.

Chemical Protection 

Microbe guiding studies. Microorganisms continue to be among the best biological materials for studying basic phenomena. Detailed mechanisms of metabolic reactions and the efficacy of chemical protective agents can be studied with relative ease with microorganisms. These inexpensive experiments often lead to suggestions for experiments with mammals.

At Oak Ridge National Laboratory, the type of metabolism that occurs during the growth of the bacterium, E. coli, before irradiation has been found to have a remarkable influence on the radiosensitivity of its cells. The resistance may be caused by a metabolically produced protective or recovery agent.

The mold, Aspergillus terreus, continues to be one of the ideal organisms for making a quick assay of mutation production. Tests showed that AET bromide (S, 2-aminoethylisothiuronium.Br), a substance used experimentally to protect against radiation effects, is slightly mutagenic at high concentrations and also is somewhat toxic. AET hydrochloride protects as well against X-rays as does AET hydrobromide, but it does not increase the frequency of mutation. This may indicate that bromides themselves have this effect. In following up this idea, investigations are in progress using 

Drosophila melanogaster (fruit flies) which indicate that bromide water fed to fruit flies will increase the mutation rate produced by X-rays.

Neutron irradiation safeguards. A number of chemical compounds have been shown to protect animals against the effects of irradiation when administered immediately before radia tion exposure. Work at Argonne National 

Laboratory established that by the activity of one such compound, cysteine, the prophylactic effect was conditional on the type of radiation as it was much less effective against neutrons than against X-rays and gamma rays. 

Two recently developed agents which are effective against X-rays and gamma radiation, AET and serotonin (5-hydroxytryptamine), have also been tested for their effectiveness against cobalt 60 gamma rays compared with fast neutrons. AET orally, or serotonin by intraperitoneal injection, was given immediately before mice were exposed to lethal doses of gamma rays or fission neutrons.

Both agents were effective against cobalt 60 gamma rays. A dose of radiation sufficient to kill all the mice within 10 days was reduced in effectiveness by 17 to 20 percent. Both chemicals were much less effective against fission neutrons. A single dose of 350 rads of neutrons was reduced by only 5 to 7 percent in effectiveness, based on deaths occurring in 30 days. Neither compound proved effective against the early death from intestinal injury that results from heavy neutron irradiation.

The results add to the evidence that the protection afforded by chemical compounds of this type, like the protection afforded by oxygen deficiency, is related inversely to the density of ionization produced by the radiation. The protection they afford is assumed to be related in some way to the linear space in which the energy of the radiation is transferred and dissipated in living systems. Further study is needed, not only on protection, but also on the unsolved problems of the mechanisms of radiation responses. It is not certain that a comparison should be made in instances such as this on the computed physical energy equivalence. 

'Scientists at Oak Ridge National Laboratory further developed the theory believed to account for this protection by AET. They found that this particular chemical grouping is an excellent "radical scavenger"-that is, it captures and neutralizes the free chemical radicals that radiation creates in body substances. Conventionally, it is believed that free radicals are highly reactive, and can give rise to harmful combinations and chemicals.

In addition, Oak Ridge has carried out a large number of studies on the action of AET in enzyme systems, and in various mammalian tissues, and have discovered various metabolic pathways by which AET is metabolized and excreted.

Treatment of mice with a combination of AET plus 5-HT (5-hydoxytryptamine) has been carried out at the University of California at Los Angeles. Given 20-30 minutes before administration of 460 r whole body X-irradiation at a dose rate of 19.4 r per minute the treatment resulted in survival of 50 percent of the mice for the entire 60-day observation period.

Similarly treated mice which received a whole body dose of 1200 r showed almost the same

survival curves as untreated mice receiving only 460 r.

These investigations were extended to include studies on the effects of increasing the time between treatment and irradiation as well as on decreasing the rate at which the irradiation dose was administered. Results indicate that a long interval between treatment and start of the X-ray exposure markedly decreases, even abolishes, the protective action of the AET and 5-HT combination. This is to be expected since both AET and 5-HT are rapidly metabolized. This same aspect of the radiation protective problem, the short persistence of these chemicals in the body, was evident when the lowest rate (1.00 r a minute) of radiation administration was tested: Little or no evidence of radiation protection was found.

Biological Research EFFECTS ON BIOLOGICAL SYSTEMS 

Biological Research at Commission laboratories, directed basically toward learning more about the way in which radiation damages living material, included during 1960 a number of studies on the effects of radiation on the genetic structure of cells, on cells themselves, and on organ systems. To determine the effects of radiation on the building blocks of the cell, increasing emphasis was placed on biochemical and molecular studies.

Tracing Radiation Damage 

Injury and recovery processes. Lethal radiation injury is a highly complex process which is assumed to include both "direct" radiation damage to cells, and "indirect" injury which takes effect through various physiological processes. Accumulation of both types of damage is modified by such physical factors as type of radiation, total exposure, and rate of exposure, as well as by varying biological factors specifically related to individuals and species. It is possible to predict some kinds of injury that will result from a given exposure and the general probability of death, but little is known of the mechanisms leading to the form the injury takes. Direct study of the mechanism of injury is difficult because the normal interdependency of various physiological processes is not fully understood. It has become apparent, however, that many processes of damage are active both during and after irradiation, and that, at the same time, other processes are active that counteract radiation effects and repair damage.

In a long-term study at Argonne National Laboratory, pathological changes have been correlated with statistical analysis of mortality data to yield information on these problems. 48 Even when study is restricted to acutely lethal exposures, it is difficult to separate and characterize the different intimate causes of the cellular destruction that results. Distinguishable indicators of damage (for instance, intestinal or bone-marrow injury) are few, and different signs of injury overlap in time. Fowl have been used in these particular studies because it is possible by means of appropriate experimental manipulations to demonstrate separable injury mechanisms more conveniently in this species than in others. 

In young chickens and chick embryos the acute mortality resulting from a single exposure to cobalt 60 gamma rays was found to depend both on total radiation exposure and on rate of exposure. The dependency of mortality rate on total exposure suggests an irreversible injury process. On the other hand, the fact that rate of exposure also was a factor suggests that a reversible process is operating at the same time. The two terms "reversible injury" and "irreversible injury" here refer to the presence or absence of repair processes that take place during and after the time of irradiation. A statistical analysis of the relation between total exposure and exposure rate combined with study of the predominant pathological changes, has led to several general conclusions. 

It was possible to divide the response of fowl to acute radiation exposure into three mortality periods that are almost completely separable and in which it is assumed that different injury mechanisms are operating.

In the first mortality period, the pattern of injury indicates a reversible effect that may, however, culminate in death within the first one or two days after exposure. The injury is produced at a rate proportional to rate of exposure and is reversed at a constant rate characteristic of each laboratory specimen. Mortality is associated with a circulatory failure probably

related to changes in cell permeability. These changes may be general, but occur especially in the lining of the finer blood vessels. Similar effects, as well as swelling of cells, are common, early changes in most animals after irradiation, but are not fatal. (The 10 to 90 percent lethal dose range for this effect, for a dose delivered nearly instantaneously, is about 750 to 1,150 roentgens (r) in both young chicks and chick embroys, and the LD50-dose killing half of the animals-is 900 to 1,000 r.)

In the second period, in which death occurs 5 to 6 days after exposure, a reversible process exists for shorter periods of exposure, but after 8 hours or more of continuous exposure the process is no longer reversible. Mortality is associated with local accumulations of fluid, especially in the embryo, but other injury processes also may be involved. (The 10 to 90 percent lethal range for the irreversible effect is 

1,700 to 3,500 r for the chick and 1,700 to 2,500 r for the embryo, with LD50s of 2,600 r and 2,100 r, respectively.)

In the third mortality period, 6 or 7 days following exposure, injury is essentially irreversible and thus depends principally on total dose. 

Mortality is associated with evidences of injury to the liver and failure of the blood-forming system. (The 10 to 90 percent lethal range for this period is 1,200 to 2,200 r for the chick and 

900 to 1,500 r for the embryo, with LD50s of 

1,600 r and 1,200 r, respectively.)

Further investigation of early radiation effects those associated with the earliest mortality of the chick—were designed to determine the behavior of the reversible processes after exposure ceased. Divided radiation exposures were delivered at a high rate separated by intervals ranging from 20 minutes to 24 hours. 

The first dose was 700 r, enough to produce 5 to 10 percent mortality. The second dose delivered after varying intervals was designed to test the sensitivity of the lethal response as a function of the length of the interval. With intervals exceeding 2 hours, between exposures, it was possible to detect a state of hyper-resistance to radiation exposure that reached a maximum at 4 to 6 hours and almost disappears after 24 hours. During the time of maximum resistance, mortality did not exceed 50 percent even after exposure to a second supralethal dose of 6,000 r. After 24 hours, however, the sensitivity of the previously irradiated chicks is similar to that of chicks not previously irradiated. 

Additional divided exposure experiments with both low and high dose rates established that resistance develops during irradiation as well as after exposure. This process requires about 6 hours to develop but is not lost during further continuous exposure to a total of 24 hours.

It is concluded from these experiments that, for effects leading to early deaths, a reversal or recovery process is present that operates at least during irradiation. In addition, a second phenomenon is present that renders the chick temporarily hyper-resistant to a second exposure. Thus it is shown that processes initiated by irradiation do not always involve simple repair alone but sometimes may result, at least temporarily in a new or different level of sensitivity.

Cell renewal systems. Normal function in the adult animal is dependent on a continuous orderly renewal of many tissues, for example the blood cells and the lining of the intestine. 

This involves the production of hundreds of billions of new cells each day. Since adults of most species show little, if any, net increase in cell mass, the production of new cells must be balanced by equivalent cell destruction, and this implies the presence of efficient physiological control mechanisms. Maintenance of the balance of cell production and destruction is of vital importance in the body economy, and many acute and chronic radiation effects appear mainly to reflect imbalances of these relationships. Radiobiologists have been aware of this for many years, but since there is only fragmentary understanding of how cell renewal is controlled under normal conditions, it has been

impossible to determine the precise means by which replacement is altered by irradiation. 

Considerable progress has been achieved recently in the techniques of studying cell renewal by labeling cells with tritium (radioactive hydrogen 3). When tritiated thymidine is administered to animals, it is selectively incorporated into cells that are actively synthesizing deoxyribose nucleic acid (DNA) in preparation for cell division, the process by which cells are renewed. Once incorporated, tritiated thymidine appears to be a permanent part of the cell's genetic material; dilution takes place only by further cell division, and loss only by cell death or migration. The labeled thymidine is available to the cells only for a short time, with the result that a single injection labels only a single generation of cells which are at a certain phase of reproduction. These cells then can be followed quantitatively by autoradiography (exposure of samples to photographic film) through subsequent stages of cell division and maturation.

The white blood cells known as neutrophils constitute one of these renewal systems. Since many morbid effects of irradiation are associated with derangement of the neutrophil system, there are interesting theoretical and practical problems to be solved in connection with how this derangement contributes to injury and mortality and how this bears on treatment of whole body radiation injury. At Argonne National Laboratory, the tritium-labeling technique was applied to the dynamics of neutrophil population in normal and irradiated dogs. It was established that, in normal dogs, precursors of neutrophils in bone-marrow divide every 10 to 20 hours, depending on their degree of maturity. When they are no longer capable of division, the maturing cells spend about 3 days in the marrow before being released to the blood. After that, their disappearance from the blood is rapid and apparently represents a random process with a mean time of 8 hours or less.

Radiation effects on these processes were evaluated in dogs injected with tritiated thymidine at specified times relative to lethal radiation exposures. When irradiation was given some time after injection, it was possible to follow the fate of a group of labeled neutrophils under conditions in which further production or new cells was greatly curtailed. The results suggest that, at least during the first few days after lethal irradiation, continued maturation of neutrophils that are no longer capable of division, remains essentially unaltered. Other evidence indicates that the mean life of neutrophils in blood is not altered by lethal levels of irradiation.

These results can be correlated with the sequence of events in blood following irradiation. In the irradiated dog, neutrophils show a transient increase during the first few hours, and then a progressive decrease, followed during the second week either by a leveling off of decrease or by a slight rise, followed finally by a further sharp decline or eventual recovery. The early transient increase (neutrophilia) is not due to accelerated release from bone-marrow, but probably represents a mobilization of neutrophils from readily available reservoirs in the circulatory system.

Even when production of new cells in the bone-marrow is greatly curtailed, the initial rate of disappearance of neutrophils from the blood is considerably slower than that calculated for normal animals. This is understandable on the basis that a 3-day supply of neutrophils exists regularly in marrow and that their development to maturity is unaffected by moderate doses of radiation. Several lines of evidence from this and other laboratories indicate that irradiation does not prevent the marrow from releasing these cells, even though their production has been stopped by irradiation. 

When marrow reserves are exhausted, the rate of disappearance of neutrophil cells approaches the normal rate of loss.

These investigations have led to a considerably clearer picture of the normal processes of neutrophil production, release, and loss, and of the events leading to white blood cell changes following irradiation. In general, the results

​R reinforce the conclusion that the fundamental changes are centered in the blood cell factory; irradiation does not materially influence the subsequent fate of developing neutrophils either in the marrow reservoir or in the blood.

Similarities between irradiated animals and normal aged animals have led to a tendency to think of delayed radiation effects as an accel eration of the physiologic aging process. How ever, the relationship between radiation effects and aging remains in doubt, since the two processes differ in certain respects. At Argonne 

National Laboratory, experiments with mice were undertaken to evaluate the influence of age on radiation effects in two tissues, one in the intestine, and one in the liver. Intestinal epithelium is characterized by a high rate of cell renewal under normal physiological conditions; liver tissue by a relatively low rate. 

The intestinal epithelium consists of a continuous layer of cells that covers each minute finger-like villus of the intestinal lining, as well as the crypt at the base of the villus. These cells arise by division in the crypts, pass to the base of the villus and travel upward to the tip, from which they are finally extruded into the intestine. The integrity of the cell covering of the villus is dependent on a continuing supply of new cells and is readily damaged by irradiation; in fact, intestinal mucosal injury is a major component of acute radiation sickness. 

During chronic cobalt 60 irradiation of mice at moderately low doses (12 or 24 r a day) the proportions of cells in division gradually decrease, and the numbers of degenerating cell nuclei in the rapidly dividing cells of the crypts increase. At 220 r a day, the animals die in about 17 days. Up to 17 days, the proportion of cells in division does not appear to change significantly, but the number of degenerating nuclei is greatly increased. 

To evaluate the effects of age on the normal processes of cell renewal, the intestinal epithelium of young, middle-aged and aged mice was observed, using autoradiography at different closely spaced time intervals after the injection of tritiated thymidine. Although tritiated thymidine itself, in the doses injected, produced some cellular changes, it did not contribute to the effects of age. Labeled cells were spread throughout the lower three-fourths of the intestinal crypts within two hours after injection.

Advancing age was associated with increases in the time required for cells to reach the base of the villus and in the time required to pass from the base to the tip, both related to a slower rate of progression. The slower emergence of cells from the crypts indicates a decrease in the rate of cell production, but there was no decrease either in the total population of cells in the crypts or in the number of cells in division.

This indicates that, in older animals, a cell must take longer to complete division-a conclusion supported by the slower rate of appearance and disappearance of labeled nuclei with advancing age.

After comparable irradiation, the intestinal epithelium is denuded somewhat earlier in older animals than it is in young adults. This may be attributable both to the decreased rate of cell production and to the increased transit time. If the radiation dose is sufficient to inhibit cell division, the net effect of radiation on the production rate may well be augmented by such age effects as were demonstrated. Also, the cells in older animals have been longer in transit when they reach the tip of the villus. 

Previous experiments have shown that the maximum life of these cells is about 120 hours, and that when they die they no longer can retain their position at the tip of the villus. 

Thus the cells of older animals have a shorter time to live after they reach the extrusion zone, and they are extruded sooner. 

The study of liver tissue, normally characterized by a low rate of cell division, showed that low-dose rate irradiation caused very little histologically detectable change, although many cells can survive for long periods and accumulate large radiation doses. Nevertheless, it seems improbable that any group of cells could live through extended exposures without being affected to some degree. A more dynamic ex-

perimental approach was used to interpret the observed damage.

In spite of their low rate of renewal, the liver cells retain their capacity to synthesize deoxyribose nucleic acid (DNA) and to divide. 

When a substantial portion of the liver is removed surgically, regeneration begins within a few hours in normal animals. The effects of age and irradiation (12 r a day for 350 days) were evaluated in terms of rates of cell division and DNA synthesis, relative amounts of DNA per cell nucleus, and extent of chromosome damage. 

Irradiation delayed the time of onset of DNA synthesis but increasing age did not, as determined by the rate at which cells took up tritiated thymidine. The number of DNA-synthesizing cells was decreased, and they appeared later in both irradiated and in aged animals than was the case in unirradiated young adults. The rate of division was markedly decreased by irradiation.

When all results are considered, it is clear that, although irradiated liver cells can synthesize DNA, the processes of division are not easily carried through to completion. The decreasing ability of liver cells to divide in unirradiated but aged mice may not be attributable to chromosome damage but to some other type of mechanism within the cell. 

Leukemia Incidence in Mice

One major problem in the evaluation of radiation hazards is the relationship between radiation exposure and occurrence of leukemia (a type of cancer of the blood). Investigations of conditions that give rise to leukemia can contribute to understanding the process. The incidence of leukemia and other cancers in mice is known to be affected to an important extent by the hormone levels and metabolic activity of the animal. If incidence is governed solely by such physiological conditions, normal environmental exposure to low levels of radiation might be assumed to be without effect since the relevant physiological conditions are not detectably affected by radiation. On the other hand, if somatic (body cell) mutations are necessary preconditions for occurrence of cancer, and if production of such mutations is governed by the same laws controlling genetic (sex cell) mutations, some increase in incidence of leukemia might occur even at doses too low to alter physiological conditions. Under this hypothesis, the incidence of leukemia would follow the exposure pattern presumed to cause genetic mutation. The central problem, then, is to determine whether somatic mutations are necessary preconditions to incidence of leukemia. Findings on this point are complicated by the fact that the amount of irradiation required in genetic work by experimental limitations also will disturb relevant physiological conditions.

Some light has been shed on this problem by an integrative study of two sets of data obtained in a program on X-ray and gamma ray toxicity at Argonne National Laboratory. Groups of mice (LAF, strain) of both sexes were exposed daily to cobalt 60 gamma rays, beginning at 

100 days of age, at seven dosage levels ranging from 6 to 64 r a day. The mice were exposed continually until death occurred, and then were examined to determine their pathological state. 

The leukemia studied was thymic lymphoma, the one most frequently induced in the mouse by radiation.

The incidence of leukemia in the mice grouped according to exposure, and in terms of the percentage of animals that came to autopsy, was markedly greater at 6 r per day than for the control group of unirradiated mice; the incidence increased with daily dose up to 32 r per day. Thereafter it decreased progressively to zero at 64 r per day because at these exposures, it appears the mice died before leukemia could be manifested. Females exhibited considerably greater radiosensitivity to leukemia, a phenomenon known to be related to differences in endocrine function.

Leukemia incidence rates were calculated according to age, and were always an increasing function of daily dose throughout the period

that animals survived. The probability of occurrence of leukemia per unit of time, in both males and females exposed to less than 24 r per day, was higher by a constant factor than that in control mice at all ages, except for a latent period of about 100 days. This constant factor was larger for the female than for the male. 

At daily dosages higher than 24 r, incidence increased relative to time as an accelerated function of daily dose.

When curves for the incidence of radiationinduced leukemia were compared with curves for depression of lymphocyte count in the mice, they were found to correspond quantitatively in their dependence on time and dose-rate. All properties of the leukemia incidence data, and of their correspondence with the lymphocyte counts, are more consistent with a hypothesis that the incidence of radiation leukemia in mice under these exposure conditions is governed more strongly by physiological factors than it is by somatic mutation.

Although the data from the experiment are clearly inconsistent with the simplest somatic mutation hypotheses, a mutational factor cannot be ruled out on the evidence so far. However, the probability that mutations will result in cancers may be governed by endocrine and metabolic characteristics of the species. These facts indicate a direction for future study of leukemo-genesis by small radiation dosages: Investigation must be aimed at obtaining more precise knowledge of the physiological state affected by radiation exposure, and of the extent to which leukemia depends on physiological state and on radiation exposure per se. 

It will be necessary to determine the dependence of leukemia incidence on radiation exposure alone, independent of physiological injury, before decision can be reached regarding the existence and magnitude of a mutational factor. This, in turn, would provide a valid basis for extrapolation downward, by several orders of magnitude of radiation dose, to the low range of radiation exposure that results. from environmental sources.

Gross Radiation Effects 

Weapons test study of mice. In a study recently completed at Oak Ridge National Laboratory, male and female LAF, mice surviving instantaneous exposure to ionizing radiation from an experimental nuclear detonation 

(Operation Greenhouse held at Eniwetok Prov ing Ground April-May 1951) were observed until natural death occurred for possible delayed effects of irradiation. Among the effects observed was a shortening of the life span, which varied with the radiation dose. The shortening of life was not attributable to any specific cause but appeared to result from premature onset of all diseases associated with natural senescence. Although the effects of radiation on incidence and severity of diseases of old age varied markedly from one disease to another, onset of nearly all diseases was advanced to essentially the same extent by any one dose level of radiation.

No consistent overall relation was found between the frequency of neoplasia and the radiation dose. The incidence of certain neoplasms (thymic lymphoma, adenocarcinoma of mammary gland, pituitary adenoma, adrenal adenoma, Harderian gland adenoma, hepatoma, ovarian tumor, and granulocytic leukemia) was increased by irradiation, whereas the incidence of others (pulmonary adenoma, mammary gland sarcoma, nonthymic lymphoma) was decreased. In no instance, was there a linear relation between tumor incidence and dose. Most neoplasms were less common after large doses of radiation than after intermediate doses. 

Depigmentation of the hair, detected as early as 3 months after irradiation, progressed at a rate and to an extent that varied with the dose, but was not observed in lightly-irradiated mice or in unirradiated controls. Similarly, cataract of the optic lens, noted within 90 days after irradiation, progressed at a rate and to an extent that varied with dose, but the radiation cataracts differed in location from the opacities occurring spontaneously in senile controls.

Atrophy of the iris, which developed spontaneously in senescent controls, also occurred prematurely in irradiated mice and progressed in severity with the dose. Radiation nephritis, or nephrosclerosis, was common in mice exposed to more than 400 rad but was rare below this dose level. In a few mice with advanced nephrosclerosis, polyarteritis was noted in the kidney and elsewhere. Miscellaneous infections and inflammatory lesions that were relatively rare in controls were not increased in frequency by irradiation.

For most of the effects observed, neutrons appeared to be more effective than gamma rays. 

Because of uncertainties in dosimetry and the relatively small numbers of neutron-exposed animals, precise estimation of the relative effectiveness cannot be made from this experiment. 

The results demonstrate that instantaneous exposure to penetrating radiation sets in motion a complex series of changes culminating in premature onset of the diseases of natural senescence and life-shortening. The findings have implications of fundamental biological significance and will be influential in shaping research to follow.

There is no evidence that the effects observed in this study differ from those that would have been produced by comparable radiations delivered at a high intensity from conventional laboratory sources.

Irradiation and large animals. In 1954, 59 burros which had survived an exposure to high levels of gamma irradiation at a nuclear detonation test in 1951 were selected for continued study throughout their life span at the Uni versity of Tennessee-Atomic Energy Commis sion Agricultural Research Laboratory to supply information on long-term effects of irradiation in large animals which have a relatively long life span. In 1957, another group of 44 burros which survived neutron-gamma irradiation were added in the study. 

The death rate in the neutron-gamma survivors was much higher and death was preceded by loss of weight which led to a state of severe emaciation. Loss of weight also occurred in the gamma survivors prior to death but it was not so severe. The number of blood platelets (cell fragments involved in clotting) in some animals gradually declined over a period of months. In others it dropped abruptly shortly before death. The leukocytes and erythrocytes (white and red blood cells) were generally at subnormal values when death occurred. logical findings were necrotic enteritis (death of intestinal tissue) and hemorrhage. Patho- 

Chronic neutron irradiation experiments. Ex tensive experiments have been carried out at Oak Ridge National Laboratory with mice exposed to various doses of fast neutrons and gamma rays at graded intensities. Results to date indicate that a given dose of gamma rays is several-fold less effective if administered over an appreciable portion of the life span than if administered in a single brief exposure, as regards effects on survival and the incidence of diseases such as cataract, leukemia, and other neoplasms. With neutrons, less dependence on dose rates is evident.

Effects on Length of Life 

Irradiated young adult female rats of a hybrid strain have been observed until death at the University of California at Los Angeles for delayed irradiation effects following total body exposure with 120 r, 240 r, and 480 r. Each dose was given either as a single exposure, or as evenly fractionated exposures at intervals of 3, 5, 7, and 14 days.

After 45.5 months all irradiated and unirradiated control rats were dead. Life span has been shortened 33 percent following 480 r, 20.5 percent following 240 r, and 13.5 percent following 120 r, as compared to longevity of controls. Thus, longevity is clearly decreased after total-body irradiation in these ranges, and fractionation of the dose did not protect the animals to a significant degree at these levels of total irradiation. The radiation dose fatal within 30 days to half a group of rats ranges from 600 r to 1,000 r.

In another study, 242 female Wistar rats were observed throughout their lifespan after receiving 1,000 r, under hypoxic conditions (decreased oxygen intake) of total-body irradiation, or, alternatively, partial-body irradiation. Hypoxic irradiation, with anesthesia superimposed, resulted in 67 percent mortality in 30 days. The fact that those which survived 1,000 r had demonstrated resistance to acute radiation effects did not influence the magnitude of long-term delayed radiation effects on this group as measured by lifeshortening.

Life-shortening during the second post-irradiation year was closely correlated with growth retardation. Life-shortening was observed after partial-body irradiation to an extent approximately proportional to the weight of irradiated tissue. Nephrosclerosis (a kidney disease) was not observed unless the upper abdomen was included within the radiation field. Other than nephrosclerosis, a similar incidence of disease was observed at death both in control rats and in those subjected to partial and whole body irradiation.

In another experiment, 168 Long Evans-Wistar hybrid female rats approximately 3 months of age were irradiated as follows: 41 received 

200 r total body X-ray irradiation; 39 received 

200 r radiation delivered to surgically exposed adrenal glands; 41 received 800 r radiation delivered to the hind quarters and tail representing 25 percent of total body weight, and equivalent to 200 r total body exposure on a radiation-to-tissue weight ratio; 47 were not irradiated. 

After 23.5 months, survival in the four groups was:
