ΟΥ Controls Percentage surviving 17 46 22 58 The radiation exposure of hind quarters and tail was designed to deliver locally in terms of absorbed energy per animal the equivalent of 200 r whole body irradiation and without appreciable radiation to internal organs with known vital functions. Increased mortality nevertheless occurred as compared to nonirradiated controls. The influence of similar gramroentgen irradiation is evident: only 17 percent survived 200 r total-body irradiation as compared to 22 percent survival after 800 r caudal exposure. This shows a similar effect of totalbody irradiation as compared with partial-body irradiation where the amount of radiation delivered on a gram-roentgen basis is the same. 

When completed, this experiment is expected to provide information relating the life-shortening effects of irradiation to its effects on various tissues and organs of the body. Results now indicate that radiation-induced shortening of life occurs even when visceral organs, known to be essential for immediate survival, are not irradiated. This suggests that irradiation injury to tissues that are not specific to any visceral organ may be a factor in shortened life. 

Toxic Excretion After Irradiation Studies on excretion of toxic material formed in irradiated animals were continued at the University of Rochester-Atomic Energy Project. 

The particular effect being investigated at present is the finding that the urine of an irradiated rabbit contains a material which, after isolation and injection into a test rabbit, will cause such irradiation symptoms as anuria, acidosis, lipemia, tetany, tachycardia (increased heart rate), and even death when large amounts are given. The rabbit excretes this material prominently in the first few days and in smaller amounts through about 18 days. Retention is directly related to both early and later deaths. The site of production, the distribution, and the chemical and physiological properties of the material are being studied. 

The material is inactivated by oxidation, by treatment with acids, and by enzymes; it also can be inactivated by the known radiation-protective agents, thiourea, glutathione, cysteamine, and cysteine but not cystine.

Initial tests with the use of injected cysteine as a protection for irradiated rabbits indicate that it reduces the increase in heart rate that follows irradiation. 

Genetic and Related Studies 

Genetic and developmental effects of radiation.

A long-term project at the Oak Ridge National Laboratory, in which mice are the experimental animals, has been providing basic information for the estimation of the genetic hazards of exposure of human beings to ionizing radiation. 

In one series of experiments, morphological defects, longevity, productivity and other vital statistics have been measured in progeny of irradiated mice. In another set of experiments, data are being obtained on the factors that influence both the frequency and qualitative nature of the mutations induced. This includes comparisons of mutation induction in the various reproductive cell stages, from immature to mature, in both males and females. The relative effectiveness of different types of radiation, and the effects on mutation frequency of both dose and dose rate also are being investigated. 

A large amount of data accumulated this year has confirmed the finding reported earlier 4 that a given dose of chronic irradiation spread over a few weeks produces fewer mutations in mice than the same dose of acute irradiation given in a few minutes. This is contrary to a basic, long-held tenet of radiation genetics that effects were directly proportional to dose, regardless of dose rate. This discovery has opened up a new field of investigation. 

The data indicating that chronic exposure has considerably less genetic effect than acute exposure now is being developed in much more detailed studies. The tests have gone down to as low as 10 r per week. This is the lowest level ever tried in laboratory mutation rate experiments with animals. The mutation frequency at this low radiation level is significantly higher than in the controls. 

See pp. 198-199, Annual Report to Congress (January 

December 1959). 

The killing effect on spermatogonia (immature sperm) for X- and gamma-radiation can now be detected in mice down to 3 rads. 

Work on the effects of radiation on mouse embryological development in mammals is continuing with special emphasis on the stages shortly following fertilization or during early stages of growth; very striking sensitivity differences have been found in this investigation. 

To verify statistical evaluation of the Oak

Ridge results with other biological species, experiments were undertaken by the Brookhaven National Laboratory in cooperation with the 

Institute for Cancer Research, Philadelphia, and Indiana University, using drosophila (fruit flies) as the test material. Cobalt 60 gamma radiation was used and the period of irradiation varied from 31 seconds to 2 weeks.

A significant reduction in number of mutations was found in the chronically irradiated flies as compared with those receiving a single equal dose, thus confirming the results with mice. Low-level radiation and fertility. In an experiment conducted at the University of 

Rochester-Atomic Energy Project over the past 

7 years, radiation effects on the fertility of male dogs have been evaluated. The findings of this study are that the dog groups irradiated at 0.3 r a week (the permitted level of whole body dose in 1950 when the experiment was designed) and at 0.6 r a week, have shown no reduction of their ability to produce sperm. On the contrary, as the dog colony has grown older, these two groups have tended to show a lower incidence of sterility than the nonirradiated control group. In the group irradiated at a rate of 3.0 r a week, 8 of the 10 dogs became infertile after a year (150 r) and have remained so. The remaining two dogs, despite an accumulated dose of about 950 r, still produce sperm and irregularly have sired litters.

In additional experiments exploring the influence of accumulated radiation dose and size of daily dose on injury and recovery of the 

See pp. 200-201, Annual Report to Congress (January- 

December 1959).

spermatogenic process, it has been found that aspermia (a zero sperm count) that is presumably permanent (512 years so far), has resulted from total whole body irradiation of 477 r of X-rays, and of 634 r given at the rate of 3.0 r a day, 5 days a week-a total of 15.0 r a week. 

Currently, the investigators are testing the effects of 375 r given in a single total-body exposure and the same dose given at 3.0 r per day (15.0 r per week) for 25 weeks.

Five dogs so irradiated chronically showed a progressive depression of sperm count beginning generally after 10 weeks of irradiation and reaching zero count by the 23rd to 25th week. The sperm counts have remained zero since that time (34 weeks).

Five dogs given a single acute dose revealed a marked depression of sperm count beginning about 10 weeks later, and becoming lowest at 13 weeks. None of these dogs has shown a consistent zero count. Since the 13th post-radiation week only one dog has shown persistent depressed sperm count and this dog had a relatively low sperm count before irradiation.

Three dogs showed variably depressed sperm counts for 6 months, after which there was gradual increase in sperm count over the next 3 to 4 months both to normal or near preirradiation levels. The remaining dog showed persistent depression of sperm count for 9 to 10 months then recovered to about 2% the average pre-irradiation level. A longer period of observation is required for assessment of recovery in all these cases. 

Reproductive physiology of rats. A project at 

University of Tennessee-Atomic Energy Com mission Agricultural Research Laboratory is attempting to characterize the effects of radiation on the reproductive physiology in animals, especially during the prenatal and early postnatal period. 

Data obtained primarily from rats show that the effects of irradiation are dependent both on the stage of development of the young at the time of radiation and the dose of radiation.

Many of the effects were not apparent at birth but became evident weeks or months later. 

In mature male rats that had received 150 r of gamma radiation during the last 4 days before birth or the first 3 days after birth, weights of testes (male sex glands) were only 20 percent of normal, and all these males were sterile. In animals irradiated during the 4th through 8th days after birth, the mature testes weights were approximately 75 percent of normal and most of the males had normal fertility. 

Female litter mates of the males, irradiated on the 4th through 8th days after birth, were almost all sterile; in the few that did conceive, the young died before or shortly after birth. 

Approximately 50 percent of the prenatally irradiated females developed mammary tumors by 15 months of age compared to fewer than 

10 percent in unirradiated females used as controls.

Male calves irradiated in utero during the 

4th or 8th month of gestation had smaller testes at 8 months of age. Previous studies with male swine irradiated during mid-gestation showed a reduction of approximately 30 percent in testes weights and total sperm production. 

Pre-fertilization irradiation. A study at the

University of Rochester-Atomic Energy Proj ect provided information that female rats given 

500 r of total body irradiation before mating gave birth to offspring with birth weights much below normal. During lactation, there were more deaths among the litter, and after weaning they remained stunted, with ruffled coats and highly keratinized (horny) corkscrew-like tails.

These data tend to support the hypothesis that irradiation may interfere with placental function which would result in inadequate fetal nutrition, but the effect may also be on the fetal structures. The studies further direct attention to differences between rat and mouse response to radiation and emphasize the difference in the sensitivity of the primary oocyte (the egg cell before completion of the maturation process) in these species. They do not, how-

ever, preclude the possibility that pre-fertilization exposure to radiation may cause alterations in the genetic material of the oocyte. 

Genetic and cytogenic effects. A group at Oak 

Ridge National Laboratory has used fruit flies to analyze the basis for several kinds of genetic effects on survival and fertility after radiation and other mutagenic treatments. The class of dominant lethals (dominant genes resulting in death) has been analyzed into several component classes caused by different kinds of radiation damage to the nucleus. Several previously unsuspected types of sex-linked recessive lethals, the class which has been used for many of the fundamental studies on mutation, have been discovered. It has been found that the probability that irradiated spermatozoa (mature sperm) will give rise to an adult organism is related to the genetic composition of the mature gamete (reproductive cell).

The grasshopper neuroblast continues to be excellent material to study radiation effects.

Extensive work with the monochromatic ultra-violet microbeam has shown that irradiation of the nucleolus, a body within the cell nucleus, can completely inhibit mitosis (a type of cell division) at higher doses and accelerate it at very low doses. There are indications that the energy absorbed by ribonucleic acid is particularly important.

The problem of the specific biochemical mechanisms which mediate the nuclear contribution of information to the cytoplasm is approached in these studies through comparisons of the synthesizing capacities of cells with and without nuclei. The organism which has been used most extensively in this type of work is the protozoan Tetrahymena. By working with the cytoplasm and the nuclei separately and interchangeably, it has been possible to analyze the synthesis of ribonucleic acid and the rate at which it is released in the cytoplasm, especially during the different periods of mitosis. 

Another protozoan, Paramecium, continues to be excellent material for studying radiation protection and recovery. The time course of cell growth and chromosome duplication has been so carefully worked out in this organism that it has been possible to make a detailed analysis of the steps that take place between absorption of the radiant energy and the subsequent irrevocable establishment of genetic damage. Ways of modifying these steps have been found and are being further analyzed. 

The research in corn genetics has developed to the point where the genetic control of protein synthesis can be followed very well in maize endosperm tissue. Bean roots continue to be the ideal material with which to study chromosome breaks and the rejoining of these chromosomes because radiation damage and recovery can be studied both microscopically and with biochemical techniques on one and the same material and experiment. The demonstration that energy, especially as supplied by adenosinetriphosphate (ATP), is necessary for chromosome breaks to rejoin, which was made for the first time with this material, is being followed up by other studies of the breakage and reunion processes. The apparent synergistic effects of far-red radiation with X-rays have been shown to be an effect on mitosis and not an effect on the breakage and reunion processes. The mold, Neurospora, continues to be a very important material for studies on gene chromosome structure and gene enzyme relationship, especially where one can use a specific mutation. 

Bacteriophage is being used for studies of the mutational processes in molecular terms. 

Treatment of the phage with radiation or chemical mutagens induce changes in the genetic material (DNA) that lead to mutation only when the phage multiplies within its host. 

The molecular changes that occur in the DNA and the steps by which the mutation is eventually established inside the host are being followed. 

Effects of heavy water. Research at the Lawrence Radiation Laboratory, Berkeley, demonstrated that deuterium oxide (DO) or heavy

water interferes with cell division in the sperm cells of mice, in ascites tumor cells, and in the alga, Chlorella. Sterility has been produced in both male and female fruit flies (D. melanogaster), and decreased numbers of offspring resulting from fertile matings have been observed.

It is believed that the genetic material of the cell is affected by D₂O, perhaps by changes in the physico-chemical forces that bind the macromolecules, including chromosomes. Heavy water may bring about these changes particularly during the time when the structures within the cell are rearranging during cell division. 

Cell Size and Radiosensitivity

In spite of many investigations, it has not been possible to predict the radiosensitivity of a species not previously studied. Studies at the 

Brookhaven National Laboratory have succeeded in offering a possible explanation for the differences in radiosensitivity and may allow better predictions of tolerance of plants (and eventually, perhaps, of animals) to ionizing radiation. These conclusions arise from more than 10 years of study.

Since chromosome size is known to be an important factor in species tolerance, and since chromosome size is related to the size of the cell 573517 0-61-5 nucleus, the nuclear volumes of actively dividing cells in the shoot tips of about 40 species assumed to be diploid (have double chromosomes) were measured. When average nuclear volumes of the cells were plotted against the chronic daily dose necessary to produce severe growth inhibition, the resultant graph showed a clear relationship: the larger the nuclear volume, the smaller the amount of radiation required to stop growth. Since nuclear volume and amount of DNA (deoxyribonucleic acid) per nucleus are related, the above data may possibly be extrapolated to relate the average amount of DNA per cell to cellular radiosensitivity.

If this correlation can be shown to hold for other types of organisms, it should be possible to predict, under specified conditions, the amount of radiation required to stop active growth of an organism. It is not yet clear whether these same factors also can be used to predict lethal radiation dosages for organisms.

BIOCHEMICAL AND MOLECULAR STUDIES 

Understanding the damaging effects of radiation on a total organism requires more complete delineation of the functions of cells and their components. Selected studies in this field are described in this section. 

Chemical Analysis of Cell Behavior 

Understanding the biochemical changes that occur in normal living cells is mandatory in order to learn how to prevent or reverse changes brought about in cells by ionizing radiation. Research has been directed toward this area and also toward the chemical basis of cellular growth as a key to understanding the abnormal tissue growth which occurs in cancer, and for assisting the possible prevention, control, and cure of other diseases.

The cells that comprise all living matter consist of an outer membrane in which is enclosed a nucleus, the carrier of the reproductive material of the cell, and a cytoplasm made up of a

liquid-like protoplasm containing particles which perform most of the cell's functions. 

The aggregation of a mass of cells that perform a specific function is known as a "tissue". 

Such organs as the kidney, liver, and thyroid are tissues made up of masses of specialized cells. Cells from different tissues are similar in many respects, but differ with the specific function of the tissue they form. Kidney cells, for example, have the special ability to excrete waste products, while thyroid cells manufacture a hormone which governs many body functions. What makes these cells function differently? Scientists believe that it is the nucleus of the cell that carries the so-called "information" which dictates the function of the cell, and this information is somewhat different for each tissue. 

A number of groups have studied the chemical nature of the genetic material in the nucleus the supposed carrier of "information".

Since the genetic apparatus always remains within the nucleus itself, attempts are being made to identify the chemical messenger which transmits this nuclear information to the rest of the cell, and to learn how this chemical messenger is manufactured by the cell.

Nucleic Acid Metabolism 

Ribonucleic acid (RNA) is believed to be involved with protein synthesis in the living cell since RNA occurs in virtually every structure. One possible way in which RNA might function is as the carrier of genetic characteristics, or "information", from the deoxyribonucleic acid (DNA) of the chromosomal gene to sites of protein synthesis in the cytoplasm. 

Such a function is very much in the realm of speculation, but a body of circumstantial evidence agrees with various parts of this. hypothesis.

Studies of the metabolism of RNA as it relates to intracellular morphology have been undertaken. At Brookhaven National Laboratory a precursor of RNA was labeled with tritium (radioactive hydrogen) and autoradiographs were made of the cells in root meristems from the bean plant Vicia faba. 

Seedlings were grown on nutrient solutions containing tritiated-cytidine, a precursor of both RNA and DNA, and at intervals roots were fixed, embedded in paraffin, and sectioned according to standard procedures. Some sections of tissue were processed onto microscope slides so that neither RNA nor DNA was removed. Adjacent sections on separate microscope slides were treated chemically to remove RNA while leaving DNA intact. Both groups of slides then were covered with autoradio graphic film and processed together so that radiation from the cells would result in selfphotographs of distribution of the tritium labeled materials.

By comparing the resulting autoradiographs of adjacent sections of individual cells, some with, and some without RNA, it was possible to study incorporation of cytidine into both RNA and DNA separately and simultaneously in the same cells. Different interpretations of the observations are possible, but the most likely one is that RNA is synthesized mainly in two regions within the nucleus of the cell called the nucleolus and the chromatin. The fraction synthesized in the chromatin remains associated with this structure for a relatively short time (possibly only a few minutes) and then separates and diffuses to the nucleolus. 

It remains associated in the nucleolus for a relatively long time (90 minutes) before diffusing to the cytoplasm (intracellular material which surrounds the nucleus). RNA synthesized in the nucleolus rather than in the chromatin also diffuses to the cytoplasm. Apparently very little RNA is synthesized in the cytoplasm itself.

These interpretations are consistent with the hypothesis that RNA is the intermediate that is synthesized under the influence of the genes, and that it carries genetic information to other sites of the cell such as the cytoplasm, where presumably it is involved in the synthesis of specific proteins.

dis TOW ་! Understanding the structure and function of nucleic acids and nucleic acid derivatives is important because these substances constitute the core and structure of the genes (basic genetic material incorporated in chromosomes). Over 

10 years ago, a group at the Oak Ridge National Laboratory worked out aspects of nucleic acid structure by means of chromatographic techniques. Continuing in this field, the group recently isolated and established the structure of another previously unidentified component of 

RNA, a compound called pseudouridine.

Scientists also have clarified the mode of action of one of the DNA-synthesizing enzymes of mammalian tissues, and the nature of the enzyme's precursor (so-called "primer") material. 

Molecular Basis of Cell Function 

At Oak Ridge National Laboratory, cell studies are concerned with the molecular basis for cell function. Experimenters have undertaken to isolate large quantities of sub-cellular particles with a minimum of alteration of their nature, breaking down the particles into their constituent macromolecules, determining the biophysical character of these substances, and, finally, defining their role in the cell.

A number of studies have been completed on cell fractions isolated from rat tissues. Soluble liver proteins were found to be unstable in glass containers at body temperature, however certain biochemicals-adenosine triphosphate and other polyanions-effectively stabilized the proteins, suggesting that they may perform a similar function in the living cell. Other studies, of the DNA protein strands (molecular chains), prepared from nuclei from thymus gland cells, showed that the strands could be broken before extraction by deforming the nuclei. Neutrons were found approximately onethird as effective as X-rays in breaking the strands. Since cell chromosomes (groups of genetic material within the nucleus), on the other hand, are damaged more by neutrons than by X-rays, this finding suggests that chromosomes may be composed of a bundle of strands. 

Enzymes and Protein Synthesis 

The problem of understanding the biochemical mechanisms underlying normal growth and cell-differentiation is being investigated at Oak Ridge National Laboratory through studies of enzymatic mechanisms-mechanisms that accomplish biological catalysis-which are involved in biosynthetic reactions in general, and of the biosynthesis of proteins, nucleic acids, and polysaccharides in particular. Most of these studies use broken cell preparations or cell-free extracts of biological tissues from a great variety of sources: rat, mouse, bactería, molds, protozoa, and higher plants. 

The role of soluble RNA and guanosine triphosphate in transport and incorporation of amino acids into proteins was found to differ widely from system to system. These studies are being conducted on mutant strains of corn as well as in the intestines of mice irradiated with 1500 (approximately twice the lethal dose) and protected by pre-irradiation dosing with AET (S, 2-aminoethylisothiuronium Br· 

HBr). The mouse study has revealed that on the fourth day after irradiation, protein synthesis is as much as 10 times greater than normal.

Another study on induced enzymes in bacteria-those present only when needed—as opposed to those enzymes that are always present called constitutive enzymes, showed that this inhibition is reversed by certain wavelengths of light photoreactivation. Analysis of this phenomenon led to discovery of a specific nucleoprotein particle seemingly involved in direct synthesis of the enzyme. 

Oxygen and Free Radicals in Cells

It has been found extremely difficult to relate the specific fundamental mechanisms of the physical or chemical events caused by inter-

action with high-energy radiation to a specific change in function of a cell or a multicellular organism. Among the difficulties are the complex and variable biochemical activities of the living cell which obscure early post-irradiation events, and the short lifetimes of the presumed intermediate states resulting from irradiation in aqueous systems.

At Argonne National Laboratory, these diffi culties were partly avoided by using living but metabolically inert test material-dry spores of a bacterium, Bacillus megaterium. It was found that the spores not only gave a reproducibility of response unequalled by most other cell systems, but could be subjected to extreme variations in dose rate, type of radiation, temperature, and gaseous environment. The biological effects of these variables were measured in terms of the changes they produced in the relationship between radiation sensitivity and dose. The criterion of radiation sensitivity was loss by the bacillus of capacity to form colonies. In parallel experiments, paramagnetic resonance measurements were made on irradiated spores for physical evidence of the presence of free radicals. (A free radical is a molecule possessing an unpaired electron, and is thus highly reactive.)

The presence of oxygen during irradiation increased the sensitivity of the spores to radiation. If oxygen was replaced by nitrogen during irradiation, but then supplied immediately afterward, sensitivity was decreased. Nitric oxide reduced sensitivity still further when given after irradiation and just before the oxygen. When it was present during irradiation, without oxygen, its net protective effect was lessened.

The results indicate the presence of three distinct classes of damage:

Class I, the most immediate, occurs whether or not oxygen is present at the time of irradiation, and is direct and irreversible. 

Class II occurs when oxygen or nitric oxide is present during irradiation, and results in additional damage, possibly attributable to quenching of luminescence with resultant conservation of energy within the spore. 

Class III damage occurs when oxygen is present after irradiation. It is the consequence of formation of relatively long-lived free radicals produced during irradiation, which can interact with oxygen to form lethal compounds. 

These radicals are rendered harmless by heating or by reaction with nitric oxide, a known scavenger of free radicals.

Paramagnetic resonance tests-a physical system for detecting free radicals-provided data that quantitatively confirmed the presence of long-lived free radicals correlated with biological damage. The first species of radicals produced were not the final radicals; this indicates that electrons or radicals can migrate within the cell.

The data relating to oxygen and heating effects were consistent with the results of the biological experiments. There was evidence of a reaction between the radicals and oxygen, supporting the idea that part of the damage can be attributed to toxic oxyradicals. Heating was followed by evidence of partial destruction of radicals, to a degree consistent with the observed reduction of biological injury: the radicals that remained were capable of reacting with oxygen.

Nitric oxide almost completely obliterated the evidence of radicals although its biological effects were the same as those of heat treatment; a possible explanation is that one portion of the nitric oxide acts by removing free radicals to prevent damage, while another portion reacts with radicals to form toxic products. 

One scavenger of free radicals that prevents 

Class III damage is hydrogen sulfide; chemically related compounds such as cysteine have been shown to protect against the biological effects of irradiation. 

The evidence leads to conclusions that a large specific portion of the damage produced in living cells by high-energy radiation results from the formation of free radicals that react secondarily to bring about biological injury. 

This conclusion establishes a physical basis for the observed effects of oxygen and nitric oxide on the response. Inconsistencies between

physical and biological results are open to reasonable explanations. Further investigation will be directed toward clarifying the means by which energy is transferred within the cell, and toward providing information helpful in explaining the mechanism of effects induced by ionizing radiations in higher organisms. 

Microbe Metabolism and Biochemistry 

The biochemical mechanisms of several basic biological systems in micro-organisms being studied at the Oak Ridge National Laboratory include peroxide breakdown, synthesis of catalase (enzyme capable of decomposing hydrogen peroxide into water and oxygen) and catalase mode of action, and fatty acid metabolism. Ionizing irradiation of aqueous systems produces peroxides which are believed to be a principal cause of radiation induced biological damage.

Studies on peroxidizing enzymes and on catalase have increased basic knowledge about normal functioning of these systems. The functions of two principal enzymes in fatty acid metabolism have been clarified, as has the role of the vitamin, biotin, in one of the steps. 

Tumors and enzyme activity. Pure cultures of tumor cells (from so-called Walker tumors-rat tumors which are easily transplanted) have been produced at the University of Rochester-Atomic Energy Project in an at tempt to identify the presence of a number of serum enzymes, proteases, and peptidases in both tumor cells and their nutrient medium. 

Serum from rats bearing Walker tumors as subcutaneous implants has been studied for the same enzymes. Observed differences between serum enzyme activities of tumor-bearing rats and normal rats may afford a basis for early detection of growing tumors.

Biosynthesis of Fully Deuterated Compounds The discovery at Argonne National Labora tory that a variety of organisms can be cultured in 99.8 percent heavy water (deuterium oxide) leads to interesting possibilities, for example, development of a practical method of producing complex compounds in which all ordinary hydrogen is replaced by heavy hydrogen.

Although it had been believed that complete replacement of hydrogen by deuterium was incompatible with life, careful experimentation revealed that a number of algae (single-celled microscopic plants) can be induced to grow in highly concentrated heavy water. Since these organisms live exclusively on carbon dioxide, water, and mineral nutrients, it follows that algae obtain all their hydrogen from water. 

Consequently, the organisms grown in 99.8 percent heavy water synthesize chemical compounds that are fully deuterated. Frequently, these are compounds that as yet defy synthesis. 

Hence, biosynthesis in heavy water offers the only practical means of producing these fully deuterated proteins, enzymes, and other important classes of compounds.

Fully deuterated compounds and organisms should be particularly valuable in the study of radiation effects in living systems, for electron spin resonance and nuclear magnetic resonance studies, and for numerous other biophysical studies. From the biochemical point of view, essential deuterated metabolites may have unique application in studying the control of metabolic processes. 

So far, seven varieties of algae have been successfully cultured in heavy water. The green algae Scenedesmus obliquus is being cultivated successfully on a large scale. Hundreds of grams of fully deuterated algae have been harvested, and gram amounts of fully deuterated compounds have been isolated by chromatography, including fully deuterated glucose, mannose, and rhamnose, and the amino acids, alanine, glutamic acid, serine, and phenylalanine. Chloroplast pigments, highly purified chlorophyll, carotene, and other pigments, are fully deuterated, are readily available. 

Milligram amounts of fully deuterated ribonucleic acid have been extracted, and experiments

are in progress to extract enzymes and other proteins. 

Using the biosynthesized deuterated carbohydrates as nutrient, experimenters have grown fully deuterated bacteria, yeast, and molds, and thus made available the synthesizing capacity of these organisms for production of other fully deuterated compounds. 

Biological Clocks

All complex living organisms have biological time-cycle mechanisms that are related by undiscovered physical-chemical processes to molecular activities of cells. Recent evidence indicates the presence within simple cells of chemical compounds that somehow regulate cyclic physiological adjustments such as those involved in wakefulness and sleepiness. Many of these biological clocks, referred to as circadian rhythms, have periods of about 24 hours.

Circadian rhythmicity in a one-celled organism, Paramecium bursaria, is being studied quantitatively at Argonne National Laboratory.

A manifestation of this organism's cyclic mechanism is the diurnal rhythm of its mating reaction which is very sensitive to radiant energy. In nature, this rhythm can be reset to a single daily maximum by visible light, or by ultraviolet light. The resetting can be reversed by exposure to light of longer wavelengths, for instance, the blue-violet of the visible spectrum.

The most light-sensitive phase of the organism's cycle is the sexually non-reactive period during which precursors of a "mating substance" are being produced. The pattern of response suggested that it might be associated with the metabolism of nucleic acids. This idea was tested by studying changes in the nucleic acid-protein ratio, and in the incorporation of labeled precursors of nucleic acids into cells, both as a function of the specific phase of the mating cycle, and as a function of radiation with various wavelengths of light. Close correlations were found between the physiological cycles in reactivity, and the biochemical cycles. The findings are compatible with the theory that nucleic acid metabolism is associated causally with the timekeeping mechanism of this organism. 

PLANT STUDIES AND PHOTOSYNTHESIS 

Recent studies are concerned with the mechanisms underlying cellular growth and differentiation in plants. Special attention was given at Oak Ridge National Laboratory to the effect of visible and ionizing radiations and the actions of chemical morphogens and plant hormones. Growth in plants can occur both by enlargement of cells and by division of cells. 

It is possible to study factors in lettuce seeds which act directly on cell division without considering growth due to expansion of cells. In this system, it has been shown that the growthacceleration caused by gibberellic acid results solely from cell expansion, and that the herbicide, maleic hydrazide, acts by inhibiting mitosis (cell division). Radiation effects on seeds and other plant tissues are under investigation, and results will be compared with these findings.

Photosynthesis studies at Oak Ridge National Laboratory are concerned mainly with the physical aspects of the light absorption and energy transfer by which plants convert sunlight to chemical energy in the form of plant substances. It has been shown that the chloroplast acts as a semiconductor in this process, and that chloroplasts are photoconducting. 

More recently it has been found that the initial energy transfer process involves free electrons and therefore the photosynthetic energy transfer problem must be assessed in terms of solid state physics.

Radioactive tracers have been widely used to study chemical reactions in the cells of green plants. Work at the Lawrence Radiation Lab oratory, University of California, has shown a difference between the reactions of the enzyme systems in the intact cell compared with the same enzyme reactions on an artificial medium

TE in a test tube. This work is useful in supplying interpretation of biochemical reactions. 

Also at Lawrence Radiation Laboratory, the cells of photosynthesizing green plants have been broken up by high-frequency (100 kilocycles) vibrations, and the various fragments have been tested for photosynethetic activity. 

This work has helped to explain the relation of structure and function in the photosynthetic apparatus.

To test theories of the way in which green plants convert the energy of sunlight into chemical energy, "model" systems of organic compounds have been constructed at Lawrence Radiation Laboratory. The compounds used in these model systems have structures and functions similar to those of the compounds believed to accomplish energy conversion in plants. 

Tests of such models have led to effects very similar to those observed in plants. For example, the light striking green plants is known to produce free, or unpaired, electrons. In an analogous result, the light striking one of the model systems produced an electrical potential. 

X-ray-Induced Chromosome Breaks In a study at Argonne National Laboratory, a biological monochromator which projects light of selected wavelengths was used to uncover the biochemical processes underlying certain biological responses to light in the far-red wavelength between red and infrared. Far-red light has been reported to prevent the rejoining of chromosome breaks induced by X-rays, and it is known that red and far-red light influence a number of plant processes. Since restitution of chromosome breaks requires metabolic energy in the form of the high-energy phosphate bonds of adenosinetriphosphate (ATP), it appeared logical that far-red light might affect the capacity of cells to form ATP. 

This hypothesis was confirmed experimentally in studies of isolated mitochondria, the subcellular particulates in which the relevant biochemical activities are concentrated. The 

ATP-forming ability of mitochondria isolated from irradiated plants was strikingly dependent on the particular wavelength of red light used for irradiation. Isolated mitochondria from rat liver were responsive to illumination; far-red light inhibited their capacity to form 

ATP, while bright red light enhanced this capacity, or reversed the inhibition.

This demonstration provides a biochemical basis for the far-red effect on chromosome breaks and probably a number of plant responses.

Role of Ultraviolet Radiation 

A comparison of the effects of different wavelengths of radiation studied at the Oak Ridge National Laboratory indicate that ultraviolet (UV) rays and X-rays, when used together, enhance the induction of chromosome aberrations in certain plants (Tradescantia pollen). 

Results with other species also indicate that increased effects of radiation, when ultraviolet and X-rays are combined, suggest that such a combination probably is quite a general phenomenon.

PLANT ECOLOGY AND RADIATION Studies Near Unshielded Reactor 

Sensitivity of different plants to radiation is a major problem in radiobiology. Few native species have been studied, but at the Lockheed 

Reactor Site near Dawnsonville, Georgia which operates an unshielded reactor used for special testing purposes, a number of observations were made during 1959-60, notably with regard to sensitivity of pine species (Pinus virginiana and Pinus echinata). • The research reactors in general use, and all power reactors, are heavily shielded with special high density concretes, and by other means, which absorb and control the radiation emitted inside the reactors. Radiation outside the shield of a reactor is too low to cause impairment of living things even immediately outside the shield. The special Lockheed reactor is deliberately unshielded to permit a high neutron and gamma radiation level outside the reactor assembly for radiation testing purposes. For this reason the reactor is in an isolated area, surrounded by fences to keep people beyond the range of dangerous exposure, and carefully guarded. It is controlled from a safe distance in shielded facilities.

Twelve months after the first irradiation, the cumulative line of sight dosage to vegetation ranged from about 30,000 r mixed gammaneutron radiation at 500 feet to about 4,000 r at 1,000 feet. All pine trees up to 1,000 feet were killed and many growth aberrations were noted at greater distances. In the autumn of 

1959 leaf fall of oak and hickory trees was one to three weeks early in areas between 500 and 

1,200 feet from the reactor. The same was true for many herbs. Some annuals in an experimental cobalt 60 irradiation field at the same site completed their life cycles three to four weeks early having accumulated about 10,000 in chronic exposure over the growing season.

In the spring of 1960, after irradiation during 1959, winter dormancy was prolonged in the trees and shrubs up to 1,500 feet from the reactor, resulting in an island of vegetation up to 3,000 feet across that remained leafless after surrounding vegetation had come into full leaf. 

Over a period of one month most of the trees leafed out beyond 700 to 800 feet from the reactor. The prolongation of dormancy was proportional to radiation dose but was reflected differentially by various species. The net result is to shorten the growing period both by early leaf fall and by prolonged dormancy. 

Within 700 to 800 feet of the reactor, most terminal buds of woody vegetation were killed. 

Growth was resumed by adventitious buds on stems several years old. Continued exposure to radiation probably would result in the eventual death of the hardwoods, not because of death of the whole tree as in the pine, but by continued destruction of growing tips. Even if lateral shoots persist, it is likely that their reduction in number and shortened photosynthetic season will result in starvation and death.

Another marked change observed was in composition of vegetation in abandoned fields, 400 to 600 feet from the reactor, where predominant plant types have shown marked shifts through the year as a result of differential sensitivity to radiation.

Shielding and scatter effects by the terrain and by woody vegetation are being evaluated. 

Neutrons and gamma rays have been scattered by the upper two thirds of an oak-hickory stand (60 to 80 feet high) so as to deflect the radiation downward and this results in biological effects on the lower part of the forest despite its being shielded by intervening rises in the land surface. Radiation in the terrainshielded area is about half that received in the direct line of sight. Tree trunks 10 to 12 inches in diameter on the other hand, have noticeable shielding effects to material on the protected side of the trunks, shielding vines and the development of adventitious buds.

Studies In Gamma Field 

Pitch pines growing near the cobalt 60 gamma field at Brookhaven National Laboratory have been killed after exposure to radiation at estimated accumulated levels of 5,000 to 8,000 r. 

Experimental work on more than 200 species of domesticated plant species has been accomplished at Brookhaven National Laboratory during the past 10 years by acute or chronic exposure to gamma radiation from cobalt 60 sources varying from less than 1 to about 4,000 curies. For each species studied several arcrows of plants were exposed simultaneously.

In this way a wide range of dose rates were given, and one or two test runs made it possible to determine the dosage necessary to stunt severely or stop the growth of the plant. 

Species tolerances were found to differ at least 1,000-fold. Pitch pine (Pinus rigida) is killed by prolonged exposure to dose rates as low as 5 r a day where as the common gladiolus can survive about 5,000 r a day through a 100 day growing season. Gladiolus exposed to 3,- 

200 per day for a full season were able to grow again the succeeding season. Those exposed to 5,000 r per day have not been tested yet during a second season. 

Yew (Taxus media var. harfieldii) at an ex-

posure of 20 r a day, and a cumulative dose of 

8,200 r, died in two seasons; at 15 r per day and a cumulative dose of 9,870 r, death occurred in the third season; at 10 r per day, cumulative exposure 6,580 r, more than half the plants died in three seasons. 

Chrysanthemum lacustre exposed to 1,340 r a day was still alive at the end of a season after a cumulative dose of 16,500 r.

Some of the most resistant plants were polyploids (those having more than 2 sets of chromosomes). Radiation Sensitive. Native pitch pine (pinus rigida) have been shown to be extremely sensitive to irradiation at the Brookhaven National Laboratory. Dose rates of three to five roentgens per day for six seasons have severely damaged or killed four trees of this species (arrows) which were growing beside the laboratory's gamma radiation plant-growth test plot. Other coniferous evergreens have shown a similarly high sensitivity to radiation. The other trees in the photo are mostly deciduous and are living.

Environmental Sciences Research WASTES AND THE ENVIRONMENT The Commission's Annual Report to Congress for 1959 (January 1960) contained a special section on waste disposal including a chapter on research programs concerned with the disposition of radioactive wastes and their effect on the environment. This report describes in the following section some recent results in continuing programs at Hanford laboratories, Oak

Ridge National Laboratory, and the National 

Reactor Testing Station. 

Waste Disposal Research 

Research at Hanford laboratories has demonstrated that slightly contaminated radioactive waste waters may safely be disposed of directly to the ground at sites having favorable soils or geological features. The wastes undergo a natural cleansing process as they percolate through suitable soils. This removes radioactive constitutents from water as a water softener does. 

The adsorbed radioactive nuclides then decay so that the radioactive atoms do not saturate the soil. This system of disposal, used successfully at Hanford, has been studied extensively to delineate the factors involved, and the behavior patterns of the wastes, in order to determine the general applicability of this system. 

Laboratory experiments in soil chemistry have been conducted to study the rate of appearance through soil columns of waste constituents. Such breakthrough curves for waste constituents were found to agree well with theory. The action of each important factor in the process was studied, including concentrations of the radionuclides and of salts, the acidity or basicity of the solution, types of soils, and temperature effects. The results provide a basis for predicting the chemical behavior of waste solutions discharged to the ground. 68 A field experiment was undertaken to test these laboratory results when applied to an actual case. 

The experimental disposal pit or "crib" was amenable to precise comparison because it could be operated with good control and was located in a region where a water table 10 feet below the bottom of the crib could be sampled, thus permitting collection of meaningful data in a reasonable period of time. 

Breakthrough data were obtained through samples taken from wells spaced around the cribs and the results agreed well with those resulting from laboratory experiments. The results, however, revealed that crib area had a much smaller influence than expected.

Mineral components in soils are known to remove radioactive constituents from wastes by means of reactions other than simple adsorption. 

For example, the removal of strontium from phosphate solutions by calcite (limestone) permits disposal of some types of high-salt waste solutions. Mineralogical research is conducted to discover such significant reactions, particularly removal of some particular isotope, or class of isotopes, by fixation in or on a solid bed. 

Soil physics. Physical effects of the earth on radioactive waters are more complex and less well understood than chemical effects, and are much less amenable to direct study. Radioactive material in solutions discharged to the ground will move through the soil under the influence of ground water gradients. The direction and rate of movement of these materials is studied to determine the effects of various factors and to test equations for predicting flow. Laboratory research is performed by means of variously-sized columns or boxes of soil or fine sand in which a simple hydrologic system may be simulated. Hydraulic characteristics of soil samples are measured, and hydrologic principles are demonstrated, to test

the validity of a mathematical model of liquid movement, or the limiting conditions assumed in its construction, or to test and calibrate instruments designed for field applications. 

The movements of liquids through soils under conditions of unsaturated flow is of considerable interest, particularly in the case of possible leaks of radioactive solutions. Unsaturated flow refers to liquid movement when the soil pores are not all filled with liquid. Laboratory studies demonstrated that considerable amounts of liquid may be transported through unsaturated soils with velocities similar to those under saturated conditions. A mathematical model was developed, and tested, to express the relationships of unsaturated flow rates to various conditions.

One of the reactions known to occur in soils which is most useful for removing selected radioactive fission products from wastes is adsorption or ion exchange with the montmorillonitic clays such as are present in small amounts in most Hanford soil. Much of the observed variation in behavior exhibited by waste components under different conditions is typical of ion exchange reactions. For example, the relative influence of other metal cations in the waste solution would be predicted on the basis of their competition for ion exchange positions on clay surfaces. 

Studies of the behavior of rare earth isotopes in wastes showed some deviation from that expected. In a critical pH (acid-base) range from about 8 to 10-moderate alkalinity—these isotopes appeared to move through soils without being removed. Further study showed that rare earths occur as finely divided precipitates that may become colloidal (suspended in liquid) in this critical pH range. The colloidal particles are negatively charged and thus do not adsorb on soils. In some cases, the fine material is able to move through soil pores, but relatively low concentrations of stable salts of other metals will prevent them from becoming colloidal.

Specific surface reactions are associated with minerals having surface adsorption sites peculiarly receptive to ions of a certain size and charge. In addition to the adsorption of cesium by certain natural zeolites (a silicate) a few other such reactions were found. Of possible significance is a specific surface reaction between a commercial zeolite and strontium ions. This material is so selective for strontium that it will remove traces of it from solution even in the presence of higher concentrations of calcium.

Geologic studies. The various geologic formations and structures have an important bearing on the behavior of wastes disposed into them. By identifying the sequence of these formations, some insight can be gained into their mode of deposition, their origin, and their position. At the Hanford site, two formations of semi-consolidated sediments overlie the basalt bedrock. The radioactive wastes are disposed into the formation comprising the upper Recent sands and gravels. The water table into which the wastes percolate lies in that formation but in places it goes into the underlying, finer-grained Ringold. Physical and chemical properties of the two formations differ, the most significant difference being in their permeability to water.

The Ringold-Recent formations were studied on the basis of properties of direct concern to waste disposal (i.e., permeability, exchange capacity, particle size distribution, and clay and silt mineralogy), as well as on the more common basis of lithology. Two erosion surfaces evidently exist on the Ringold formation, the first capped by caliche and overlain by Palouse soil, the second incised into the first as a network of old Columbia River channels. 

Disposal of wastes to the ground preferably should be where the caliche and Palouse soil are present to decontaminate and disperse the liquids, and where Ringold sediments can decontaminate the wastes and retard the movement. Avoidance of the channel areas is desirable, owing to the high velocity of movement of ground waters at those points. 

Recent studies of the basalt bedrock beneath

Hanford were begun to determine the potential feasibility of injection of wastes deep within or beneath these rocks. The studies were aided by procurement of logs of an oil test well, drilled in 1957 and 1958 adjacent to the Hanford Works to a depth of 10,655 feet. The well failed to penetrate into pre-basalt formations. 

More than 100 basalt flows were encountered, generally separated by beds of tuff and zones of scoriaceous basalt. While some of the beds and zones might be suitable receptors of radioactive liquid wastes, it would be extremely difficult to determine the composition and extent of individual beds to that degree permitting disposal of wastes into them. 

Instruments and techniques. The ground water hydrology and geology studies require special techniques and instruments if they are to characterize aquifer formations and to delineate geological structures. Seismic and magnetometric measurement methods were explored to determine their applicability in defining geologic structures of interest to radioactive wastes disposal. Seismic methods were found useful in determining the depth of basalt and, under some conditions, the water table could be identified. 

Airborne magnetrometer measurements made by the U.S. Geological Survey were correlated with known geologic entities, with conclusion that magnetometry could usefully supplement seismic research. 

In measuring moisture in the soil, a device emitting 12 cm radiowaves appears promising; according to models in the laboratory these waves were found to be strongly absorbed by moisture in soil. In-well measurements of moisture using a neutron probe proved an accurate method for field work. 

To sample ground water in wells, some of which contain over 500 feet of water, a sampler was designed which permits sampling throughout this column. Tests in 19 wells showed no significent difference between concentrations of isotopes in water at the top and at the bottom of these wells. 

Ecological Research

Ecological research in White Oak Lake bed takes advantage of radioactivity resulting from waste disposal in this area to obtain information on the movement, distribution, and biological cycling under field conditions of radioactive fission products (strontium 90, cesium 137 and ruthenium-rhodium 106). This 35-acre area provides sufficient space for basic and applied studies including movement in soil, uptake by plants, rates of transfer through food chains, and effect of chronic irradiation on a variety of life forms. Essentially, this area is the laboratory for a giant tracer experiment in which contained radioisotopes are being used to obtain fundamental information on ecological relations between organisms and their environment. Insect accumulations. A three-year study of insect population on White Oak Lake bed has identified and measured the insect populations, and delineated their role in the turnover of radioisotopes. As major plant consumers, the insects are an important link in the biological food chain.

Recent analyses permit a comparison of the radioisotope concentrations in insects with those in other phases of the White Oak Lake bed ecological system. Concentrations of strontium 

90 in insects on a unit weight basis were about 25 percent of soil values; cesium 137 concentrations about 1 percent. However, all insects combined for an area were estimated to contain only about one millionth of the amount of activity in the soil for the same area. Concentrations of radionuclides in plants and in insects, combined with laboratory data on radioisotope elimination by insects, suggest that about 5 percent of the radioactive materials taken up by plants may be consumed by insects during a growing season. 

Accumulation by birds. Radioassay of song sparrows living on White Oak Lake bed revealed a striking seasonal difference in fission product uptake, which illustrated two ecological

routes by which body burdens of radioactivity were accumulated. 

Birds resident only in summer accumulated fission products through fruit and insects with the result that the burden of strontium 90 in bone was greater that that of cesium 137 in muscle, even though the concentration of cesium 137 in the soil was 25 to 75 times greater than that of strontium 90. 

Winter bird populations accumulated more radiocesium than radiostrontium as a result of eating fallen seeds coated with lake bed soil. 

Although the quantities accumulated were small, the difference observed between summer and winter populations of the same species emphasizes the importance of detailed studies on interrelationships between organisms and their environment.

Ecological systems. Data on movement of radioactive isotopes in contaminated sites, and in tracer experiments carried on elsewhere in the Oak Ridge reservation, provide information on individual steps in the general circulation of chemical elements in ecological systems such as forests, fields, or bodies of water. An objective of basic ecological research at Oak Ridge is to understand the circulation of the nutrient elements that comprise and maintain ecological systems. The research has theoretical and practical implications for ecological systems elsewhere over and above its purpose of studying local environmental contamination. 

Research on the breakdown and loss of radioactive isotopes in forest litter in cooperation with the University of Tennessee gave information on one step in the transfer of organic matter. Leaves were placed in nylon net bags in deciduous and conifer forests at Oak Ridge and in the nearby Great Smoky Mountains. 

Rates of leaf decay decreased with increasing altitude and with decreasing temperature for both deciduous and conifer forests; rates were higher for deciduous forest at all three altitudes. There was a wide variation between species in rate of leaf decay in one year (53 percent decay for mulberry, 34 percent for sugar maple, 36 percent and 34 percent for white and shumard oaks but only 18 percent for beech leaves indicating large difference in the release of nutrient elements recycled in the forest).

At Oak Ridge, both litter breakdown and release of radioactive isotopes are being followed in bags weighed and measured for radioactivity at weekly intervals. So far, the losses of strontium 85 and ruthenium 106, incorporated into leaves while on the trees, closely parallel losses of weight. This suggests that the measurements of physical breakdown of the leaf litter show approximately the rates at which radioisotopes are released from leaf litter into the soil. 

Fallout on Forest Foliage 

In eastern North America, forests accumulate a large fraction of the total fallout deposited in that area and provide a reservoir of dilute contamination which is being passed on slowly to animals and runnoff. In one forest study of the Oak Ridge National Laboratory ecology program, analyses of tree leaves by gamma ray spectrometry have compared the results of weapons fallout with effects of local stack discharges from reactors and isotope production plants.

For several square miles around Oak Ridge National Laboratory, iodine 131 was the highest contributor to gamma ray activity, but this decreased to low levels near the margin of the Oak Ridge Reservation, and could not be detected outside the reservation. Over most of the reservation, the levels of radioactivity of other isotopes did not exceed that of leaves collected in several states and Canada. 

These results show that careful standardization of tree foliage collection, so as to avoid differences which exist between species, can provide a practical basis for monitoring fallout patterns as a function of distance and direction from isolated sources. The pattern corresponds in many features with that predicted by electronic computer from standard turbulence theory, but emphasizes the importance of gravi-

tational settling near the source during calm and perhaps during rainy weather. 

Iodine 131 Accumulation in Mammals

Levels of radioisotopes deposited in the natural environment by activities at the National 

Reactor Testing Station generally are very low. 

Of prime interest is iodine 131, a thyroid gland seeker, and experiments have been conducted to determine quantatively the amount of iodine 

131 concentrated in the thyroid glands of wild living jack rabbits. Low levels of iodine 131 in the environment now are routinely monitored by measuring activity in these animals. 

FALLOUT STUDIES 

During 1960, studies of the mechanisms of radioactive fallout placed emphasis on the trends in fallout rates and related uptake of radioactivity in foods during a period when no large-scale weapons tests (French tests were not of this type) had not been conducted for more than a year. The fallout studies program was described in detail in the 1959 Annual Report, and periodic reports on fallout deposition, distribution, and uptake are sub mitted to the U.S. Public Health Service, Department of Health Education and Wel fare which regularly publishes interpretive summaries. 

Strontium 90 measurements on soil samples taken during 1959 from a large number of locations, with emphasis on the Northern Hemisphere, have been studied in order to obtain information on the rapid stratospheric fallout following far-northern nuclear tests in 1958.

These studies were performed by the Commis sion's Health and Safety Laboratory, New 

York, N.Y., the U.S. Department of Agriculture, and the U.S. Weather Bureau through cooperative arrangements. A comprehensive 

7 See pp. 239-266, Annual Report to Congress (January 

December 1959). 

report of research results by these three groups has been prepared for publication. A bibliography of fallout references is given in 

Appendix 1. 

Uptake Studies 

Uptake from soils. Studies made at the University of California at Los Angeles have shown that the uptake of cesium 137 by Ladino clover depends on soil type, and that the uptake increases as soil potassium is reduced by continued use of the soil to grow plants. Addition of stable cesium to the soils increased the uptake of radioactive cesium, but addition of potassium decreased uptake.

Other studies showed that some crop plants take up to 10 times as much strontium 90 as other plants on the same soil. For cereal crops, the grain itself showed concentrations of strontium 90 only about one-fifth the level of the leafy portion of the plants. Tops of potato plants contained about 50 times as much as the potatoes themselves. Temperature and light intensity were shown to affect strontium 90 uptake in barley and beans.

Studies of the uptake of stable and radioactive isotopes of cesium, potassium, strontium and calcium have shown that addition to the soil of the stable isotope of each mineral caused an increase in the uptake of the respective radioactive isotope. The stable isotopes appear to increase the water soluble fraction of the radioactive isotope in soils and clay minerals. 

Effect of fertilizers and additives. Studies at 

Hanford laboratories have shown that addition of as much as one ton of phosphorus per acre caused no reduction of strontium 90 uptake from acidic cinebar soil, but in calcareous (limerich) soils uptake was depressed two- to fourfold as a result of addition of phosphorus.

Although this provides a method of reducing 8 A preliminary report is included in the Health and Safety 

Laboratory Fallout Summary Report, HASL-SS, July 1, 1960. 

The final report will be published in Science.

uptake of strontium 90 from calcareous soils, the amount of phosphorus required is so great that the minor benefit gained would not warrant general use. The effect which phosphorus had on calcium uptake appeared to be comparable to that on strontium 90 uptake. Results of studies of strontium 90 movement in plants grown on different soils suggest that strontium 90 movement is impared when plants are grown in alkaline soils.

After irrigation with water containing zinc 65 a marked increase in uptake of this nuclide was observed only for grass grown where a sod was established. Presumably such a sod acts as an absorbing mechanism for taking the zinc 65 up into the plant material. 

In other studies, the uptake of cesium 137 was halved when potassium was increased 100fold in the nutrient solutions in which plants were grown, but was enhanced by the addition of nonradioactive cesium. Such increases in uptake from soil usually have been ascribed to changes in availability, but this would not appear to be the case with nutrient solution. The fact that it took a 100-fold increase in potassium to cut the uptake of cesium 137 to one-half its former value indicates that, with the increase in potassium, the plants change in their ability to discriminate between cesium and potassium. 

Consequently, the cesium 137-to-potassium ratio is of little value in predicting the future cesium contamination levels in plants that will result from known levels in soils.

Visible toxic signs in plants are produced where ratios of stable cesium to potassium are one or greater in the root substrate. These signs are typical of potassium deficiency, but analysis of plant tissues failed to show any decrease in potassium concentration. The results suggest that, in the metabolism of these two ions, there is a competitive mechanism which does not appear to exist for the absorption and translocation processes, since in that situation potassium and cesium seem to behave independently. In similar studies, an increase of two- to three-fold in potassium uptake in plant tissue depressed rubidium 86 concentration by five- to ten-fold. Thus cesium and potassium appear to be independently accumulated, whereas there occurs a partial interaction of potassium on rubidium uptake. The variability of the observed ratios for rubidium-topotassium and cesium-to-potassium demonstrates that neither rubidium nor cesium is an ideal tracer for potassium in plant uptake studies.

There was evidence that the roots and stems, in that order, exhibit a preference for strontium, and that there is a preferential translocation of calcium out of these tissues. This selective retention of strontium occurred only at low strontium-to-calcium substrate ratios. 

Bean plants' uptake of cesium 137 was greater when soil moisture was limited than when soil moisture was ample. Variations in available soil moisture did not influence uptake of calcium 

45 or strontium 90. 

Animals discrimination. Studies at Hanford show that the mature rat's fecal excretion of an intraperitoneally administered radioisotope is essentially the same for strontium and calcium, whereas urinary excretion is influenced by the calcium level in the diet. Retention of both strontium 90 and calcium 45 were dependent on dietary calcium-calcium 45 retention was influenced to a much greater extent than strontium 90. Discrimination between strontium and calcium is effected primarily in the process of absorption from the gastrointestinal tract. 

These results combine to give a picture of strontium metabolism which differs in significant details from the behavior of calcium. 

Uptake after detonations. A study of changes in availability of strontium 90 to plants, as a function of the environment in which nuclear tests have taken place, was completed in recent months by the Los Alamos Scientific Laboratory in collaboration with U.S. Department of 

Agriculture and Commission staff. To ascertain whether any of several materials lowered the availability to living plants of contained fission products, nuclear debris was examined in which

the principal component was, variously, sodium chloride from explosions at the ocean surface, ferric oxide from iron tower explosions and air bursts, aluminum oxide from aluminum tower explosions, and siliceous material from tests conducted in the presence of large amounts of silica, and calcium oxide from tests on coral islands.

The largest effect observed was a decrease, by approximately a factor of two, in strontium 90 availability from nuclear devices exploded on iron towers or exploded in air at altitudes high enough to avoid inclusion of ground debris.

Those materials from which strontium was found to be least available were iron oxide and aluminum oxide.

Removal of radioactivity. An ion exchange separation procedure was developed at Hanford laboratories for removing metabolized strontium 90 from sheep milk. The radiochemical yield is greater than 82 percent even in the presence of 100 times as much calcium as strontium. This study showed that metabolized strontium 90 can be removed from sheep milk by ion exchange techniques without the necessity of special pretreatment.

Studies in removal of radioactivity from certain foods also have continued at the University of Tennessee-Atomic Energy Commission 

Agricultural Research Laboratory.

It was found that when beef loin roasts are cooked with bones, strontium moves from the bone into the meat and drippings. Cesium 134, used as an artificial contaminant, has been removed from milk by an ion exchange resin. 

With use of a batch technique, 75 percent of cesium 134 was removed. With use of a column of resin about 99 percent was removed from the first 150 milliliters of milk passed through the column, and 95 percent was removed from the 

20th 10-milliliter portion. About 90 percent of iodine 131 has been removed from milk by batch techniques with a resin.

To study the effect on nutritive value of ion exchange-treated milk, homogenized, vitamin D fortified milk was treated with Dowex 50-W in the calcium form by the batch technique using a milk-to-resin ratio of 20 to 1. Both the treated milk and the control were fortified with copper and iron and fed to weanling rats as the sole source of nutrients. Failure to achieve normal weight gain was noted for the males but not for the females. No impairment of male or female fertility was noted at the end of 8 weeks.

Interpretive Studies 

An extensive interpretive review and study of fallout problems involving short-lived radioactivity and so called "hot spots" resulting from past nuclear tests was completed in May 1960, by the Fallout Studies Branch of the Division of Biology and Medcine. The entire report of this study will be issued by the Office of Technical Information as AEC report TID-8527 and made available from the Office of Technical 

Services, U.S. Department of Commerce, Washington 25, D.C.

In addition to studies of fallout from weapons tests, other studies are underway on environmental radioactivity from various uses of atomic energy. Among these are a study of the possible environmental contamination that might result should projected future programs of nuclear power for aircraft and space vehicles be carried out, and a study undertaken at the request of the Congressional Joint Committee on Atomic Energy, of the implications of fallout on the basis of certain hypothetical schedules of weapons tests. The continuing measurement of fallout and natural radioactivity in the stratosphere, lower atmosphere, rain, soil, foods and man is providing an improved experimental base upon which to develop prediction models for the evaluation of the possible radiation effects of these and other proposals for the peaceful and military applications of atomic energy. 

"The Contribution of Short Lived Isotopes and Hot Spots to Radiation Exposure in the United States from Nuclear Test 

Fallout," June 6, 1960. Prepared in response to inquiries by the Joint Congressional Committee on Atomic Energy.

3Radiation Detection Instruments Improved Counting Methods 

An improved device for measuring radioactive contamination was developed at Hanford. A sodium iodide scintillation crystal, 93% inches in diameter, and 8½ inches thick with a 3-inch diameter well, is employed. The increased sensitivity and counting 10 efficiency of this detector is sufficient to allow direct analyses of many radioactive contaminants directly from a 500 milliliter sample of water (about a pint) placed in the well. Increased precision and accuracy and shorter analysis times were obtained over present methods which involve concentrating gallon samples and plating them prior to counting. A further advantage of well crystal counting is its ability to measure the presence of arsenic 76, scandium 46, and copper 64 directly without the separate beta absorption and coincidence counting technique required in present counting methods. 

A factor of 20 to 25 increase in sensitivity for gamma-emitting radioisotopes is obtained in vegetation analyses using a 500 ml plastic bottle full of vegetation counted on a simple 3-inch crystal. This increased efficiency also makes this apparatus more suitable for counting crop samples. 

10 "Counting" is a term used to describe the determining of the amount of radioactivity in a sample by using a device to count the number of radioactive disintegrations per unit of time. 

Personal Radiation Monitor

The development of a miniature instrument for detecting gamma radiation, small enough to be worn in a shirt pocket has been completed recently at the Oak Ridge National Laboratory. The instrument, officially designated as the Personal Radiation Monitor or PRM, is the size and shape of a fountain pen. Clipped in the pocket of workers in atomic installations, it warns them of unexpected radiation by means of a flashing light and by an audible "screamer" alarm. The pitch and loudness of the alarm rise in proportion to the radiation level. By this means the worker can tell if he is entering or leaving a danger area. He can also obtain a quantitative measure of the radiation level by timing the flashing rate of the device. 

In the interest of safety, no on-off switch is provided. Continuous operation for over a month is provided by a small mercury battery at a cost of about two cents a day. Temperatures from 0° to 120° F can be tolerated. 

Atmospheric Studies 

Special purpose instrumentation was developed and placed in use at Hanford Works to measure particle dispersion for atmospheric physics studies, and a complete, 20-data-station telemetry system was completed for use in obtaining plant-wide atmospheric physics and radiological information. 573517 0-61 75

The Commission's program of physical science research is directed primarily toward determining the natural laws of the physical world relevant to development, use, and control of nuclear energy.

In addition to sponsoring research in the Commission's national laboratories and research centers (see Appendix 2 for list), and under contract at institutions of learning and research, the Commission contributes to the design and construction of complex machines and other facilities required for research in nonprofit institutions. The most costly facilities center around high energy particle accelerators, the so-called "atom smashers", and nuclear reactors that produce high concentrations of neutrons some of which are used to manufacture isotopes useful in research.
