NUCLEAR FORCES 

Research to determine the nature of the forces that operate inside the atomic nucleus has been uppermost in the programs of many scientists

Meson Tracks. A "K" meson (heavy line), produced in the Cosmotron at Brookhaven National Laboratory, comes to rest in emulsion and decays into a positively-charged light meson (dotted continuation of heavy line). 

The properties of the light meson were accurately measured, showing that the K meson decayed into one charged pi meson and one neutral pi meson. The energies of these pi mesons were also accurately determined, proving that the decay pattern of the charged K meson is similar to that of the neutral K meson, differing only in the charges of the pi mesons produced. since, in the second decade of this century, Rutherford discovered the atomic nucleus. 

Full development and use of atomic resources depends on understanding these forces. The present state of progress in use of atomic energy is based on partial understanding, on experimental results, and on incomplete theory. 

Progress in evolving theory, and in comparing theory with experimental results, has been slow. 

Among the important contributions to this progress was the publication in 1935 of the famous meson hypothesis of Hideki Yukawa. 

He postulated, as a factor in the forces that bind together the nucleus, the existence of a particle with a mass intermediate between the light particle, the electron, and the heavier particle, the proton, and called this particle the mesotron; later it was called the meson, from the Greek prefix, meso, meaning intermediate. 

Physicists long have been seeking to discover and identify the nuclear forces on which Yukawa's hypothesis is founded. The existence of mesons with differing charges and mass has been confirmed, and the pi-meson with a mass some 270 times that of the electron, appears to be the "glue" of the nucleus. Much effort has

gone into development of meson theory, and certain experimental discoveries have lent support to fundamental aspects of the theory. 

Very rough approximate solutions of equations appear to agree with experimental knowledge of nuclear forces at distances between particles which are relatively large in nuclear termsseveral times the radius of an atomic nucleusbut, at shorter distances, experiment disagrees with theory. So far, physicists have not been able to determine whether this disagreement denotes faults in the experiments or in the basic equations themselves.

To resolve this uncertainty, research projects necessarily follow the traditional approach of building on experimental data. Special emphasis is placed on measuring the scattering from a beam of nucleons on a target in which the same nucleon predominates-for example, directing a proton beam at a hydrogen targetthe hydrogen nucleus containing a single proton. The attempt is to delineate the features of nuclear forces which any meson theory that is successful will have to account for quantitatively. Such work has demonstrated, for example, that the range in which nuclear forces act-the distance beyond which the force falls to insignificant strengths-is very short even on an atomic scale. It is of the order of one fermi (10-13 cm) or roughly equivalent to the radius of an atomic nucleus. This is only one of many necessary clues.

In order to refine nuclear theories, experimental data are needed over a wide range of nuclear energies, giving information on the interactions between nucleons (a) in different charge states (between protons, between neutrons and protons, and between neutrons), (b) in different spin states (direction of spin parallel or antiparallel), (c) in different angular momentum states (rates of rotation called Swaves, P-waves, D-waves, etc.), and (d) in states for which the polarity of spin is oriented in several possible ways with respect to the angular momentum of the orbital motion of nucleons within the nucleus.11 The problem is enormously complicated, since nature has created a nuclear force which can vary with almost every possible aspect of a two-nucleon system. 

Scientists at the University of Rochester have found a way of accounting theoretically for an enormous variety of data, including the results of almost all reliable experiments so far performed. The range of energy covered in the investigation goes from zero to about 300 Mev (million electron volts). Energies above 300

Mev are not typical of nucleons within the nucleus, and experiments using nucleons at a higher energy are likely to produce new particles that add to the difficulty of interpreting data on ordinary nuclear forces. 

Supplementing theoretical work, experimenters at the Rochester synchrocyclotron have undertaken a broad program of studies in nucleon-nucleon scattering. Early work in this field was confined to "single scattering", using a single target for the accelerator beam, in which the results of one nucleon striking another is recorded by measuring the probability of scattering in a given direction. In 1953, it was discovered that beams of nucleons can be strongly polarized by such a scattering. They leave the target with the axes of their spins pointing predominantly in one direction. This led to studies of many details of nuclear forces by "double scattering”—actually using two successive targets in which a second scattering acted as an analyzer for the polarization of nucleons produced in scattering by the first target. 

Recently, techniques have been developed at 

Lawrence Radiation Laboratory, Berkley, for triple scattering, using three successive targets for nucleons scattered by a preceding target. 

This program of studies on nuclear forces is world-wide and the aims and techniques of all groups supplement one another. With very few exceptions, the experimental data from various laboratories can be compared. In the theoretical analysis, the University of Rochester 11 The energy levels of protons and neutrons (nucleons) within the nucleus have many correspondences with those of electrons outside the nucleus, and may be conceived of as involving both individual and composite "spin", and so-called orbital movement within the nucleus.

investigators are collaborating with scientists at Los Alamos Scientific Laboratory, which is another major contributor. The variety of energies produced by accelerators at various laboratories here and abroad provides a valuable check against predictions based on the nuclear models delineated by experimental results.

Much experimental work already has been accomplished, and much is in progress. The results have been qualitatively, and sometimes quantitatively fitted into an empirical theory in which a group of potentials (rather than a single nuclear "force") is assigned to the several states of the nucleon system. The interaction between two protons found in this way is rather complex and shows two kinds of central force, one for each possible relative orientation of the spin polarity of the reacting nucleons. In addition, two kinds of non-central forces have been identified, depending on the relative orientation of the spin and the orbital angular momenta. One simplifying fact, however, has emerged. So far, it appears from the analysis of all experiments that the nuclear forces between two nucleons are independent of the electrostatic charge on the respective nucleons. 

Thus the nuclear force between two neutrons, or between a neutron (no charge) and a proton (positive charge), is identical with the force between two protons, provided all pairs are examined in identical states of spin and angular momentum.

Nuclear Structure 

In fundamental studies of nuclei, use is made of radioactive decay of nuclei to provide information about the characteristics of both parent and daughter nuclei. Continuing its investigations of the nuclear properties of heavier elements, Argonne National Laboratory studied the radiations from the 7,300-year half-life alpha emitter, thorium 229, to determine the nuclear levels in the decay daughter, radium 

225. Nine levels of energy of radium 225 nuclei have been deduced from the alpha spectrum. The ground-state alpha transition has not been observed and must be rare. An examination of the gamma spectrum with scintillation and proportional counters, and of the conversion electron spectrum with a two-lens beta spectrometer, has shown the presence of at least seven associated gamma transitions.

The characteristic energy levels of a nucleus occupy a central role in both experimental and theoretical studies to determine the details of nuclear structure, just as work in atomic spectroscopy was concerned with the structure and energy levels of electron orbits about nuclei and gave rise to the modern theory of quantum mechanics. The structure and energy levels within nuclei are quite complex, but it is hoped that ultimately they may be explained in detail. 

At low energies of excitation, the levels of various energy states are relatively few and far apart, and it is feasible to describe each individual state. These states are explored by beta decay, alpha decay, Coulomb (inter-nucleon electrostatic) excitation, etc., aand may be interpreted in terms of single particles and/or collective models. 

As the excitation energy of a nucleus is increased, the nuclear levels become more numerous and they draw closer together. At excitation energy of about 6 Mev (corresponding to the excitation energy added by capture of a thermal neutron), the average spacing between energy levels is about 10 electron volts.

When a nucleus captures a neutron, the resulting compound nucleus is formed in a highly excited, usually unstable, state. The excitation energy is equal to the binding energy 12 of the neutron plus the initial kinetic energy of the captured neutron. The nucleus then gives up this excitation energy by such processes as scattering (reemission of the neutron), fission, or gamma radiation, and ends up at lower energy states. By observing the corresponding energies and steps by which the nucleus becomes more stable, information is obtained as to the char- 13 Nuclear binding energy may be defined as the energy equivalent of the difference in mass between an atom and the sum of the component particles in the atom (electrons, protons, neutrons). It is also the energy required to remove a particle from the atom.

acteristics of the unstable nucleus and the various nuclear energy states. 

Capture of a neutron is most probable when its kinetic energy corresponds to one of the excited levels of the resulting compound nucleus. At these energies occur the peaks (called resonances) in the graph of total cross section as a function of neutron energy. Measurements of this general type are made with crystal and fast chopper neutron spectrometers on neutron beams from reactors, and with pulsed particle accelerator neutron sources. 

Although many experiments have measured the probabilities of the different modes of discharging excitation energy caused by resonant capture, no direct experimental information has been obtained on what fractions of the transitions from a particular excited state result in which particular states of lower energy. Research at Argonne National Laboratory, Brookhaven National Laboratory, the Materials 

Testing Reactor, Oak Ridge National Labora tory, and several universities is directed toward this problem.

In an experiment at Argonne with manganese 55, the excited state of the compound nucleus was selected by using a fast neutron choppera fast acting shutter interrupting a neutron beam to separate neutrons on the basis of their velocities. After neutron capture, the nucleus gave up its excitation energy by a very complex cascade of gamma rays in the course of dropping to successively lower energy levels. The technical difficulty of distinguishing between the many different gamma rays presented a serious obstacle to measuring the relative probability of particular transitions from the initial state. By applying coincidence techniques to the study of these gamma-ray cascades so that high-energy gamma rays are counted only if emitted almost simultaneously with a particular low-energy one-this difficulty was overcome enough to allow observation of transitions to the first few excited states.

The marked difference between the gammaray spectra resulting from the capture of thermal (0.025 ev) as contrasted with 337 ev neutrons found for manganese 55 had not been anticipated. This difference suggests that the thermal cross section is strongly influenced by a low-energy resonance and not only by the 337 ev resonance, as had previously seemed probable. The spectra for the resonances at 1,080 and 

2,360 ev also differed from each other in a marked way, even though the compound nucleus resulting from neutron capture has the same spin state in both cases.

Similar studies have been made at Argonne with mercury 199, platinum 195, and tungsten 183 as target nuclides. In each of the nuclides studied, the results showed that excited states with the same spin and almost the same energy have quite different probabilities for transitions of the excited nucleus to particular low-energy levels. 

At the Materials Testing Reactor (MTR), crystal spectrometer studies of the relative production of a metastable state and the ground state following neutron capture, for neutrons having energies corresponding to different resonances, are being made. 

The high intensity of the neutron beams from the MTR have made possible studies of the characteristics of particular excited levels formed by the addition of a low energy neutron to a stable fissionable nuclei. It has been found that the relative number of particular fission product nuclei produced can vary significantly with very small changes in neutron energy. 

Also, some progress is being made toward understanding the fission process and what it can reveal about nuclear deformations by studying in detail the asymmetries and interferences found in fission resonances.

An important mode of energy release from an excited nucleus is the emission of electromagnetic radiation, such as gamma rays. An alternative mode is the interaction of the nucleus with one of its own surrounding electrons, and the subsequent ejection of an electron with a kinetic energy equivalent to the energy of a gamma transition minus the binding energy of the electron. This process is called internal gamma ray conversion. The

relative probability of the emission of conversion electrons and of gamma ray photons is very precisely related to the type of gamma radiation being emitted, i.e., the multipolarity of the gamma transition. In the great majority of cases there is a close agreement of experimentally determined ratios with those computed from theory. 

Recently, some striking differences between experiment and theory have been found at 

Lawrence Radiation Laboratory, Berkeley, in a special group of gamma transitions occurring in the decay of isotopes of very heavy elements. 

These transitions are of electric dipole type. 

In addition to the conversion electron anomaly, these transitions also show transition probabilities which are orders of magnitude less than the usual probabilities for the electric dipole type. In these elements the most tightly bound electrons spend appreciable parts of their orbital trajectories within the nuclear volume. 

Ordinarily this nuclear penetration does not affect the conversion process significantly, but for special reasons the effect of the nuclear penetration is crucial in the case of this special class of delayed transitions. The importance of this discovery is that detailed alternate theories of the internal structure of nuclei will give greatly different predictions for the conversion anomalies. Hence the conversion electrons can be used to select between these alternate choices. 

Measurements of Secondary Gammas 

Neutrons from the thermal column of the 

Omega West Reactor at Los Alamos Scientific 

Laboratory have been used to produce gamma rays that follow neutron capture for a new series of measurements, made possible by a recently completed Compton spectrometer of unique design, on which construction began in 

1957. The gamma ray spectrum from thermal capture in natural titanium has been measured from 1.3 Mev to 7.6 Mev. 

A series of nuclear experiments recently completed at Lawrence Radiation Laboratory, Livermore, using a fast-coincidence scintillation spectrometer, has contributed valuable information about the atomic nucleus. The experiments were performed by irradiating the rare isotope vanadium 50 with thermal neutrons, and analyzing the gamma ray spectrum emitted from the highly excited vanadium 51 nucleus created by capture of a neutron. 

Vanadium exists almost monoistopically in nature as 99.76 percent vanadium 51 and only 

0.24 percent vanadium 50. The experiments were conducted with a sample, supplied by Oak 

Ridge National Laboratory, enriched to 44.1 percent of vanadium 50 which represented essentially the entire free world's supply of the element at this enrichment. The binding energy of the added neutron in vanadium 51 was measured by a technique in which the energies of gamma rays that were related in cascade were added electronically.

A decay scheme for the de-excitation of vanadium 51, from the capture state to the ground state, was established by detecting cascade radiations in various fast-coincidence experiments including a recently employed sum-coincidence method whereby only double cascades with radiations having energies that add up to the binding energy are recorded. These studies revealed nuclear energy levels in vanadium 51 with extremely high angular momentum.

Study of the decay scheme allowed spin assignments to be made for most of the energy states in vanadium 51 below 4 Mev. An analysis of the experimental observations reveals that nuclear forces in the vanadium 51 nucleus behave as though a weak coupling exists between the inner nuclear core surface and outer orbital protons of the shell model. The experiments are providing additional nuclear data which will be of value in expanding nuclear theories. 

Other experiments were conducted in which a thermal neutron was added to the nucleus of vanadium 51 to make vanadium 52 and thus determine the nuclear changes introduced by coupling an additional neutron to the vanadium 51 core. Again a decay scheme was established and details of low-level transitions were ob-

served. Some evidence suggests that the lowlevel vanadium 51 and vanadium 52 states are related since these vanadium 52 states may arise from the same core protons which are responsible for the vanadium 51 low-level excitations. 

Americium 242 Isomer Decay Pattern 

A long-standing puzzle concerning the existence of two isomeric forms of an isotope of the transuranium element americium has been solved at Lawrence Radiation Laboratory, Berkeley. Isomeric forms are nuclear species with identical neutron and proton content but with distinctly different probabilities for radioactive decay. One form of americium 242 has a half-life of 16 hours; that of another is 152 years. The difference in the decay probabilities is linked to a difference in the amount of intrinsic angular momentum (rate of rotation) in the isomeric forms. 

Previous difficulties in understanding the properties of these isomers have been cleared up by showing that the spin content of the isomer decaying with a 16-hour half-life is one unit, and that decay of the 152-year form produces the 16-hour form by emission of a gamma ray which carries away 4 units of angular momentum. This fixes the spin of the 152year isomer as 5. These spin values can be correlated with detailed information on the structure of nuclei in the transuranium element group of isotopes. This work has provided new insights into strucure of nuclei.

Interactions of Light Nuclei 

Studies of energy levels and interactions of light nuclei have been carried out with various particle accelerators at Los Alamos Scientific Laboratory. Energy levels of nuclei ranging from hydrogen to oxygen 20 have been measured. 

Total cross sections of helium 3 and helium 4 are being investigated, initial measurements being made at an incident neutron energy of 22.5 Mev. A systematic summary of all available data on few-nucleon interactions has been made.

A full understanding of the features of the "optical model potential" (which describes the properties of nuclei in general) requires measurements with polarized particles, i.e., particles whose spin orientation is known. By use of a scattering process it has been possible to make sufficient numbers of about 10 Mev polarized protons to conduct scattering experiments on boron 10, magnesium, sulfur, calcium, vanadium, manganese, and other nuclides. These experiments have been useful in elucidating that part of the nuclear interaction involving spin and orbital angular momentum.

Binding Energy of the Deuteron ess. 

The energy that binds together the deuterium (heavy hydrogen) nucleus has been measured repeatedly in the past since the mass of the neutron may be measured by this proc- 

One of the most direct ways to measure the binding energy is to measure the energy of the gamma ray resulting from neutronproton capture. Scientists at Lawrence Laboratory, Livermore, are using a bent quartz crystal spectrometer to diffract gamma rays resulting from neutron capture by the hydrogen in a polyethylene target in the Livermore Pool Reactor. This method offers high absolute precision. The gamma ray has been successfully diffracted and preliminary results indicate that the experiment is capable of yielding significant new information on the binding energy of the deuteron.

Resonant Absorption of Gamma Rays When the gamma ray emitted by an excited nucleus results from transition to the ground state of energy of the nucleus, the radiation should be capable of exciting another similar nucleus to the same excited state. This is the analogue of resonance radiation, in which atoms selectively absorb the radiation of other atoms of the same species.

Spectrometer Assembly. An Argonne National Laboratory technician assembles a bent crystal gamma ray spectrometer which will be used in studies of the binding energy of the deuteron. The use of this type of spectrometer has furnished a means of attaining high absolute precision in gamma ray diffraction and is capable of yielding significant new knowledge on the deuteron's binding energy. 

Some interesting experiments involving resonance absorption have been performed recently at Argonne National Laboratory, Los 

Alamos Scientific Laboratory, Brookhaven 

National Laboratory and in several other laboratories. The experiments make use of the so-called "Mössbauer Effect" (see below) as a very sensitive detector for small shifts in radiation energy, and, therefore, also for small shifts in frequency and wavelength. 

Some of these detectors apparently may be sensitive enough to measure, under laboratory conditions, the "gravitational red shift" predicted by Einstein's Theory of Relativity.

The theory of the "red shift" predicts that the frequency of a particular line emitted by an atom or nucleus should vary slightly with the position of this atom or nucleus in a gravitational field. In particular, the visible light emitted by a given atom would be at a slightly

lower frequency (that is, redder) if the atom were near the surface of the Earth than if it were at a higher elevation. Astronomers have been searching for evidence for this red shift in the spectral lines observed in light from the sun and other stars that have very strong gravitational fields. While many observations show a shift of some sort the results are not at all clear cut. It is generally accepted today that since there are many effects other than gravitation which can give rise to stellar spectrum shifts, the evidence for a gravitational red shift from astronomical measurements is ambiguous. 

R. L. Mössbauer, a German physicist, found that if an excited nucleus which decays by gamma ray emission is embedded in a solid, it can give up its recoil momentum to the entire solid. Therefore, the emitted gamma ray will lose very little energy due to recoil and will thus have essentially the same energy as that of the excited state. Similarly, if the absorbing nucleus also is embedded in a solid, the recoil momentum given up by the gamma ray in being absorbed is taken by the entire solid. This means that the absorption cross section will be very large, and that it is necessary to give the source only a small velocity relative to the absorber to destroy the resonance effect. In the case of iron 57, a velocity of a few millimeters per second is sufficient to shift the frequency of the emission line-by the Doppler effectso that absorption no longer occurs. 

Experiments are underway at Harvard University and at the Atomic Energy Research 

Establishment, Harwell, England, to measure the resonance absorption with the source and absorber at different heights from each other. 

Preliminary results indicate that there is an effect, even though the quantitative data still are insufficient to provide a conclusively sensitive test of the gravitational red shift. 

Nuclear Magnetic Resonance 

A new technique, nuclear magnetic resonance (NMR) has been tested at Argonne National Laboratory with lithium 8 nuclei, an unstable species of 0.8 seconds half-life. Nuclear magnetic resonance, observed previously only with stable or very long-lived nuclei, is a well-known phenomenon which can be used for the precise measurement of the magnetic strength of different nuclear species, as well as for the study of a wide variety of chemical and solid-state phenomena. In the conventional NMR technique, the response of the nucleus to a highfrequency magnetic field is detected by its effect on a sensitive electronic circuit. Because the nuclear magnetism is feeble, a large number of nuclei must contribute to produce a detectable effect, and samples of about a gram are required. 

In the case of short-lived nuclei, samples of this size would be extremely radioactive even if it were possible to produce them. 

The new method takes advantage of a sample's radioactivity by exploiting the recent discovery of the "nonconservation of parity" in beta decay.13 The experiment which first demonstrated parity nonconservation showed that beta particles are not emitted by decaying nuclei in all directions with equal probability. 

Rather, there is a preferred direction of emission. If the nucleus is thought of as a small spinning globe like the earth and the preferred direction as the North Pole, most of the betas are emitted in the northern hemisphere. If a sample of nuclei is polarized so that most of the nuclear "north poles" point in the same direction, the asymmetry becomes easily detectable by selective placing of beta counters. 

A detectable degree of nuclear polarization normally is extremely difficult to achieve, the standard technique requiring that the sample be cooled almost to absolute zero-a procedure that requires much time. Nuclei whose lifetimes are short could not be polarized in this manner. Another method of nuclear polarization applicable to a number of nuclei, is to reflect slow neutrons from a surface as light is reflected by a mirror. If the "mirror" is made of cobalt or certain cobalt-iron alloys and is 

13 See pp. 91-12, Twenty-second Semiannual Report to Congress (January-June 1957).

magnetized, the reflected neutrons are completely polarized. When such neutrons are captured by stable nuclei, the resulting nuclei are themselves polarized to an appreciable degree and, if they decay by beta emission, the asymmetry may be observed. Observation of the decay of a few thousand atoms is sufficient to establish the asymmetry, requiring a sample of only 10-19 gram (one-ten billion-billionth of a gram), compared to one gram required for conventional techniques. 

The effects due to an applied radiofrequency magnetic field appear as changes in the asymmetry of beta decay. Typically, as in the usual 

NMR experiment, no effect is seen unless the applied frequency is almost exactly equal to a particular value, the resonance or Larmor frequency. At the Larmor frequency, the asymmetry disappears. If the applied frequency is varied until the asymmetry is observed to vanish, the Larmor frequency is determined and from this the magnetic dipole moment of the nucleus may be calculated. 

Knowledge of nuclear moments is of considerable importance for the development of nuclear theory because these data provide one of the most convenient tests of the predictions of particular theories. In the case of lithium 8, the experimental value disagreed seriously with the prediction of each of the two simplest theories, but agreed well with the result of a more sophisticated theory.

It should be possible to measure the magnetic moments of a number of other short-lived nuclei with the new technique which may also lend itself to some novel and interesting experiments in solid state physics.

Beta Decay Studies 

Longitudinal polarization of electrons and positrons emitted in the beta decay of nuclei has been studied during the past 3 years as an aid in the investigation of the beta decay interaction. The longitudinal polarization exists as a consequence of the violation of space inversion symmetry discovered in the beta decay process of cobalt 60 in 1957.

Measurements of the polarization for the electron emitters cobalt 60, cesium 137, and promethium 147 have been pursued at Yale University, using the asymmetry that is produced in intensity of scattering when transversely polarized electrons impinge on nuclei of high atomic number ("Mott scattering"). These experiments have shown that the accuracy with which polarization can be measured by this technique is critically dependent upon proper evaluation of systematic errors associated with this experimental method. A systematic study was made of the effects of multiple and plural scattering in the target depolarization in the source material, the inherent geometric asymmetries of the apparatus, and several other depolarization effects.

Two beta decay precision experiments have been undertaken at Oak Ridge National Laboratory. In the first, theory predicts that the beta rays emitted from radioactive nuclei will have their spins parallel with the direction of motion (for positrons), or anti-parallel to the direction of motion (for electrons). This alignment will be proportional to the velocity of the betas, and should approach completeness as the velocity of the particle approaches the velocity of light. Results obtained on phosphorus 32 as an emitter show that this prediction holds true to an accuracy of better than 1.5 percent.

The second experiment involves the intensity relationship between beta ray and neutrino as a function of the angle between them in beta decay of nuclei. Since the neutrino has no charge, and very small rest mass, this experiment is usually carried out by observing the energy spectrum of the recoiling nuclei. In the decay of helium 6 the shape of this spectrum is quite different for the two possible forms of the beta interaction. This experiment is still in progress, but clearly agrees with theory in that the axial-vector interaction is dominant. Final results are expected to have a precision of a few percent.

JAR JUR JAR JUE JAR JUR DAN 

Data Collector. A 400-channel pulse-height analyzer, a magnetic memory, and a readout system are used in connection with Oak Ridge National Laboratory's 86-inch cyclotron for collecting and analyzing nuclear reaction data. The recently-developed particle selective system is used to record simultaneously the proton, deuteron, triton, and alpha-particle spectra from proton-induced reactions. The readout system produces data in a punched-card form which is then analyzed in a high-speed digital computer. 

All these beta decay experiments are in agreement with a universal theory of weak interactions first developed by scientists at the 

University of Rochester and at the California

Institute of Technology in 1957. This V-A 

(V stands for vector, A for axial vector) theory has been remarkably successful in explaining not only all beta ray experiments but also many weak interaction processes. The V-A theory predicted the incorrectness of certain earlier experiments (among them, an earlier measurement of the angular correlation between the neutrino and electron from helium 6) and all these predictions subsequently were verified by more refined experiments. It appears that the beta decay interaction is finally defined. 

Nuclear Model Studies 

Nuclear reaction studies show that energetic protons striking atomic nuclei can pick up single neutrons or pairs of neutrons from the nuclei to form deuterons or tritons. The 22 

Mev protons from the Oak Ridge National Laboratory 86-inch cyclotron are especially well suited for studying such pickup reactions. 

They travel fast enough to approach positively

Scattering Chamber. Scientists at Oak Ridge National Laboratory use this scattering chamber to determine the energy of heavy particles as they are deflected out of a specially built 63-inch heavy particle cyclotron. The deflected beam of nitrogen (N³+) ions enters the chamber from the bottom and strikes a target at the center. A counter placed at various angles detects and measures the energy of particles produced by nuclear reactions in the target. Two counters may be used to detect two particles coincident from a single nuclear event. charged nuclei and pull out tightly bound neutrons, yet are not fast enough to pass through nuclei without interacting. Pickups occur with little disturbance to the remainder of the target nucleons.

The Oak Ridge experiments show that a nuclear shell model, such as accounts for many nuclear properties, provides a good description for pickup reactions. In this model the neutrons and protons making up the nucleus are pictured as being contained in certain quantum mechanical bound states or "shells", similar to those governing orbital electrons outside the nucleus. Each shell can hold a definite quota of neutrons or protons. When a proton picks up one or two neutrons from a shell, the energy of the resultant deuteron or triton indicates the characteristic binding energy of the shell; the angle at which the resultant particle comes out serves to identify the depleated shell. 

Most recent Oak Ridge results for iron isotopes show that the simplest form of the shell model, in which nuclei are pictured as being spherical, is not an adequate description. Instead, it appears that the presence of an incompletely filled intra nuclear shell causes the whole nucleus even the filled shells to be distorted from spherical shape. According to calculations of shell energies in flattened and elongated nuclei made in Copenhagen a few years ago this distortion has the effect, for example, of breaking up the seventh shell in iron, which can hold eight neutrons, into four shells, each of which can hold only two neutrons. The new data are in qualitative agreement with the Copenhagen calculations and show shifts in the shell energies consistent with the picture of nuclear distortion caused when neutrons enter incompletely filled shells. 

Heavy Particle Physics 

The heavy particle physics program at Oak Ridge National Laboratory uses high-energy nitrogen ions to investigate both the structure of light nuclei and also the mechanisms by which reactions proceed between complex nuclei. A specially designed cyclotron accelerates nitrogen ions to 28 Mev, at which point the beam is deflected out of the cyclotron for experimental use.

Research performed with nitrogen ions has yielded information about nuclear sizes, the detailed structure of the nucleus surface, and

the behavior of highly energetic nuclear matter. Several reaction mechanisms also have been elucidated.

In some cases, it was found that as two nuclei approach one another, small fragments are torn from one and transferred to the other. In these reactions, the nature of the two nuclei is not drastically changed. The interaction takes place at much larger nuclear distances than previously observed.

In other reactions, the two nuclei are brought into more intimate contact and coalesce to form a compound system which then discharges light particles, such as neutrons. The final result of this reaction is a fusion of two nuclei to form a very different nucleus. Its mass is slightly less than the sum of the masses of the reacting nuclei.

Reactions Induced by Fast Neutrons Particle accelerators of several types are used at the Los Alamos Scientific Laboratory to bombard materials with fast neutrons to reveal the structure and behavior of the atomic nucleus. During the past 12 months, the spectra of inelastically scattered neutrons have been measured for more than 20 elements at an incident energy of 7 Mev. For some of the same elements, and for many others, neutron scattering spectra have been observed also for lower energies, and for both elastic and inelastic scattering.

These measurements lead to an understanding of the properties of nuclei in terms of a parameter which may be interpreted as a "temperature", analogous to the ordinary concept of temperature in a gas or liquid. For the first time these data cover nearly the whole range of nuclear species. Temperature values are being established.

Neutron-deficient Isotopes 

The high-intensity 22 Mev proton beam of the Oak Ridge National Laboratory 86-inch cyclotron is used to produce relatively small quantities of a wide variety of neutron-deficient isotopes which are of considerable importance in basic research. At 22 Mev the cross sections for many reactions become large enough to make isotope production efficient, especially since a beam of up to 3 milliamperes is available. The proton-induced reactions result in products of high isotopic purity and high specific activity. Some 30 isotopes are regularly produced, and the techniques for produc- 

Particle Accelerator. 

The 9,500,000 electron volt Van de Graaff particle accelerator at Los Alamos Scientific Laboratory is one of the largest and most powerful 

Van de Graaff machines in the world. Photo shows the separation column portion of the accelerator with its pressure vessel removed. The particle accelerator is used to bombard materials with fast neutrons to obtain knowledge of the structure and behavior of the atomic nucleus.

tion of several other uniquely important isotopes, such as calcium 47, are being investigated. 

This cyclotron is the only source for several of these isotopes. For example, arsenic 74 is regularly produced in 100-millicurie quantities for diagnostic tests in cancer research. Recent applications of the Mössbauer effect to laboratory-scale investigations of the theory of relativity, as described earlier, are made possible by the production of cobalt 57 on this cyclotron. Cobalt 57 is being considered as a substitute for cobalt 60 in labeling vitamins for metabolism studies.

New Iron-free Spectrometer 

A new double electron coincidence beta-ray spectrometer is being developed at Argonne National Laboratory. This instrument should assist in work involving nuclear spectroscopy by permitting more precise determination of energy levels in atomic nuclei, by furnishing information about spins and parities in nuclear states, and by yielding knowledge of the progression of events in the decay of excited nuclear states.

The fact that it is constructed of iron-free materials, and thus is free from the problems created by ferromagnetic material, means that the spectrometer can be used for high-precision measurements depending essentially on the precision of measuring the current in the coils and on the resolution of the instrument.

Since the instrument is sensitive to even very slight extraneous magnetic fields, it is housed in a specially constructed iron-free building. 

A 20-foot cubical degaussing coil array surrounding the instrument enables cancellation of the earth's magnetic field to a negligible value. Astrophysics Evidence has been obtained in recent research that supports a theory concerning the source of energy a class of stars called "red giants."

Stars in early stages of evolution are composed mainly of hydrogen and their energy is believed to come from fusion of hydrogen in thermonuclear reactions leading to the production of helium. When most of the hydrogen has been consumed, however, certain stars called "red giants" continue to glow.

A British physicist in 1954 suggested that the energy of these red stars could come from fusion reactions of helium to form stable carbon 12 atoms. A research team at California Institute of Technology was able to produce carbon in an unstable form and observe the reverse process, its breakup into three helium atoms.

Attempts made by a number of investigators, however, failed to establish that the unstable form of carbon 12 could change to stable carbon. 

On theoretical considerations, it was predicted that, if unstable carbon 12 could change to stable carbon 12 it would occur by the emission of an electron pair with 7.6 Mev of energy.

To test this concept, use was made of the 5.5 

Mev Van de Graaff accelerator at Oak Ridge National Laboratory and a magnetic pair spectrometer designed and built at Brookhaven National Laboratory and transported to Oak 

Ridge. Beryllium targets were bombarded with a beam of alpha particles, producing unstable carbon 12 atoms in 7.65 Mev levels. In all the runs, the 7.65 Mev electron pair line was observed with approximately the same intensity relative to the 4.43 Mev electron pair conversion line known to identify a specific excited state of carbon 12. Calculations indicate that one in every 200,000 carbon 12 atoms in a second specific excited state (7.65 Mev) changes into stable carbon 12. The remainder breakup into three helium atoms which, in red giant stars, then can recombine into new unstable carbon atoms, some of which will become stable, and some break down into helium in a continuously repeating reaction. Because of the enormous number of helium atoms in red giant stars, it is believed that sufficient burning reactions of this type can take place to account for the heat and light radiated from these stars.

Cosmotron Control Room. View from the control room of the Cosmotron at Brookhaven National Laboratory. 

Through the window can be seen the 65-foot diameter, 2,200-ton magnet, covered by a white plastic "cocoon". 

The Cosmotron, which began operation in 1952, was the world's first billion plus electron volt accelerator. 

The weakness and infrequency of the transition to stable carbon accounts for the fact that it previously has eluded detection. The experiment established an important link in the chain of nuclear reactions by which heavy atoms are built up from lighter atoms.

Since it began operation in 1954, the six billion electron volt Bevatron has been the world's leading producer of high energy physics knowledge. More powerful accelerators now are operating. A brief description of high energy and elementary particle physics, what it is, and what is to be learned from research in this field, is given in Appendix 4.

Some 30 elementary nuclear particles have been discovered through high energy physics, and almost half this number are "antiparticles".

Almost every charged and neutral particle has a twin, or antiparticle, identical in some proper-

ties and opposite in others. Whenever a particle and its twin come together, their mass is converted into lighter particles, as is the case with the antiproton, the antiparticle of the proton. 

A considerable fraction of research is devoted to the study of these antiparticles. In

Bubble chambers. The 72-Inch Liquid Hydro gen Bubble Chamber at the Lawrence Radiation

Laboratory has been used for research since March 1959 to detect and study elementary particles and their interactions with matter. the last six months an experiment designed to study interactions of antiprotons of high momentum has been carried out. Antiproton collisions with the hydrogen in the chamber permitted discovery of new antiparticles-the antilambda and the antisigma zero (neutral) hyperons. Creation of 10 antilambdas was observed, somewhat fewer than expected. 

Annihilation of an antiproton by a proton also created many new particles; among them pions (pi-mesons with 1/7th the mass of a proton), and "strange" particles-a group of particles and their antiparticles discovered during the last few years. Lambda and sigma hyperons belong to this group as do K mesons. One research objective is to determine the relative frequency with which various strange particles are created in the annihilation process. At the energy of this experiment, K mesons were created about three times more frequently than when the annihilation occurs between particles at rest. Also, it was found that only in very rare cases are two mesons the sole final product of the annihilation. In only 15 of 20,000 annihilations recorded was there evidence that two pions alone were emitted, and only in five annihilations were two K mesons emitted. 

Scientists consider it surprising that the antiproton-proton process tends to create multiple particles in preference to creating only two, which would by themselves be sufficient to satisfy the law of conservation of energy and momentum. This scarcity of the two-particle results in disagreement with the predictions of statistical theory. Such discrepancies have been noted in earlier experiments, but the statistics of results from the present experiment make it possible to fix limits on the frequency of occurrence of the rare processes.

The same experiment gave information on interactions of antineutrons with neutrons. 

Antineutrons were created by antiprotons exchanging their charge with protons in the bubble chamber, and the frequency at which antineutrons annihilate was found to be about the same as that for antiprotons.

The properties of positive K mesons are being investigated in an experiment utilizing a 15inch bubble chamber. Interactions with both hydrogen and deuterium (heavy hydrogen) are being investigated. Many theoretical predictions, particularly theories referred to as "dispersion relations", can be tested by detailed investigations of this process. So far, experiments have shown that the frequency with which positive K mesons interact with deuterium to create neutral K mesons is strongly dependent upon the energy at which the interaction occurs. When the energy of the positive K meson is high enough, pion production takes place in addition to neutral K meson production. 

Counting techniques. In the last six months, a number of experiments carried out at the Bevatron used the electronic counter technique rather than a bubble chamber. This technique is able to detect a particle in as short a time as one millimicrosecond (one billionth of a second) and is immediately ready to detect the next one. When high fluxes of particles are available, a large number of interactions can be detected in a short period of time. Whereas the bubble chamber records reactions occurring in the substance with which it is filled, the counter techniques uses an external target. Counters are small in size-about 100 square centimeters is exposed to the beam-and they are placed around the target to detect reaction products resulting when accelerated particles pass through the target.

CHAMBER 

Bubble Chamber. View of the experimental apparatus located atop the huge 72-inch, 200-ton, bubble chamber located at the E. O. Lawrence Radiation Laboratory. The chamber, lower center of photo, is filled with liquid hydrogen at extremely low temperatures and under pressure. It is used in studies of high energy nuclear particles generated by the adjacent Bevatron. The circular openings, at bottom of photo, are part of the hydraulic "feet" which can be used to "walk" the chamber.

Particle Tracks. An example of the type of pictures obtained in bubble chambers is this photograph taken in a propane-filled bubble chamber at the Brookhaven National Laboratory's Cosmotron accelerator. The incoming beam is a mixture of pions and protons. The heavy tracks stopping in the chamber are the protons, and the light tracks going entirely through the chamber are pions. A number of interactions can also be noted. 

A major breakthrough in counter technique was accomplished for a study of interactions between pions. Pions have been referred to as the "glue" of the nucleus. Continuous exchange of pions between neutrons and protons binds them together into a nucleus. Consequently, pion-pion interaction is basic to understanding nuclear processes. Studies of pion interactions are difficult since one deals with very short-lived particles, existing only about one. twenty-five billionth of a second, and methods. had to be developed to circumvent this obstacle. 

In one experiment a beam of energetic pions (both positively and negatively charged) was directed into a target filled with liquid hydrogen. Approximately one in a million of the pions interacted with the hydrogen protons of the target to cause two pions and a neutron to emerge from the target. To detect these emerging particles, 84 scintillation counters were arranged in the form of a section of a spherical shell which looked something like a dish-shaped radio telescope. One and one half tons of plastic scintillators were used in building this 10foot diameter "dish." The 84 scintillators were used to define the positions of impact of the three emerging particles and hence to measure their angles of emission, as well as their time of flight. The information from the scintillation counters was transmitted in the form of electrical pulses to a data storage system which was designed and built at the laboratory, using some 4,000 to 5,000 transistors. The "stopwatch" phase of this measurement had an accuracy of about one billionth of a second. 

After the data storage bank was filled, the stored information was punched out on paper tape and subsequently analyzed with the aid of a large commercial electronic computer. However, since the first experiment did not yield sufficient results, it is planned to repeat it. The data obtained will take several months to analyze.

Another experiment carried out by counter technique was an investigation of negative K meson interactions with hydrogen and deuteri-

um over a large range of energies. A large array of scintillation counters and Cerenkov counters (designed to distinguish velocities of particles) surrounded the hydrogen target. 

The experimental data clearly indicated that as yet unexplained processes begin to occur at energies of about one billion electron volts. 

Beam separations. Much of the success of experiments using bubble chambers depends on sorting the particles generated by the accelerator beam. Many types of particles of various energies are produced by the impact of the accelerator proton beam on a target. The experimenter desiring to study the nature and properties of one particular particle must devise a technique to remove unwanted particles. Instruments of this nature have been developed at the Lawrence Radiation Laboratory at Berkeley.

The technique consists of what might be called a force filter. A crossed electric and magnetic field is produced which will permit the desired particles to pass through the system, but will exert a force to divert unwanted particles.

The higher the energy of the particles the more difficult it is to separate them. A limitation is the strength to which stable electric fields can be raised without generating an electrical discharge. A recent discovery at the laboratory will permit the separators to operate with considerably higher electric fields. 

It was found that discharges will occur two or three times less frequently when the positive electrode of an insulated high voltage system is made out of glass. Operation is much improved when the glass is heated to a temperature at which it behaves as a conductor. This discovery will permit experiments that have been impossible previously. 

Research at the 184-Inch Cyclotron

Research with the 184-inch cyclotron at Berkeley is confined to the studies of pions, muons (mu-mesons) and nucleons. The unstable pion decays into a muon and a neutrino, and the muon, in turn, decays into an electron, neutrino, and antineutrino. The muon, although one of the first known among unstable elementary particles, is one of the most puzzling. 

It behaves in every way like an electron except that its mass is 215 times greater. 

The interactions of muons with matter are of particular interest since, through these, it may be possible to learn some distinguishing features of the muon as well as the behavior of protons and neutrons within the nucleus. An experiment to explore muon interactions has been carried out by measuring the nuclear excitation produced when complex nuclei capture negative muons. When the capture occurs, the muon's mass is partially converted into energy which excites the nucleus. The excited nucleus will then emit some of its constituent neutrons and protons. A measurement of the neutron multiplicity resulting from muon capture by various elements was carried out. Since improvements in the 184-inch cyclotron have been completed, the pion beam produced by this accelerator is probably the most intense of its kind in the world. This large flux of pions affords the possibility of performing extremely refined experiments in pion physics. One most recently completed is a very accurate determination of the mass differences between neutral and charged pions, which now is known to an accuracy of two parts in one thousand.

Alternating Gradient Synchrotron 

A comprehensive experimental program is being prepared for the Alternating Gradient Synchrotron (AGS), which began test operations in mid-1960. Groups of physicists throughout the United States are helping to determine the initial requirements for research equipment to carry out the program. Development work has started on a beam separator system with 24-inch wide electrodes. A study of possible experimental beam arrangements has been completed and a basic assortment of bend-

88 Pion Producer. The 184-inch cyclotron at the Lawrence Radiation Laboratory has undergone a number of modifications since its construction was first conceived. It was redesigned during the building period so that the frequency of its oscillations could be changed, thus making it a "frequency modulated cyclotron" or synchrocyclotron. Since it began operation in 1946, its energy potential has been increased from 370 to 750 million electron volts. It was first used to accelerate deuterons, today it is used to produce pion particles. The "inch" designation (i.e., 184-inch) given a cyclotron refers to the diameter of the magnet pole faces. ing and focusing magnets for experimental beams has been designed. 

With completion and operation of the 72-inch bubble chamber for the Bevatron at Berkeley, the feasibility of large bubble chambers was established. An 80-inch bubble chamber is being planned as the main detection instrument at the AGS. Many factors were taken into consideration in selecting the size of the chamber. 

One of the more important measurements in any particle physics program is the particle momenta encountered in reactions initiated by the primary particles. At the energies resulting from the AGS beam, momenta will have to be determined by measurements of angles and track curvatures. Since these will be often quite small, tracks will have to be observed over considerable distances for precise measurements.

Another important aspect of high energy research is observation of particle decay. At 

AGS energies even short lived high energy particles will travel through considerable distances before decaying, and observation of these processes also requires a long bubble chamber path. Finally, a large chamber will make it possible to create a greater number of events for each synchrotron pulse.

The Brookhaven chamber will consist of a stainless steel vessel about 80 inches long, 25

Positioning Magnets. Inside the new Alternating Gradient Synchrotron at Brookhaven National Laboratory, technicians use precise surveying instruments to determine the preliminary positioning of the magnets. The magnets are used to keep the particles in a stable orbit as they take a one-second, 130,000-mile journey around the half-mile diameter, circular synchrotron which began test operation in mid-1960. inches wide, and 30 inches deep. A total of 900 liters of liquid hydrogen will be illuminated through a 612-inch thick glass window by light reflected by a combination of mirrors and lenses. 

Stereoscopic photographs of tracks will be taken through the same window. The chamber will be cooled by means of a liquid hydrogen refrigerator and surrounded by a radiation shield kept at liquid nitrogen temperature. 

The whole apparatus will be mounted inside a large vacuum chamber maintained by diffusion pumps. Automatic photographic cameras and light sources will be located outside the glass windows of the vacuum chamber.

In order to produce tracks, the chamber pressure will be relieved by a large 36-inch diameter piston which, at the proper time, will be allowed to be propelled outward. The corresponding expansion of liquid will then produce the superheated condition required to produce bubbles. 

The chamber is surrounded by an electromagnet containing about 300 tons of iron and 30 tons of copper in the form of water-cooled coils, powered by a four megawatt electric direct current power supply. The whole installation will be about 20 feet high and 15 feet wide and long.

Cosmotron counter for Bev neutrons. A sensitive gas-filled instrument has been designed for

detection of billion-volt neutrons at the Cosmo- Since neutrons cannot be directly detected, neutron energy is determined by threshold detection of protons produced by charge exchanges with neutrons in a converter. The 

Cerenkov velocity threshold was investigated by varying gas pressure in the instrument. 

The counter is 90 cm (centimeters) long by 10 cm in diameter and contains carbon dioxide at variable pressures up to 100 atmospheres (1,400 pounds per square inch). Tests in an external proton beam of the Cosmotron show that the counter has the correct threshold characteristics, and that background pulses generated by counter materials are negligible. 

METALLURGY AND MATERIALS New Metal Structures 

Metal physics research at the California Institute of Technology has devised a new technique for producing extremely rapid rates of cooling of metal systems. Through the use of this method, new structures in metal systems, predicted theoretically but never before observed, have been produced. Use of this technique contributes to an improved understanding of the fundamental nature and behavior of solids.

The classical method for achieving high rates of cooling consists of injecting a small droplet of molten alloy into a liquid quenching bath. 

The cooling, accomplished by transfer of heat from the alloy to the bath, is slowed by a gaseous layer between alloy and liquid caused by evaporation of the quenching liquid. The new technique avoids this limitation by replacing the liquid bath with a solid wall made of a good heat conductor. To accomplish rapid cooling, the liquid alloy must strike the cold surface at high speed in order to spread the liquid into a thin layer, and a good contact must be assured between the liquid alloy and the target conductor. A small liquid droplet (about 25 milligrams) was propelled by means of a shock wave against inside surface of a high speed rotating cylinder of solid copper. The centrifugal force of the rotation, acting on the molten alloy, insured a good thermal contact with the copper target, and also helped in spreading the liquid alloy over a larger area to assure a large heat transfer rate. 

This technique permits small amounts of liquid alloys to be cooled to room temperature or below at rates high enough to prevent the normal process of nucleation and growth of equilibrium phases. The structure of these alloys is different from that normally obtained and their properties are unusual. Since these alloys are metastable, the effect of radiation on their structure will be quite drastic. They are obtained in the form of foils about 10 to 15 microns thick and are ideally suited for studying the effect of electron irradiation on the structure of alloys.

The new method was first applied to alloys of silver and copper, which normally form a eutectic. Under fast cooling conditions the two metals were formed into a solid solution. This result was predicted on the basis of certain criteria for the existence of complete miscibility (mutual solubility) between two metals. The rapid cooling technique was then applied to silver-germanium alloys. In this system, normally a eutectic type, complete miscibility is precluded by dissimilar crystal structure. After rapid cooling from the melt, a new compound of the approximate composition 

Ag,Ge (silver germanium) was discovered.

It has been found that under certain conditions some alloys can be quenched fast enough from the molten state to retain a quasi-liquid structure. Such alloys could be studied in the solid state and thereby provide a greatly simplified experimental approach to studying the structure of liquids. 

Metal Surface Studies 

Efforts to characterize the factors that determine the degree of protectiveness of oxide films on metals have been continued at Oak Ridge National Laboratory. A new apparatus for

measuring oxidation rates was constructed, and the transition from the protective to the nonprotective stage of oxidation of niobium (columbium) was investigated by means of extended oxidation rate measurements with polycrystalline specimens at temperature of 

400° to 425° C. 

Electron microscopic studies of these heavily oxidized specimens emphasized that the oxidation rate depends on crystallographic orientation and revealed the development of a layerlike structure in the oxide. This layerlike structure was thought to be indicative of a patchlike cracking of the oxide film, a pattern consistent with the previously proposed oxidation mechanism for niobium. Preliminary oxidation-rate studies with monocrystalline. niobium specimens have been undertaken to determine the influence of the metal's crystallographic orientation on the mechanism of crack formation in niobium oxide films. 

Calculation of Radiation Effects 

The process by which nuclear radiation produces lattice defects in a metal has been investigated at Brookhaven National Laboratory with the aid of a new technique which might be called "mathematical microscopy." The equations of motion for a many-body system are solved numerically on a high speed computer. Calculations involving some 500 atoms interacting with realistic forces have been made.

An atom on the interior of the model is initially set into motion as though it had just been struck by a bombarding particle. The computing machine then determines the trajectories resulting from this motion and establishes the final atomic configuration.

Detailed pictures of a variety of typical low energy damage events have been obtained by this means. The minimum energies required for the production of permanently displaced atoms as a result of irradiation are being determined as a function of direction. The propagation of energy away from the site of radiation impact occurs largely along certain crystallographic lines, and is channeled along these lines by dynamic "focusing" effects.

Atoms are seen to move readily from site to site, and for each permanent displacement several "replacement" events occur. Interstitialvacancy pairs are unstable unless separated by a minimum distance somewhat larger than a lattice spacing, and the interstitial atom is found to reside in a configuration of lower symmetry than was assumed. After the more violent stages of an event initiated by radiation impact, the initial kinetic energy becomes heat energy. Previous attempts to allow for the many-body character of such events (thermal and displacement spike models) now are seen as a result of the mathematical microscopy method studies, to be very crude. Better ideas of these events are being formed. 

Applications of the calculations outside the realm of radiation damage also have been found. It is possible to estimate how long a recoiling atom in a crystal "remembers" its initial velocity, and this has been useful in interpreting experiments on resonance fluorescence of gamma rays.

Radiation Effects in Semiconductors 

The possibility that irradiation can be used to introduce chemical impurities in certain electrical semiconductors is being explored at the Oak Ridge National Laboratory. Besides alterations of the electrical properties caused by induced transmutation products, lattice defects are created by recoil of excited nuclei when emitting radiation after capturing neutrons. In view of the importance of semiconductor devices in space vehicles, and the fact that such devices are exposed to natural radiation in space, the importance of a thorough understanding of transient processes in semiconductors is evident.

Valuable information on the fundamental behavior of semiconductors with appropriate nuclear properties can be obtained from thermal neutron irradiation. The fixed rate of radio-

active decay after irradiation with thermal neutrons permits observations of time-dependent electronic properties. The rate of shift of chemical potential with impurity introduction may be used to determine the ionization energies of defects or impurities. 

Carrier concentration measurements on germanium have been employed to verify the neutron absorption cross section, decay scheme, and half-life predicted by nuclear physics.

Minority carrier lifetime, i.e., a measure of the time required for a disturbance in the concentration of electrons or holes in a semiconductor, to relax toward equilibrium, is one of the most sensitive indications of lattice defects in semiconductors. Lifetime measurements have been utilized to investigate the electronic structure of irradiation-produced lattice disorder. One of the more notable results of these studies is the observation that chemical impurities, present in germanium to the extent of only several parts per billion, have an important influence on the course of annealing radiation-produced lattice defects at temperatures near 100° C.

Different chemical impurities have different specific effects. This evidence of extensive interaction between imperfections present in such low concentrations indicates the need for a better understanding of the principles of defect migration in semiconductors and long-range interactions. 

Transport Phenomena in Solids

Since the process of extracting large amounts of heat from a metal-clad fuel element in a nuclear reactor results in a large temperature gradient, it is important to clarify these effects by additional experimental and theoretical work. Studies being done under a Commission contract at Atomics International, a division of North American Aviation, Inc., include the effect of large temperature gradients on the interdiffusion of different metals in contact. 

The temperature gradient changes the diffusion rate appreciably in several combinations of metals. Both increases and decreases in diffusion rates occur depending on which metal is at the higher temperature. Existing diffusion theories do not explain this effect. 

Irradiation of Graphite The chemical reactivity of surfaces of crystalline graphite have been studied at Argonne National Laboratory by methods that combine the results of optical and electron microscopy with those of kinetics. This had led to a delineation of the roles of lattice defects and of catalytic substances. 

Graphite crystals oxidized in the presence of metallic catalysts are attacked on the basal plane only when lattice defects are present. 

This fact has been used in a technique to detect the presence of lattice defects. It has been established that a lattice defect, believed to be the "single lattice vacancy" type, is present in a detectable equilibrium concentration at temperatures above 2,500° C, and that another as yet unidentified defect is present in comparable numbers at about 2,800° C.

The kinetics of several reactions of irradiated and of unirradiated graphite have been studied in detail at Argonne National Laboratory. The reaction of graphite with oxygen was found to be very susceptible to catalysis by water, an effect that can be eliminated only by prior heating in a vacuum. But this method of eliminating water catalysis cannot be used with irradiated graphite because it results in annealing most of the radiation damage. It has been found, however, that catalysis by water can be suppressed by small amounts of chlorine.

The study of the formation of lamellar compounds of graphite has been extended at Argonne to include the reactions of lithium and of sodium with graphite. Although neither sodium nor lithium alone will react with graphite, compound formation does take place in the presence of such impurities as oxygen, hydrogen, or water. Attempts to prepare a lamellar compound of graphite containing hydrogen by proton bombardment of graphite,

or by electrolysis of molten lithium hydride between graphite electrodes were unsuccessful. 

Measurements of magnetic effects on the conducting properties of polycrystalline and single-crystal graphites have been carried out at Argonne. Samples tested included irradiated graphite, chemically doped graphite, and irradiated doped graphite, measured at several low temperatures. The resulting measurements suggest that the concentration of electron traps created in graphite by irradiation is strongly dependent upon the temperature.

This interpretation is very tentative, because anomalous variations of certain measurements of pure graphite occurred-an anomaly that disappears when the graphite is doped or irradiated, irradiation being relatively much more effective.

Burning Studies in Graphite Channels 

Studies have been conducted at Brookhaven National Laboratory on the effects of graphite temperature, gas flow rate, channel diameters, and channel lengths on the conditions leading to graphite burning when air is passed through a hot graphite channel in an adiabatic system. 

These studies relate to problems in the operation of graphite-moderated reactors, particularly during anneals to counteract radiation damage to graphite when the graphite temperature is high and the gas flow rates are low. 

The "burning" occurs when the heat generated by oxidation becomes greater than the flowing gas stream is capable of removing. 

In the Brookhaven experiments, channels of 

10-foot lengths with diameters from 1 to 2.6 inches were used. The results obtained show that it is possible to predict the length of channel that can be cooled as a function of the temperature, flow rate, diameter, and graphite reactivity. Thus, it was demonstrated that, under flow rates available at the graphite research reactor, burning does not set in until temperatures of the order of 750° C are reached. These data settle a major uncertainty in the field of graphite-moderated reactors. 

Cryogenic Studies in Reactor 

Basic studies of the effect of reactor irradiation on the properties of materials require a facility which permits bombardments at very low temperatures since an appreciable fraction of the damage anneals out at temperatures below 40° K (-387° F). A cryostat, or lowtemperature chamber, designed and in operation at Oak Ridge National Laboratory permits bombardments of materials at temperatures as low as 3.8° K (-453° F). Conditions in the reactor where the cryostat is placed-gamma ray heating and a relatively inaccessible position-require a high-capacity refrigeration system. The system consists of a compressor section which compresses helium gas from 2 atmospheres to 20 atmospheres, a counterflow heat exchanger and expansion engines. The refrigeration proper is provided by the helium gas expanding from about 20 atmospheres to 2 atmospheres in the engines, which causes the gas to be cooled. Cold gas then is passed through vacuum-jacketed lines into the center of the reactor, cooling a region approximately 11½ inches in diameter and 2 feet long to a temperature of about 10° K (-441° F).

To obtain still lower temperatures, about 2 percent of the gas from the compressor is cooled to near 10° K by the main helium-gas stream as it leaves the expansion engines. 

This gas then is allowed to expand in a manner similar to that used in commercial refrigeration systems so that temperatures of about 3.8° K (-453° F) can be reached in a chamber approximately 1/2 inch in diameter and 6 inches long in the center of the chamber which was previously cooled to 10° K (-441° F) by the main helium-gas stream.

As an illustration of the complexity of the problem, approximately 50 horsepower is expended in the compression of the helium and the gas then is passed through a refrigeration system weighing 6 tons-all this to cool the small volume, 1/2 inch in diameter and 6 inches in length, to 3.8° K (-453° F).

This facility has made it possible to measure

OAK RIDGE NATIONAL LABORATORY 

Cryogenic Studies. More than 6 tons of refrigeration equipment are used to cool a tiny cryostat less than 4 inches long used in cryogenic irradiation studies inside the X-10 reactor being made at the Oak Ridge National Laboratory. The upper photo shows a technician monitoring the controls of the refrigeration machine which produces temperatures below 400° F. through the compression of helium gas. The refrigeration unit has been dubbed the "African Queen". The lower photo shows the cryostat beside a ruler for size comparison.

the effects of irradiation on energy release, yield stresses, irradiation-expansion data, and changes in electrical conductivity in several materials. Fission-fragment damage has been studied in aluminum and copper by doping these elements with one-tenth atomic percent of uranium 235. Of particular interest was a study of expansion of these materials resulting from defects created by fission fragments. It was deduced that about 700 defects (vacancyinterstitial pairs) were formed per fission event. 

This facility could readily be used to study the types of materials problems associated with space propulsion, since the facility can reproduce conditions affecting a space vehicle. 

Intense Magnetic Fields 

A small-scale study at Los Alamos Scientific Laboratory is investigating the feasibility of producing intense magnetic fields by using implosively propelled metals. The purposes are: to produce and measure magnetic fields of intensities never achieved before in laboratory work; to investigate related special problems in field-plasma and field-dielectric interactions; to investigate resulting mechanical-electrical energy transduction; and to examine the possibility of accelerating metal projectiles to high velocities by applying magnetic field pressure.

Fields of 10 to 15 million gauss (over a volume of the order of one cubic centimeter, and for time durations approaching one microsecond) now are fairly reliably produced by implosion of a cylindrical conductor inside of which an initial axial magnetic field of about 

75,000 gauss has been generated. The initial field increases in magnitude as the implosion decreases volume of the field. At peak field intensities the associated pressures-5 to 10 million atmospheres, or some 70 to 140 million pounds per square inch-are so large that ordinary laboratory experiments on such magnetic fields are impossible.

Emphasis is being given in the plasma phase of the experiment to producing a large neutron burst within a deuterium or a deuteriumtritium plasma. The plasma will be placed in a tube along the axis of the cylindrical implosion assembly, pre-energized by electrical discharge, and further heated and compressed during the magnetic field implosion.

Studies of Magnetism by Neutron Diffraction 

New equipment is now in use at the Oak 

Ridge Research Reactor which significantly increases the scope of investigations of the basic properties of magnetism at the atomic level. 

This new equipment consists of an electromagnet with an associated cryostat (low temperature chamber) and a single crystal goniometer. The magnet sits on the table of a neutron spectrometer. The associated cryostat holds the sample to be studied between the poles of the magnet at which point it is irradiated with reactor neutrons which have been made monoenergetic by reflection from a beryllium crystal. A single crystal sample can be held at temperatures between 1.2° Kelvin and many hundred degrees centigrade. It can be rotated at will at these temperatures for the study of its magnetic neutron reflecting properties. In addition, the magnet and cryostat can be rotated about both a horizontal and a vertical axis to allow the magnetic field to be applied at any angle with the crystal and with the neutron beam.

Neutron Diffraction of Iron Sulfide 

The peculiar magnetic properties of iron sulfide compounds containing from 50 to 53 atomic percent sulfur have challenged experimenters and theoreticians for more than two decades. 

Although structurally similar throughout this range of composition, and over the range of temperatures that have been investigated, these compounds nevertheless exhibit abrupt and striking changes in magnetization as a function of temperature and of composition. 

The gross magnetic behavior of these ma-

terials can be accounted for by assuming that the atomic magnetic moments associated with each iron atom are arranged in layers corresponding to crystal planes, with all atomic magnets in each layer pointing in the same direction, but with adjacent layers pointing in the opposite direction. This model provided a satisfactory explanation of the general features of the magnetic behavior, but discontinuities in magnetization were found which pointed to unexplained subtleties of the magnetic structure. 

High intensity neutron beams, recently available from research reactors, provided a new and powerful technique for studying the atomic magnetic structure of crystalline materials. The neutron, by virtue of its magnetic moment, provides a unique probe for determining the position and orientation of the atomic magnets which govern the behavior of all magnetic materials. 

Using a beam of neutrons from the Lawrence

Radiation Laboratory's Livermore research reactor, the scattering from carefully prepared samples of iron sulfide is being studied over a wide range of temperatures. A neutron diffraction spectrometer permits examination of selected reflections corresponding to the magnetic layers of primary importance, and provides direct data concerning the orientation. of the atomic magnets.

Of particular significance has been an investigation of a suggestion that some of the magnetic anomalies are caused by an abrupt rotation of the atomic magnetic moments. This type of change due to temperature is a rare event, and the forces involved are imperfectly understood.
