Most recent data, however, show that such a rotation of magnetic moments does take place in certain iron sulfide compounds but, surprisingly, at a temperature different from that at which the abrupt change in magnetization occurs.

It is expected that additional data obtainable with the neutron diffraction technique will assist in explaining magnetism at the atomic level, and in clarifying the relationship between atomic magnetism and the behavior of magnetic materials. 

Metal Studies at Ames

New analytical procedures were developed in connection with the pure metals program at Ames Laboratory. These include methods for determining oxygen in fluorides spectrographically, by inert gas fusion, and by vacuum distillation. A method was developed for simultaneous determination of oxygen, nitro- 

Numerous gen and hydrogen in metals. methods were devised also for determining other trace impurities in metals. 

Further improvements were made in the crystal bar process for vanadium. A method was developed for removing oxygen from thorium to less than 16 parts per million (ppm), starting with thorium containing up to 1,300 ppm oxygen. This is below the lowest reported oxygen content in crystal bar thorium. 

The method consists of treating the metal at temperatures of 1,000° to 1,200° C in an atmos- Proportional Counter. The largest proportional counter of its kind in the free world is used in nuclear spectroscopy work at Ames Laboratory. The device is used in determining oxygen in fluorides spectrographically in connection with the pure metals program being carried out at the laboratory.

phere of calcium vapor. The oxygen diffuses to the surface and reacts with the calcium to form calcium oxide, which then can be removed from the metal. The method has been found to be applicable also to niobium and vanadium metal. 

The oxygen content of chromium metal was decreased from 5,000 ppm to 125 ppm by precipitation from molten calcium. 

Rubidium and cesium were prepared in reasonably good yields by a reduction of the fluorides with lanthanum in a vacuum.

Investigations in alloy theory and solid state physics resulted in information that will lead to a better understanding of the nature and properties of solids. The phase diagrams of a number of alloy systems were determined. Considerable information was obtained concerning electrical and thermal properties of metals and semiconductors. A new microwave technique was developed for obtaining information on electronic conduction in solids. New semiconductors were found among the tungsten bronzes.

The crystal structures of a number of intermetallic compounds were determined and fundamental data were obtained concerning their physical properties. These compounds have potential utility at elevated temperatures. 

In order to understand better the physical properties of metals, measurements were made. on elasticity, thermal expansion, vapor pressure, heats of transition, cohesive energies and heats of fusion. Many of these measurements were made on single crystals and over an extensive temperature range, and some at high pressures. 

Single crystals of a number of rare earths, including dysprosium, erbium, thulium, and terbium were grown. The magnetic and electrical properties of dysprosium and erbium were measured.

Electronic Structure 

In metals the energy distribution of the electrons (electronic structure) plays a dominant. part in determining the electrical, thermal, mechanical, and magnetic properties; hence, any basic understanding of these properties depends on a knowledge of the electronic structure. This project at Atomics International combines measurements of magnetic properties, electronic specific heat, electron transport properties and theoretical work to determine electronic structure in metals and the way in which this structure is deformed by alloying additions. 

During the past year, a pulsed magnet capable of achieving homogeneous magnetic fields up to 200 kilogauss has been constructed to determine magnetic susceptibility at high fields. The periodic variation in susceptibility with increasing magnetic fields is known as the "de Haasvan Alphen effect" and analysis of such data provides the most powerful means known for determining the energy of electrons in metals. 

This de Haas-van Alphen effect was observed for the first time in calcium, and the data are expected to give a definitive description of its electronic structure. 

Special titanium-rich molybdenum alloys were studied extensively by measurements of the electronic specific heat and electron transport properties. These alloys are quite unusual in that their electrical resistivity is nearly independent of temperature from room temperature down to near absolute zero. In addition, lowtemperature super-conductivity can be maintained in magnetic fields as great as 30 kilogauss. Such studies provide a basis for evaluations of theoretical models for the structure and stability of this unusual class of alloys and indicate potentially new technological applications as a result of their unusual electrical properties. 

Ultra High Pressure Tests 

Equation of state of metals. Shock pressures and associated compressions of 19 metallic elements have been measured at Los Alamos Scientific Laboratory to about two million atmospheres. The pressure-compression curves were used in calculations to determine a com-

plete high-pressure equation of state 14 for each metal. This program extends experimental work on equation of state from 100,000 atmospheres attainable in precise laboratory static measurements and the 500,000 atmospheres attained at Los Alamos in previous shock wave work. Data from both shock wave programs and available data from static experiments appear consistent within experimental uncertainties of one or two percent.

The study is currently being extended to include six additional metals. 

Elastic-plastic waves in metals. At low shock pressures, material strength tests create double shock waves, rather than the single shocks observed at high pressures. Double waves, usually observed in the 10,000 to 100,000 atmosphere pressure range, are generated either by high-explosive detonation or by impact against a plane-surface using a conventional cannon discharging a flat-nosed projectile. 

Complete elastic wave determinations have been made at Los Alamos for some 10 metals. 

Additional but less complete results have been obtained for the plastic wave which follows the elastic pulse. This work should provide a fairly complete description of plane elastic-plastic wave propagation for several metals. Dynamic strength then can be inferred and compared to corresponding material properties obtained from static laboratory tests.

Shocked and recovered metals. At Los Alamos several metals have been subjected to shocks in the 50,000 to 1,000,000 atmosphere range, recovered, sectioned, and studied metallurgically. Photomicrographs reveal mechanical twinning (Neumann bands), the abundance and orientation of which have been determined for various shock pressures. An increase in hardness also is associated with dynamic workhardening. Metal grain size and orientation are preserved during the shock process. 

14 An "equation of state" is a mathematical formulation which, when developed to the ultimate, would permit calculation of the properties of a given substance under any set of conditions. 573517 0-61- 8 Irons and steels have been studied most extensively. An anticipated wave interaction in iron has been identified as an abrupt change of hardness in the recovered samples and the observation has been used to determine pressure-compression data, for iron and iron alloys, in the 200,000 to 300,000 atmosphere pressure range.

Electrical conductivity of insulators. The high-explosive shock wave technique also has been applied to electronic structure of solids because of the importance to theory of this state. Electromagnetic measurements are key factors in this analysis. In 1956, in high explosive experiments at Lawrence Radiation

Laboratory, Livermore, electrical conductivity was measured behind a strong shock wave in several types of normal insulators. 

Interesting qualitative results have been obtained in subsequent experiments. Among the alkali halide crystals, those of largest molecular weight exhibited high electrical conductivity at shock pressures above 200,000 atmospheres. 

At these pressures they are either transforming to a metallic phase or the "forbidden" energy gap for electron excitation has considerably changed from its normal value. In the light alkali halides having a pure ionic bond, the electron energy bands are less affected by pressure. By contrast the bands associated with molecular crystals like phosphorus, or lithium hydride produce large conductivities up to 

100,000 atmospheres.

The phase transitions of red and yellow phosphorus to black phosphorus in shock wave pressures of about 30,000 to 80,000 atmospheres respectively has been measured in conductivity experiments. The transition pressure in yellow phosphorus as measured by conductivity changes has been shown to be unaffected by lowering the initial temperature as much as 

200° C.

In recent conductivity experiments, the major effort has been made to improve the accuracy and reproducibility of these results, and to make quantitative comparison with conduc-

ATOMIC-ENERGY RESEARCH IN THE LIFE AND PHYSICAL SCIENCES 

tivity measurements made in hydrostatic high pressure apparatus. For this purpose iodine and red phosphorus are being studied in the shock pressure range from 30,000 to 60,000 atmospheres. The variation of the conductivity with the initial temperature of the samples (before shocking) will then be used to measure for the first time directly the temperature and electron energy gap behind a strong shock front.

Creating new materials by explosions. An extreme though temporary pressure can be generated in a solid material when it is subjected to an explosively driven shock. Pressures of a few million atmospheres lasting for about one millionth of a second have been obtained in this way. Such material exists for short times under conditions very much like those at the center of the earth since the shock also gets the material quite hot. This technique makes it possible to learn the properties of the material comprising the earth core. Results so far obtained show that they might be quite different from the same material under ordinary conditions.

At these high pressures and temperatures all materials have quite different properties. For example, it was found at Lawrence Radiation Laboratory, Livermore, that almost all materials which are insulators under ordinary conditions become metallic or conduct electricity very well. Thus, ordinary table salt behaved as a metal at pressure of about 500,000 atmospheres.

Most materials under these conditions also have a different arrangement of their atoms than they do ordinarily. The big problem was whether this rearrangement of the atoms could take place in the extremely short time of one millionth of a second. The surprising result was that the rearrangement did take place, even though when the same rearrangement was tried by applying pressure slowly the transformation required hours in some cases, if it took place at all.

It was thus possible to make diamonds from graphite at pressures above 700,000 atmospheres, even though the diamonds soon disintegrated because of the heat.

Another type of rearrangement of atoms that has been observed results in the destruction of chemical bonds. At high pressures the electrons responsible for the chemical bond have been squeezed down to such small volume that they spread out over the entire volume of the material. Thus, the bond is destroyed and a new metallic material is created. The material for which this was observed was iodine at a pressure of about 700,000 atmospheres. 

Diffusion of Chromium in Alloys

The chemical processes in corrosion of nickelbase alloys by molten fluoride mixtures at high temperature have been investigated at the Oak Ridge National Laboratory. Self-diffusion coefficients which agreed within the precision of measurement were obtained by three different experimental techniques; extrapolations of these data yielded values which were consistent with those obtained by other investigators with generally similar alloys at higher temperatures. 

These self-diffusion coefficients have been used to predict rates of chromium removal when the alloy is exposed to molten fluoride mixtures containing strong oxidants. The predicted concentrations for chromium were in excellent agreement with those determined experimentally in alloy test loops exposed for several hundred hours to circulating molten fluorides. 

Semiconductor Particle Detectors 

Semiconductor detectors for nuclear particles with remarkably good qualities for detecting particles of specific energies have been developed at Oak Ridge National Laboratory. An energy resolution of 15 kilovolts for 5 Mev alpha particles has been achieved-the best with any type of detector. These devices are capable

of detecting alpha particles, fission fragments, protons, heavy ions, neutrons, electrons, and gamma rays.

Since these detectors can be made in a variety of sizes, some as small as a pinhead, their use as medical probes appears promising. The detectors already are being used in fission experiments, scattering experiments, as a fast neutron spectrometer and as a small neutron detector which can be inserted between fuel element plates of close spacing for the purpose of measuring neutron flux distribution in a reactor.

Other applications of these devices include neutron dosimetry, small portable alpha and neutron survey meters, detection of airborne alpha contamination by using energy discrimination against the radon and thoron always present in the atmosphere. 

Metals for Reactors

Ames Laboratory is working extensively with yttrium which shows promise as a fuel container material for reactors. Ames is attempting to correlate the purity of yttrium with its properties, and determine the effect of added impurities.

Improved methods were developed for lowering the oxygen content of the metal. One method utilized a salt bath to dissolve interstitial impurities, particularly oxygen, and reduced oxygen to about 150 parts per million (ppm) of the metal. 

It was found that fluorine can be removed from yttrium sponge by electron beam melting; the fluoride content was decreased from 600 to 

40 ppm in two melting operations.

Dynamic tests were made on yttrium as a container material for fuel alloys in the range of 800° to 1000° C with encouraging results. 

Yttrium was successfully cold-extruded into complex shapes, thus demonstrating the inherent ductility of the metal.

In other experiments, Ames Laboratory gained valuable engineering experience with molten metal that will be applicable to development of a molten metal reactor. Thoriummagnesium eutectic was circulated in a niobium loop for 650 hours at 800° C with no sign of mass transfer or erosion.

In a study of the distribution of protactinium between a magnesium-thorium phase and a uranium-chromium phase, it was found that the protactinium was essentially quantitatively extracted by the uranium-chromium. This information is of particular importance to the thorium 233-uranium 233 cycle in breeder reactors.

Alloy extractants, in particular the magnesium-silver eutectic, were shown to remove rare earth fission products from the uranium-chromium eutectic with a very large decontamination factor.

Data obtained concerning vapor pressures of components of zinc-magnesium and zinc-aluminum alloys will be of value in the application of these alloys to high temperature processing.

CHEMISTRY Production of Transplutonium Elements 

A program initiated several years ago has resulted in increasing production of transplutonium elements for experimental purposes. 

Approximately three years ago, one of the normal uranium shim rods in the Materials 

Testing Reactor (MTR) in Idaho, was replaced by a specially fabricated shim rod containing 

78 grams of plutonium. After two years of irradiation in the high neutron flux and four months of cooling, the rod was chemically processed at Argonne National Laboratory to separate the remaining plutonium isotopes and the heavier elements formed by neutron capture.

The fuel plates comprising the shim rod were estimated to contain a total of 100 curies of alpha activity, mainly from curium 244 and curium 242, and about 15,000 curies of fission products.

The processing yielded about one gram of americium 243, a half gram of curium 244 and five grams of plutonium 242-the largest quantities of these heavy isotopes ever prepared.

The materials will be converted to oxides, mixed with aluminum powder, and compressed into pellets for reirradiation in the MTR. In approximately three years, these samples are expected to yield about one milligram each of berkelium and californium, and some micrograms of einsteinium.

A long range program to produce very rare elements for research also has been inaugurated. 

Twelve kilograms of plutonium were inserted in a reactor at the Savannah River Plant in 1959. By 1963 and 1964 this program should produce relatively large quantities of plutonium 242, americium 243 and curium 244 as feed material for further irradiations in the MTR and in a new special High Flux Isotope Reactor the Commission plans to build at Oak Ridge National Laboratory. This would lead eventually to production of gram quantities of berkelium and californium. With relatively large quantities of very heavy nuclides available, it should be possible during the 1960's to synthesize elements 103 and 104, or perhaps even heavier elements. 

A particular phase of this overall program is the measurement of the low energy cross sections for as many of the heavy isotopes as possible in order to predict more accurately the amount of certain important isotopes that will be produced by a selected irradiation program and to work out more efficient schedules for their production. The MTR fast chopper has been equipped with a heavily shielded radioactive sample changer in order to measure the cross sections of heavy isotopes whose cross sections as a function of energy could not otherwise be measured. Fairly complete total cross section data with detailed resonance information can be obtained up to 20 ev with 50 to 500 mg samples. Approximate resonance information in the near thermal region can be obtained with samples as small as 1 mg. Such measurements are important to nuclear physics research in the heavy isotopes and to the evaluation of breeding and recycle reactors. 

Heavy Element Chemistry

Tetravalent americium has been obtained in previous experiments only under anhydrous conditions. Scientists at Los Alamos Scientific 

Laboratory have proved that a black hydroxide of tetravalent americium can be produced by treating americium trihydroxide with dilute hypochlorite solution. This new compound is highly reactive chemically. It dissolves in acid solution undergoing extremely rapid selfoxidation and reduction, and produces a mixture of the tri-penta- and hexavalent states. 

Small scale experiments at Lawrence Radiation Laboratory, Berkeley, using microgram to milligram quantities of transuranium elements to determine their chemical properties produced the following recent results: (a) development of a new technique for preparing curium metal and the compounds, americium monoxide and plutonium monoxide; (b) restudy of the crystal structure of several forms of americium metal; (c) first isolation of microgram amounts of californium in the form of a pure compound, californium iodate.

Lawrence Radiation Laboratory also conducted experiments directed toward defining the pattern of electron orbits around the nuclei of a heavy element. Transuranium elements are known to be members of the "actinide" group, which is similar to the lanthanide elements, or rare earths, in electron arrangement. 

Characteristically, elements of the rare earth group have identical outer orbital electron shells the shells which determine chemical behavior of elements-and differ in the arrangement of electrons in inner orbital shells. This makes it difficult for many rare earths to be separated from each other chemically, and such physical separations as ion exchange have to be used.

Scientists have differed over whether the lightest members of the actinide group-thorium and protactinium-are true actinides characterized by the presence of orbital electrons of the 5f (rare earth) type. Experiments at

Lawrence Laboratory demonstrated that one

form of protactinium, the tetrapositive ion, exhibited paramagnetism characteristic of a 5f electron pattern. This result was obtained from a study of the paramagnetic resonance spectrum of a small amount of protactinium incorporated in a crystal of calcium zirconium chloride (Ca, Zrcl。). 

Studies of the behavior of actinide elements on ion exchange resins assists in understanding their chemistry and separation from each other and from lanthanide elements. In work carried out jointly by Oak Ridge National Laboratory and Florida State University, a series of experiments have been conducted using cation exchange resins such as are used in rare earth separation and elution (removal by washing of the resins) with various concentrations of hydrochloric acid. Researchers tested the behavior of the transplutonium series of actinide elements with respect to each other and with respect to elements of the rare-earth elements. They found that with increase of acid concentration, the usual order of elution was reversed for several actinide pairs including curium and americium, berkelium and curium, and, in the lanthanide series, for promethium, samarium, and europium. These reversals also were somewhat dependent on the particular type of cation resin used.

These results lead to new explanations for the behavior of the elements of these two similar series of elements. 

Fission Induced by Heavy Ions

The existence at Yale and Berkeley of heavy ion linear accelerators (HILAC), which produce high-energy beams of the nuclei of elements such as carbon, nitrogen, oxygen and neon, permits the investigation of nuclear reactions and the production of radioactive isotopes which cannot be produced in machines which accelerate lighter nuclear particles.

Much has been learned about special types of nuclear fission reactions through the HILAC machines. Such elements as lead, or gold or even lighter elements lighter than those usually thought of as fissionable can be induced to fission when struck with ions of carbon or other heavy ions.

Fission probably results because the target nucleus is thrown into rapid rotation when struck off center by the heavy ion projectile. 

The result of this rapid rotation (high angular momentum) is a strong observed correlation between the direction of fission product emission and the direction of the incoming beam.

Theoretical calculations of the lowered energy barrier to fission resulting from high angular momentum are being compared to experimental results, as are also theoretical calculations of the angular distribution of the fragments. Radiochemical studies of the products of fission induced in gold targets indicate a narrow and symmetric distribution of mass in fission fragments which is very different from the broad asymmetric distribution occurring in fission of uranium.

Studies of fission induced by heavy ions assist in understanding the basic mechanisms of fission reaction.

Ion Exchange Studies 

Methods of engineering analysis are being developed at Oak Ridge National Laboratory to aid in design and operation of ion exchange processes of interest to the Commission. A specific system, the anion exchange of uranium (VI) between aqueous sulfate solutions and

Dowex 21K, is being studied in detail to produce the equilibrium and kinetic data required for a comprehensive mathematical analysis. 

Equilibrium data have been obtained and correlated. 

Experimental methods have been developed for defining the kinetics of uranium transport in ion exchange resins, and experimental data have been obtained on a number of resins.

These studies have included a range of solution concentrations and resin size.

Mathematical models which describe the uranium transport within anion resins have been developed, and programs for machine cal-

Heavy Ion Accelerator. The Heavy Ion Linear Accelerator (HILAC) in operation at the E. O. Lawrence Radia tion Laboratory is allowing scientists to investigate nuclear reactions and produce radiactive isotopes which require the acceleration of heavy nuclear particles. Particles fired down the length of the tube reach energies of 200 million electron volts and higher. The machine also is being used in attempts to synthesize new forms of matter heavier than element 102. (All elements above 92 are manmade.) culations are being prepared to explore the relative merits of the models. The next phase of analysis will be to extend calculations to predict the behavior of continuous and fixed ion exchange columns. 

Experimental work is underway to evaluate the hydrodynamic conditions that cause a stagnant film to surround the resin and limit ion exchange rates.

An ion exchange process has been developed for 99.9 percent removal of strontium and cesium from low level wastes. Typical coprecipitation processes now used achieve only about 90 percent removal. The ion exchange procedure also decreases the volume of waste requiring storage and is more economical than other processes giving the same degree of activity removal and waste volume reduction. 

This development is based on the unusually high selectivity and capacity of phenolic ion exchange resins for cesium and strontium at high levels of alkalinity.

Highly specific absorption of cesium from acidic salt solutions has been accomplished at the Idaho Chemical Processing Plant. Am monium phosphomolybdate removes cesium from aluminum-nitrate and aluminum complexed zirconium fluoride solutions in the presence of one molar nitric, hydrochloric or sulfuric acid with a decontamination factor of at least 2×103 and a loading indicative of a chemical exchange.

Surface Studies 

A new experimental electro-osmotic technique has been developed at Oak Ridge National Laboratory to estimate various properties of settled beds of powders at elevated temperatures (250° C). Determinations were completed for several thorium oxide powders in water and for a wide variety of dilute electrolytic solutions at 25° C. Similar measurements at 90° C and 150° C are in progress. 

Calculations of the surface potential required to provide a defined degree of peptization (colloidal solution) for suspensions of thorium oxide in water, and in dilute electrolytic aqueous solutions over a wide range of conditions, have been completed and the results published. 

Studies of the adsorption of ionic species by thorium oxide surfaces at high temperatures have continued.

Thorium Oxide Studies 

Either platinum or palladium when adsorbed on thorium oxide surfaces in suitable fashion. display marked capacity to catalyze the combination of hydrogen and oxygen into water. 

Catalysts of this sort are useful in certain water. moderated reactors in which radiation decomposes water into its constituent gases and must be recombined. Palladium has been prepared in the form of a stable colloidal suspension which, when added to a thorium slurry, provides adequate and reproducible catalytic activity. The catalytic activity of platinum decreases with time or repeated usage while that of palladium is retained for much longer times.

The effectiveness of both catalysts is influenced by the gas mixture. Palladium loses activity greatly under oxygen-rich mixtures and to some degree under comparatively high hydrogen content. Platinum retains its activity much better with oxygen-rich mixtures but loses it in hydrogen-rich atmosphere.

In-pile tests at Oak Ridge National Laboratory of the palladium catalyst have disclosed no harmful effects due to irradiation or the presence of fission products.

Solvent Extraction Mechanics 

In solvent extraction of fission products from unburned uranium in spent reactor fuel, the process which determines the speed and efficiency of the operation-and consequently size and cost of equipment-is the transfer of the molecular species of interest from one liquid phase to the other-water to organic solvent. or vice versa. Since all transfer must take place across the interface, or boundary, between the two liquids, studies at Oak Ridge National Laboratory are directed toward understanding how transferring molecules reach and cross the interface, with particular emphasis on speed. 

A completed study showed that uranyl ions promptly cross the interface between water and tributyl phosphate solution, once the ions reach the interface. This was learned by establishing and maintaining a sharp difference in concentration across an interface-which caused a steady, known rate of mass transfer-measuring photometrically, the concentration profile on both sides of the interface and then extrapolating the measurements to the interface itself. Then the experiment was repeated with a commercial detergent to modify certain properties of the interface. The experiments showed that the limiting factor in mass transfer in uranium extraction is not resistance at the interface, but rather the transport of uranyl ions to and from the interface. 

Since acidic orthophosphates are widely used in separations in actinide and lanthanide chemistry, a study of the mechanisms involved in extraction of certain metallic ions by this chemical was initiated. The study has been supplemented at Argonne National Laboratory by a study of acidic phosphonates containing alkyl, aryl, or mixed alkyl-aryl groups or substituted variants. All the di-acidic esters studied have proved to be polymeric. 

These systems have been applied successfully to separation of californium and cerium, of

californium and yttrium, and of americium and promethium.

Large-scale fractionation of rare earths into products of moderate purity, using an acidic phosphate mineral acid system developed at

Argonne, has been inaugurated by at least two industrial producers.

Solvent Extraction Processes 

Metal salts. The extraction of metal salts from a fused salt solution by an immiscible organic solvent has been investigated at Argonne National Laboratory. The two phases studied were: a salt phase, consisting of an eutectic mixture of lithium and potassium nitrates with a melting point of 120° C; an organic phase, consisting of an active extractant, normal tributyl phosphate, dissolved in an inert solvent. 

The extraction behavior of the elements lithium, potassium, cobalt, europium, neodymium, americium, neptunium and uranium were investigated. As expected, the distribution coefficients were found to be much greater than those for aqueous nitrate systems. The solvation numbers (the number of solvent molecules associated with one molecule of solute), and the separation factors were found to be similar to those obtained in aqueous systems. By adding complexing ions such as the chloride ion, it was possible to increase the separation factors for such mixtures as europium and americium. Further investigation of the effect of complexing ions may lead to the development of more efficient separation processes. 

Fluoride extraction. Liquid-liquid extraction of zirconium by tributyl phosphate from mixed nitric-hydrofluoric acid solutions has been studied at the Idaho Chemical Processing 

Plant. Zirconium as well as fluoride are extracted from nitric acid.

Equipment for dissolution of uranium zirconium alloy fuels, used in submarine reactors, has been developed and demonstrated at Oak Ridge National Laboratory. This system will allow use of the fluoride volatility process for recovery of fuel as a uranium fluoride with two potential advantages that would decrease fuel cycle time and inventory: (a) fuel cooled only a short time can be processed without adverse process effects, and (b) the uranium hexafluoride product may be used directly in a gaseous diffusion plant for reenrichment. The fuel is dissolved in a mixture. of lithium, sodium and zirconium fluorides agitated with hydrogen fluoride. The equipment will be designed for scheduled replacement because corrosion rates are high.

New solvents for atomic energy processing have been investigated for several years at Oak Ridge National Laboratory. This program established uranium recovery processes now widely used in the uranium milling industry. 

Continued investigation of these and related solvents is confirming their wider utility in radiochemical processing, and has established basis for new or improved processes to (a) concentrate and purify plutonium from nitrate process solution with a secondary or tertiary amine; (b) scavenge plutonium from nitrate waste with a tertiary amine or from sulfate waste with a primary amine; (c) recover and separate neptunium and plutonium from nitrate process solution with a tertiary amine; (d) recover and separate fission product strontium and rare earths from nitrate waste with a dialkylphosphoric acid; (e) recover and separate technetium, neptunium, and uranium from fluorination residues with a tertiary amine; and (f) recover and separate uranium and thorium from nitrate process solution with a phenylphosphonate ester. Solvent stability. The mechanisms of solvent instability are being examined by Phillips Petroleum Co. at the Idaho Chemical Processing

Plant. Zirconium in the organic phase has been found to accelerate appreciably the decomposition of tributylphosphate, basically by a dealkylation reaction. The major volatile decomposition product is n-butyl nitrate. A zirconium dibutylphosphate reaction product has

been isolated. The effect of acid in the system also has been evaluated.

The zirconium-tributylphosphate reaction and its products can have significance in future processing of zirconium fuels or highly-burned fuels containing fission-product zirconium in relatively large concentration. 

Promethium. A process was developed at Oak 

Ridge National Laboratory for separating promethium, useful as a radioisotopic heat source, from mixed fission product rare earths by continuous multistage extraction between tri-n-butylphosphate and nitric acid. A twocycle system with 20 stages in the first cycle and 34 stages in the second can give a yield of 90 percent of the promethium in a typical mixture with a purity of greater than 80 percent. 

Technetium-neptunium. A process was developed at Oak Ridge to recover and separate technetium, neptunium, and uranium occurring in uranium fluorination plant residues, in good yield and with low cross-contamination. After being leached into acid aluminum nitrate solution, all three values are coextracted by a hydrocarbon solution of a tertiary amine. They are then stripped in succession. 

Strontium and rare earth. Large quantities of the beta-emitting fission products, strontium 90, cesium 144 and promethium 147, may be required for future industrial applications. Current small-scale production of these isotopes from reactor-fuel processing wastes utilizes ionexchange, precipitation, or crystallization. Recently a new process for potential large-scale production has been developed at Oak Ridge National Laboratory. In this process, strontium or rare earths, or both, are recovered from the wastes by a continuous solvent extraction system, using di (2-ethylhexyl) phosphoric acid (D2EHPA). In specific application of this process, the iron in the waste solutions is complexed with tartrate and the pH (acid-alkaline balance) is adjusted with caustic. Strontium and rare earths then are extracted with D2EHPA-TBP (tributylphosphate)-Amsco and stripped with nitric acid. Processing the strip product through additional solvent extraction cycles yields isolated, concentrated strontium and rare earth fractions. 

Rare Earths

The rare earths, or lanthanide elements, comprise a group of elements which have special characteristics common to them all. Among them are chemical elements which are difficult to separate by chemical means although they are distinct elements. The outermost electron shells of such elements-the shells which determine the chemical characteristics of elementsare identical, but they apparently differ in the arrangement of electrons in inner shells.

Closely related to the lanthanide group are the actinide group which include transuranic heavy elements. All are important to the atomic energy program. Many fission products from reactors fall into the rare earth group. 

Electronic structure. The motion of electrons in unexcited orbits is relatively well understood for most elements. However, the method of optional spectroscopy classically used to study orbital electrons is not adequate to define the orbit patterns of the rare earths. Until recently, little was known about electronic structure of these elements. 

Several years ago, the atomic beam resonance technique was found to be useful in this research, and during the past year Lawrence Radiation Laboratory, Berkeley, has been making electron structure measurements on the rare earth group. A theoretical study also has been underway to explain the results of this work in terms of fundamental atomic theory. This theory gives agreement with all measurements completed to date, on the basis of a classical model of the atom, modified by certain effects of the theory of relativity. Research is not yet complete, but it is hoped that it will soon provide a basis for understanding the electronic structure of the rare earths.

Rare Earth Separation. Ion-exchange columns (back ground) are used at the Ames Laboratory to separate mixtures of rare earths. The process was developed at the laboratory and has made compounds of 14 unfamiliar elements available to the scientist. In above photo an element in a suspended-solution form is being drawn from one of the ion-exchange columns. 

Electrochemical properties. Research at Los

Alamos Scientific Laboratory on electrochem ical properties of rare earths has led to a discovery that all rare earth elements behave differently when electrolyzed in the presence of each other at a lithium amalgam cathode. Some rare earths are very easy to electrolyze into the metallic cathode phase; others are very difficult and require exacting conditions. The ease of electrolysis is: europium, ytterbium, samarium, lanthanum, and the other rare earths in the order of their atomic numbers. Yttrium falls between erbium and thulium. Hundred gram and kilogram scale electrolytic fractionations were done to prove that this method of separation is specific and practical. 

Polarographic investigation at the Idaho 

Chemical Processing Plant of the behavior of major fission product ions in a sulfate solution in both the absence and in the presence of gross quantities of iron, chrome, and nickel being conducted as an aid in understanding processes taking place at a mercury cathode. Results have shown that strontium, cesium, cerium, yttrium, or niobium are not co-removed at potentials used in the electrolytic reduction of iron, chrome, or nickel, but tracer amounts of zirconium and perhaps ruthenium may be coremoved to a significant extent in an electrolytic separation procedure.

Separation. Basic research at Ames Labora tory on ion exchange resulted in further improvements in rare earth separations processes. 

The process has been made public and industrial companies are producing rare earths in quantity. In special cases where research samples of rare earths cannot be obtained commercially, Ames Laboratory has continued to produce them. 

Electrolytic Systems Studies 

In support of reprocessing technology for spent reactor fuels, electrochemical techniques are being used at the Chemical Processing Plant to study the mechanisms of metal dissolution and corrosion in various solutions. 

Study of the dissolution of zirconium in hydrofluoric acid has revealed that the reaction is controlled by the mass transport of hydrogen fluoride to the metal-solution interface.

Two different mechanisms are observed in the electrolytic dissolution of zirconium in hydrogen chloride-methanol medium. In one potential region ordinary dissolution takes place with electron transfer as the slow step, while in the electropolishing region chemical dissolution of a surface film determines the rate.

The electrolytic dissolution of a wide variety of stainless steels and aluminum, and to some extent zirconium, in aqueous nitric acid has indicated a dissolving approach for many metals. 

Precipitation From Molten Fluorides 

Selective precipitation of oxides from molten fluorides has been shown, in studies at Oak Ridge National Laboratory, to permit chemical separations which may be of value in nuclear technology. Addition of reactive oxides such as water or oxides of calcium or beryllium to

molten mixtures of lithium, beryllium and uranium fluorides has afforded quantitative precipitation of uranium oxide. Separation of uranium from alkaline earth and rare earth cations has been demonstrated, but no separation from zirconium was achieved. Quantitative precipitation of uranium and protactinium, with some coprecipitation of thorium oxide has been achieved with beryllium oxide as precipitant in molten mixtures lithium, beryllium and thorium fluorides, which are of probable value as breeder blankets for reactors. 

Protactinium has been quantitatively removed as oxide from such mixtures by use of thorium oxide. Precipitation of uranium with thorium oxide from similar very dilute solutions has not been shown to be complete. 

Metal-Salt Systems at High Temperature 

Research at Atomics International under a

Commission contract, as well as that conducted at Oak Ridge National Laboratory, has shown that in many cases, metals are quite soluble in salts of the same metal. This phenomenon is being investigated with metallic halides to learn more of the nature of high temperature interactions between metals and their salts. A main objective is to determine the various species in metal-salt solutions. Even the species existing in pure fused salts often are unknown. To date, there are two main theories about species in solutions of metals in salts: (a) solution by reaction of the metal with the salt to form another salt known as a subhalide; and, (b) solution of the metal as metal atoms. 

Emphasis in the project has been placed on studies of solutions of bismuth and its salts. 

The bismuth systems have the lowest temperatures for complete solutions, that of the bismuth-bismuth tri-iodide system (Bi-BiI,) being about 465° C. Recent experiments with electrical cells have indicated that these solutions are complex. Dissolved bismuth exists in more than one form, and the abundance of each form depends upon temperature and concentration. 

Since these solutions may possess properties as semiconductors and be used for direct conversion of heat to electricity, their other electrical properties are being studied. 

Another project at Atomics International on high temperature thorium chemistry seeks to obtain basic thermodynamic data on thorium and its compounds. One problem being investigated is the interaction at 2,000° C of thorium with oxygen and with ceramics such as thorium dioxide. In both reactions the relatively volatile oxide, thorium monoxide, is produced. 

Thus, thorium metal that contains oxygen or thorium dioxide as an impurity can be purified by a high-temperature vacuum vaporization. 

Fused Chloride Chemistry

Study of the properties and reactions of reactor fuel metals in fused chlorides by Phillips Petroleum Co. in Idaho has led to development of a head-end treatment of zirconium and aluminum alloys which provides an immediate separation of matrix material from uranium. 

This system may help simplify waste storage. 

Zirconium alloys are dissolved in lead chloride at a temperature just above its melting point. 

Zirconium tetrachloride readily volatilizes at this temperature leaving uranium chlorides dissolved in excess solvent.

Addition of chlorine to the melt dissolves stainless steel also, in addition to other reactor metals such as niobium, chromium, molybdenum, magnesium and nickel.

Association of Alkali Halide Vapors 

At Oak Ridge National Laboratory, the properties of molten salts are being studied at high temperatures because of their potential use as reactor coolants or reactor fuels. The alkali halides (fluorides, bromides, oxides, and chlorides in this case, chiefly fluorides-) have received much attention, since as simple salts they afford a convenient means for comparisons of theory with experiment. Complete systematization of their experimental behavior, undertaken by measuring their proper thermo-

dynamic quantities, has been plagued by an inconsistency. The vapors of these halides are known to form molecular complexes, but the degree of association of this as measured by different techniques have appeared to be different. 

Further, the derived heats and entropies of their associations have not been consistent with other quantities. 

The vapor phase association of seven alkali halides has been measured recently over a range of temperature by the most direct method, the determination of the density of the vapor. Although this method is easy to apply to substances volatile at moderate temperatures, it had not been used previously in ranges around 

1,000°C. The technique used depended upon the use of molten gold as a pressure measuring fluid to register the vapor pressure exerted by a known weight of salt confined in a fused silica vessel of known volume. This technique appears to provide dependable values for the association balance of these salts, and make possible the correlation of all their thermodynamic properties at high temperatures.

Vaporization Behavior of Oxides 

The vaporization or sublimation (direct gasification of solids) of condensed phases, and the thermodynamic properties, of molecular gases are determined principally by the energies of the chemical bonds involved. The systematization of the elements in the Periodic Table of the Elements reflects variations in these energies. 

Simple and quantitative molecules in the isolated state (i.e., the gas state) must be investigated before a complete explanation of the complex molecules and the role of environment, such as is found in solution, can be determined. 

Studies at Argonne National Laboratory have been concerned with the chemistry and thermodynamic properties of gaseous compounds of thorium, protactinium, uranium, and the transuranium elements in relation to the elements of groups IV, V, and VI A of the Periodic Table. 

In a continuation of transpiration and effusion studies the free energies of formation of various gaseous polymers of molybdenum trioxide, tungsten trioxide and uranium trioxide have been measured.

The vaporization properties of the oxides of tungsten have been determined more accurately and more reliably than previously. The measurements show that tungsten trioxide loses oxygen preferentially to the extent that a constantly subliming composition is formed at an oxygen-to-tungsten ratio of 2.96 to 1. In vaporization behavior, the oxides of uranium resemble those of tungsten more closely than they do those of the actinide series. The principal gaseous uranium oxides are those of valence two, four, and six.

The vaporization properties of uranium monosulfide have been studied, and molecular and atomic species effusing from a tungsten effusion cell containing the monosulfide have been identified.

Solubility of Strontium Sulfate 

Research at Princeton University has led to some interesting results on the effect of particle size on the solubility of strontium sulfate in water. The particle sizes were measured by electron microscopy, and the solubility of the compound's particles in the colloidal range was investigated by radioisotope tracer techniques. 

The results are in good agreement with theory, showing that small particles have a greater solubility than larger particles, and the smallest particles therefore appear to control the degree of solubility. 

Stable Mercurous Complexes

Stable complexes formed between mercurous ions and pyrophosphate anions have been discovered at California Institute of Technology.

These are the first known mercurous complexes that are stable against spontaneous transformation into the mercuric ion and metallic mercury (quick silver). This same research led to discovery of a whole class of stable mercurous

complexes formed with anions of dicarboxylic acids such as oxalic, malonic, and succinic acids. Further investigation of these complexes should result in contributions to the theory of complex ions.

Preparation of Fluorides 

Although, by analogy with other similar elements, polonium might be expected to form a volatile fluoride compound, previous attempts to use conventional methods to prepare a polonium fluoride that readily becomes a gas have been unsuccessful. Recently, Argonne National 

Laboratory applied the experience and techniques developed in work with highly reactive plutonium hexafluoride, and demonstrated the existence of a volatile polonium fluoride. 

Gaseous polonium fluoride tends to decompose even more rapidly than plutonium hexafluoride because of its thermodynamic instability and much greater radioactivity. 

One use for a volatile polonium fluoride would be in preparation of a gaseous source of neutrons, useful in many experiments and in industry, the neutrons being produced by the reaction of polonium alpha particles on fluorine atoms. The present work demonstrated that twice as many neutrons are produced when the polonium is in vapor phase as in a condensed phase. When the polonium is condensed, half the alpha particles it emits are ejected into the wall of the containing vessel. 

Argonne also prepared for the first time a heptavalent fluoride of rhenium. This compound, ReF7, is a pale yellow solid which melts at 43 degrees centigrade and exhibits a much lower vapor pressure than rhenium hexafluoride (ReF.). The compound is quite stable and can be kept unchanged for long periods of time in nickel or glass storage vessels.

Separation of Hydrogen Molecules 

A theory of separation by adsorption at low temperatures has been developed at Ohio State University by considering the energy levels of two molecular species of hydrogen, ortho- and para-hydrogen, which differ from each other only in the different relative spins of their two component atoms. The theory predicts that ortho-hydrogen in which atomic spins are parallel is more strongly adsorbed than parahydrogen, in which atomic spins are opposite, and that para-deuterium is more strongly adsorbed than ortho-deuterium. This is in agreement with experiments carried out earlier at Ohio State University where pure orthohydrogen and pure para-deuterium were prepared and studied for the first time. The separation of the hydrogen and deuterium isotopes by selective low temperature adsorption also was studied experimentally and theoretically.

A cooperative study is being carried out by Brookhaven National Laboratory and the Los Alamos Scientific Laboratory on vapor-liquid equilibria for tritium hydride in hydrogen, and for tritium deuteride in deuterium. The study showed that low temperature distillation may be an effective process for separation of tritium from hydrogen and deuterium. Because of quantum effects, the separation factors for tritium hydride in hydrogen are lower than those observed for deuterium in hydrogen. At the Oak Ridge National Laboratory tritium is removed from deuterium by successive electrolysis of heavy water; the resultant tritiumfree heavy water has been used effectively in bubble chambers. 

Intermolecular Forces at High Pressures

The use of high pressures to investigate intermolecular forces and the structure of matter is finding wide application in several Commission laboratories and Commission-supported university programs. At the University of 

Illinois, optical studies are being carried out on the structure of solids at pressures up to 3 million pounds per square inch and at temperatures up to 500° C. These studies include: measurements of the gap between the conduction band and valence band for zincblende

types of semiconductors; investigations of the energy levels of transition metal ions in various environments; and determinations of the structure of rare earth ions.

Reactor-Mixture Studies 

A series of critical measurements have been undertaken at Lawrence Radiation Laboratory, Livermore, on systems which are quasi-homogeneous mixtures of enriched uranium and two neutron moderators: graphite and beryllium oxide. To date, approximately 30 graphiteuranium and 70 beryllium oxide-uranium systems have been studied. The measurement program has engineering value in itself since it provides experience in fabrication of the materials (particularly beryllium oxide). Several power reactor designs involve such mixtures. The critical measurements provide data on geometrical effects, fuel density, flux distributions, and on the effect of resonance neutron absorbers. The latter was emphasized heavily. Materials used were primarily those alloys which appear most promising for use as structural and control elements in high-temperature reactors. Data from these measurements have been disseminated to reactor physicists throughout the country. 

This program also has served as a vehicle for the study of a new field of experimental reactor physics, the pulsed neutron source technique. This is essentially an application of high energy physics techniques to reactor physics; the quantities measured and the analyses applied to results are basically different from those hitherto used. The pulsed technique has proved to be a powerful tool for studying the neutronic properties of reactor cores. 

Basic study of the interactions of slow neutrons with many component reactor materials and in complex geometric arrangements is being made with a phased rotor velocity neutron selector at the Materials Testing Reactor, in

Idaho. At the present state of development the velocity selector consists of four rotors, or choppers, located over a distance of 3.5 meters along an intense neutron beam from the MTR, rotating at 5,000 rpm and with appropriate phase differences to allow the neutrons of a selected energy to get through all four rotors and pass on to a sample of a reactor material. The energy gains and losses and the angular distributions of the neutrons scattered from the reactor material are then measured. With this information Monte Carlo calculational techniques can be used to study neutron behavior and hence the reactor characteristics of a complex arrangement of fuel plates, channels with flowing liquid or gas coolants, moderator components, layers of reflectors, etc. 

This research can help provide basic information on the energy states of solids, liquids, and gases and spectra of vibrational frequencies present in crystallized material. 

Kinetics of Chemical Gas Reactions 

Studies of the kinetics of the hydrogen-iodine reaction at Los Alamos Scientific Laboratory have given the first indication that a bimolecular reaction can have a negative temperature coefficient. Experiments showed that atommolecule reactions of hydrogren and iodine, as well as the classical bimolecular reaction H₂+12=2HI, are necessary to explain the results obtained. Rate constants were obtained for the six reactions involved in the mechanism of iodine hydrogen reactions. The rate constant for the very rapid reaction H+I₂=HI+I was shown to have a negative temperature coefficient, that is, to decrease with increasing temperature. 

Paramagnetic Resonance Absorption In recent years, a technique known as paramagnetic resonance absorption has begun to give evidence for the existence of free radicals long postulated as intermediates in radiation induced chemical reactions. A study completed at Oak Ridge National Laboratory which shows in detail the connection between hydrogen

atoms stably trapped in dilute sulfuric and perchloric acids after irradiation at low temperatures, and the hydrogen gas formed on melting the irradiated solids. Within the precision of the measurement, all the hydrogen atoms measured in the solid by paramagnetic resonance appear as hydrogen gas upon melting. 

This result is surprising, because the solid contains oxidizing radicals in equal concentration, and they might be expected to combine with the hydrogen atoms as the latter begin to diffuse on warming. 

These experiments demonstrate that paramagnetic resonance can be a useful tool for unravelling the details of radiation chemical reactions. Previously, it has been used only for identification of free radicals.

Intermediate Chemical Species 

Direct identification and study of transient intermediate chemical species (such as free radicals, excited molecules, ions, and electrons) produced by the interaction of ionizing radiation with chemical systems is necessary for a complete understanding of these reactions.

Knowledge of these intermediates has been derived indirectly from the kinetic behavior of the initial reactants and the final products in the system. Recently, utilizing a pulsed linear electron accelerator in conjunction with associated electronic and optical equipment,

Argonne National Laboratory has made direct observations of short-lived intermediates in several chemical systems. The accelerator furnished ionization radiations in high intensity pulses of very short duration. The resulting transient species produced were directly observed by photographing their ultra-violet absorption spectra. The radicals detected include the benzyl radical, the di-iodide ion-radical, and the monovalent bromate radical, with half-lives ranging from about 10 to 50 millionths of a second. 

This development opens a new field of radiation chemistry. Indirectly deduced reactive intermediates, as well as some unsuspected reactive intermediates, now can be directly observed and identified, and their rate constants for reaction can be measured. This permits measurement of the fundamental chemical reactivity of one of these transients in a reaction, and the determination of the proportions by which activation energy and entropy contribute to this reactivity. Such knowledge will greatly increase our understanding not only of radiation chemistry, but also of other reactions initiated by heat or light rather than by ionizing radiation. 

A knowledge of the lifetime of the various intermediate processes and species involved also is important to a basic understanding of these changes. At Notre Dame University new techniques have been developed to measure radiation-induced luminescence involving lifetimes of less than one-billionth of a second.

Luminescence in mixed cyclohexane-benzene scintillator systems was found to have lifetimes of about 2 billionths of a second. The addition of benzene at low concentrations to cyclohexane solutions of a scintillator caused a sharp lengthening of decay times. This information assists in understanding of the mechanisms of radiation protection of liquid organic systems by aromatic compounds (e.g. benzene).

New 100-inch Mass Spectrometer 

Argonne National Laboratory has just completed construction of a new large mass spectrometer expected to assist many research projects. 

Fundamentally a device which sorts atoms according to their mass, the mass spectrometer is used both to measure the mass of atomic nuclei accurately and to determine the relative quantities of atoms of isotopes of different mass in mixtures. Possible applications of the instrument range from determination of specific properties of atoms and molecules, such as half-lives, ionization potentials, molecular bond strengths, etc., to measurement of geological and meteorite ages.

The spectrometer is double-focusing, both by direction and by velocity, and uses a spherical electrostatic radial field and magnet in a

Ancient Log. Studies on the origin of uranium ore deposits may also lead to new knowledge concerning the formation periods of this earth. In above photo, a miner inspects an ancient log which has been completely mineralized by radioactive asphaltite at a mine near Temple Mountain in Utah. (Engineering and Mining Journal photo.) special arrangement. The radius of the curvature is 100 inches. The instrument is approximately 10 times the size of a conventional machine. Smaller spectrometers, such as the 

12-inch radius, 60 degree sector mass spectrometers first introduced at Argonne in 1949, played an important role in high-sensitivity isotopic analysis of extremely small samples of transuranic elements and other nuclear chemistry applications. This work has resulted in the first identification of numerous isotopes, such as plutonium 244, curium 247, curium 248, and curium 251, and measurements of cross sections and half-lives of various nuclei. The sensitivity of conventional analytical mass spectrometers is sufficiently sensitive to detect a sample of a transuranic element that contains some 20 to 200 million atoms. new large mass spectrometer is expected to increase this sensitivity by several orders of

The magnitude and, in combination with improved ion sources, to be able to detect a sample of a transuranic element containing as few as 1,000 to 10,000 atoms. 

The new instrument achieves a transmission of 95 percent of the ions produced by conventional ionization sources with a resolution of about 10,000. Conventional large analytical mass spectrometers transmit only 1 to 10 percent with a resolution of about 1,000. High transmission means greater sensitivity and greater absolute accuracy in measuring isotopic radiations. The much greater resolution will eliminate most interference at any specific mass by impurity ions of the same mass number but of a different molecular composition which results in slightly different mass due to different molecular building energies. This will greatly increase the sensitivity in many analytical problems. 

High Temperature Viscosity of Gases 

A circular channel viscometer has been de veloped by Los Alamos Scientific Laboratory for measuring viscosities of noncorrosive gases from room temperature up to 3,000° C, thus extending previously possible measurements by about 2,000 degrees. Preliminary measurements on helium and hydrogen in the viscometer at 1,000 degrees centigrade and atmospheric pressure agree with expected values. 

Extension of measurements to higher temperatures and pressures, and to other gases, is limited only by the structural material of the viscometer. Structures made of tungsten or molybdenum are used for hydrogen, helium, nitrogen, and argon. Platinum or rhodium could be used for oxygen and other normally corrosive gases but would be limited to 

1,000° C. 

The Origin of Uranium Deposits 

A study on the origin of uranium deposits is in progress at Yale University. The project involves a study of sulfur isotope abundances

in sulfide minerals almost always associated with uranium deposits. Although begun as basic research, the result obtained is of economic interest in that it identifies important information regarding the formation of sandstone uranium ores.

The sulfur 32-sulfur 34 ratios of sulfides associated with sandstone ores characteristically exhibit broad variations and are commonly enriched in sulfur 32 relative to meteoritic sulfur. Inasmuch as the isotopic, geologic, mineralogical, and chemical evidence suggest a low temperature of formation, the indication is that the sandstone ores and contemporaneous sulfides were deposited from ground water solutions. The precipitating agent was hydrogen sulfide, variably enriched in sulfur 32, generated through bacterial reduction of sulfate. The evidence appears to indicate that bacterial evolution of hydrogen sulfide accompanied primary mineralization, although it may have preceded this reaction. 

The details of the mineralization process cannot be established conclusively until the ages of the deposits are known with greater certainty. Cosmochemistry

The use of radioactivation analysis to determine concentrations and isotopic compositions of trace elements in meteorites has resulted in the discovery of two classes of chondritic meteorites. One group, which included most ordinary chondrites such as Forest City, Modoc, Holbrook, etc., contains thallium, lead, and bismuth concentrations which are orders of magnitude lower than are observed in the second group, which includes enstatite and carbonaceous chondrites. The content of uranium and barium varies little in the two classes. 

The concentration of mercury in different kinds of meteoritic matter has been measured at Argonne National Laboratory, and an effort was made to determine how tightly the mercury was held in the samples. The concentrations. and the results of relative volatilization experiments seem to be correlated with the apparent 573517 0-61-9 thermal history of the meteorite. High concentrations of mercury were found in the meteorites which evidenced the least heating. The mercury 196-mercury 202 isotopic ratio was determined to be about the same as terrestrial mercury.

Geochronology based on isotope ratios of lead, has always had to assume that all primordial lead in solar system matter has the same isotopic composition. This view has gained confirmation from measurements of the lead 208-lead 204 isotopic ratio in the enstatite and carbonaceous chondrites.

The concentration of lead in the ultrabasic rocks, measured at Argonne and Carnegie Institute of Washington, was 0.1 parts per million range and lower by a factor of 10 than values from previous mass spectrometric analyses. 

Meteorites as Space Probes for Cosmic Rays An experiment has been conducted at Brookhaven National Laboratory to test the spatial constancy of high energy cosmic radiation within the solar system by measuring shortlived and long-lived radioactivity induced by cosmic radiation in a recently fallen meteorite. 

This test depends upon the fact that meteorites have highly eccentric orbits, and therefore serve as space probes over the region between the earth's orbit and the asteroidal belt. The measured short-lived activity is produced while the meteorite is in the vicinity of the earth's orbit; the long-lived activity is produced over the entire orbit of the meteorite. Thus, a comparison of the spatial constancy of cosmic radiation over some 300,000,000 miles can be obtained.

The measurements were performed on the 

Hamlet chondrite that fell in Indiana on October 13, 1959. The 35-day argon 37 and the 

260-year argon 39 activities were observed. The relative amounts of these activities were compared to the production rates of these isotopes derived from bombarding a sample of the meteorite with three Bev protons from the Cosmotron. The ratio of argon 37 to argon 39 in

the sample bombarded at the Cosmotron was found to be consistent with the ratio determined in the meteorite. This result indicates that cosmic rays are constant throughout the space probed by the meteorite.

A study of the natural abundances of the elements and isotopes and the properties of meteorites and other astronomical bodies is being carried out at University of California, La Jolla. Measurements on the cosmic ray ages of stone meteorites indicate that they have been exposed to cosmic rays for from 3 to 50 million years; one was found to have been exposed for 

240 million years. Measurements at other laboratories on metal meteorites indicate exposures from tens of millions to one or two billion years. 

The conclusion of the La Jolla scientists is that the irons and stones came from different places; the stones may have come from the moon and the iron meteorites from the asteroidal belt.

CONTROLLED FUSION RESEARCH 

The successive goals in controlled thermonuclear research are: a) To heat and confine a plasma at a particleenergy sufficient to yield significant amounts of thermonuclear energy during the time of confinement. One measure of the particleenergies required is the so-called ideal ignition energy. By definition, at this level of energy, the kinetic energy added to the plasma by the fast-moving charged-particle products of thermonuclear reactions would be just sufficient to overcome the loss of X-ray energy by the hot plasma. For the reaction of a deuteron (H.) with a triton (H), the ideal ignition energy is about 

5,000 ev, while for the deuteron-deuteron reaction the value is about 40,000 ev. b) To study such a thermonuclear plasma at ignition energies, and, if it is found possible to design and build a pilot plant yielding net power. c) To assess, by experience, the possibility of economic generation of fusion power. 

Present research is concentrated on the first goal, to heat plasma to the so-called ignition energy range. Good progress is being made and there is no doubt that further substantial advances can be made. Workers in this country are confident that the ignition energy can be reached. However, it is not likely that there will be any sudden breakthrough.

The dominant feature of current fusion power research is the struggle against instabilities in hot plasmas, made up essentially of positively charged hydrogen nuclei (ions) and free electrons. The ions must achieve sufficient kinetic energy in random motion to be able to accomplish a fusion reaction upon collision, forming a heavier nucleus with the release of energy. In all experimental devices, magnetic fields are used to confine the plasma within a region of the vacuum chamber in which the reaction takes place. The objective is to confine plasmas of sufficient density and energy for a time long enough to permit an appreciable fraction of the fuel nuclei to react. However, plasma instabilities, occurring especially during the heating (energizing) process, tend to carry ions and electrons to the walls of the vacuum chamber with great loss of energy, in spite of the powerful magnetic fields employed to limit the movements of the gas. Large energy losses also occur as a result of radiation arising from the presence of impurities in the plasma. The margin between ultimate success and failure in confining the plasma is very small, and is basically dependent on the length of time that the gas can be stably confined and the maintenance of a satisfactorily low impurity level. 

If and when the ignition energy of plasma is attained, the emphasis will quickly shift to finding means to increase the time of confinement of the gas, and to engineering and chemical studies, such as intensive work on wall materials, shielding, and magnetic coil design.

Until the early months of 1959, it was thought that potential thermonuclear reactors would necessarily be of very great size, a single plant being suitable only for a large power-consuming area. It was known that some reduc-

tion of size might be attained by economies in magnet power to be achieved by cooling to temperatures in the liquid hydrogen range the coils that create the magnetic field. Renewed studies on low-temperature coils, cryogenically cooled, at the Lawrence Radiation Laboratory, Livermore, indicate that, through the use of such coils to provide a steady-state "magnetic mirror," to confine the plasma, the power consumption of a working power plant could possibly be brought down to about 10,000 kilowatts in a machine designed to produce a net useful power output of the same order of magnitude. 

As a result, if stable confinement of a hot plasma were once achieved, the task of working toward a power-producing pilot plant might prove simpler than was thought possible prior to 1959.

The Various Approaches 

Research on controlled thermonuclear reactors is being carried out along different lines at four major Commission research centers: 

Princeton University; E. O. Lawrence Radiation Laboratory (LRL); Los Alamos Scientific 

Laboratory (LASL); and Oak Ridge National Laboratory (ORNL). Supporting work on specialized aspects is carried on at the Naval 

Research Laboratory (NRL), Bureau of 

Standards, New York University, and at a number of other universities and industrial organizations. (See list of contracts, Appendix 3.) 

The main lines of attack are as follows:

Name and Location Characterization 

1. Stellarators; Princeton... Gas is heated in endless tubes provided with very strong, externally applied, spiral magnetic fields. 

2. Injection into magnetic mirrors; LRL, LASL,

ORNL. 

3. Self-confinement methods;

LASL, LRL, NRL. 

Ions or neutral atoms are supplied from external sources, and must be trapped between strong magnetic field regions called "mirrors." Injected material is provided with energy sufficient for thermonuclear reaction before it enters.

Strong currents are induced in the plasma, generating magnetic fields that are the main agents for compressing or "pinching" and therefore heating, the plasma. 

Name and Location 

4. Astron; LRL. 

5. Rotating plasmas; LASL,

LRL. 

Characterization 

Electrons of relativistic energy (velocity approaching that of light) are injected to form a rotating cylindrical layer. 

The magnetic field of this layer, together with external fields, is able to confine ions, and the fast electrons impart kinetic energy to the ions. 

A smaller effort. Ions and electrons are accelerated by electric fields applied to the plasma in such a way as to impar rotational motion.

The multiple-project approach in controlled thermonuclear research is used for two main reasons: a) As higher temperatures, higher densities and longer confinement times are achieved, various plasma instabilities occur. Scientists cannot yet predict even theoretically the confinement geometry and methods of heating that will yield the best results. b) Different heating methods are adapted to widely different plasma densities, varying from 10 billion to more than 10 million billion particles per cubic centimeter. Within limits, there is a choice of studying either high density plasmas confined for several microseconds, or lower density plasmas with a much greater confinement time at present, usually several milliseconds.

Four of the approaches are directed toward an ultimate goal of a steadily operating device. 

These include the Stellarator, the high-energy injection device at Oak Ridge, a neutral-atom injection device at Lawrence Radiation Laboratory, and the Astron device.

Even in a steady-state device, the confinement time of an ion is a basic consideration. What matters in this type of machine is the time for escape of an individual fast ion, even though it may be replaced by feeding in another. In present work, nearly all the plasmas are produced in a transient manner, since this type of experiment economizes on the electric capacitors and generators required to supply magnetic and heating power. In the Oak Ridge

1497 Power Bank. Thermonuclear research requires great amounts of electricity in short pulses. Photo shows workmen completing the 4,032-capacitator bank which will provide electricity equal to the entire power output in the United States in a pulse of ten millionths of a second for the thermonuclear experiments at the Los Alamos Scientific Laboratory. Probably the biggest energy storage capacitator bank in the world, the unit is called ZEUS after the ancient Greek god of the elements. injection machine, however, steady-state operation is feasible and useful, and therefore is attempted.

The Various Devices 

Controlled thermonuclear research is carried out with relatively small devices which, however, require large power sources with abundant capacity for relatively short times. For example, a doughnut pinch device with a major diameter of about 12 inches required more power for microseconds than the entire output of Hoover dam could supply. Power for the machines is provided by building it up in capacitors which can release energy in a short pulse.

In some cases, three or four devices are used according to their respective capabilities to explore different aspects of a single approach.

The useful life of such a device may range from one to three or more years. A single source of power could run a series of machines.

The experimental machines being used as of mid-1960 are as follows:
